\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/02_activations-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-activation-comparison}Activation Function Profiles.
Comparison of ReLU, Sigmoid, Tanh, and GELU. Notice that GELU is smooth
and non-monotonic for small negative values (\(x \approx -0.17\)),
allowing small negative activations that preserve gradient flow through
deep stacks.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Softmax and the Shift-Invariance
Invariant}\label{softmax-and-the-shift-invariance-invariant}

While element-wise activations transform each scalar independently,
multi-class classification and attention mechanisms require normalizing
an entire vector of unconstrained logits \(\mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{R}^K\)
into a valid discrete probability distribution
\(\mathbf{p} \in \Delta^{K-1}\):

\[\text{Softmax}(\mathbf{z})_i = \frac{e^{z_i}}{\sum_{j=1}^K e^{z_j}}\]

\subsection{The Overflow Hazard in Naive
Softmax}\label{the-overflow-hazard-in-naive-softmax}

Consider a model generating logit predictions for vocabulary tokens:
\(\mathbf{z} = [800.0, 850.0, 900.0]\).

When an implementation computes \(e^{900.0}\), 32-bit floating-point
registers overflow to \texttt{+inf}. Dividing \texttt{inf\ /\ inf}
yields \texttt{NaN} (Not a Number), instantly corrupting model
parameters and terminating execution.

\section{The Shift-Invariance Principle of
Softmax}\label{the-shift-invariance-principle-of-softmax}

For any arbitrary constant scalar \(C \in \mathbb{R}\):

\[\text{Softmax}(\mathbf{z} - C)_i = \frac{e^{z_i - C}}{\sum_{j=1}^K e^{z_j - C}} = \frac{e^{z_i} \cdot e^{-C}}{\sum_{j=1}^K (e^{z_j} \cdot e^{-C})} = \frac{e^{z_i} \cdot e^{-C}}{e^{-C} \sum_{j=1}^K e^{z_j}} = \frac{e^{z_i}}{\sum_{j=1}^K e^{z_j}} = \text{Softmax}(\mathbf{z})_i\]

Subtracting a constant from all logits leaves the mathematical
probability distribution completely unchanged.

By choosing \(C = \max_j(z_j)\), the exponent argument is guaranteed to
satisfy:

\[z_i - \max_j(z_j) \le 0 \quad \forall i\]

Because \(e^u \in (0, 1]\) for all \(u \le 0\), the exponential term
\textbf{can never exceed \(1.0\)}, completely eliminating the
possibility of floating-point overflow.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Softmax:}
    \CommentTok{"""Numerically stable Softmax activation along specified axis."""}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, axis: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{):}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.axis }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ axis}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{):}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ []}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
\NormalTok{        x\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.data}
        \CommentTok{\# Shift invariance: subtract max along axis with keepdims=True}
\NormalTok{        max\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(x\_data, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.axis, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        exp\_shifted }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.exp(x\_data }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ max\_val)}
\NormalTok{        sum\_exp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(exp\_shifted, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.axis, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(exp\_shifted }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ sum\_exp)}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_call\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.forward(x)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Pitfall: Forgetting \texttt{keepdims=True} in
Multi-Axis
Reductions}{Pitfall: Forgetting keepdims=True in Multi-Axis Reductions}}\label{pitfall-forgetting-keepdimstrue-in-multi-axis-reductions}

When computing \texttt{np.max(x\_data,\ axis=-1)} on a 2D batch tensor
of shape \((B, D)\), omitting \texttt{keepdims=True} collapses the array
to 1D shape \((B,)\). Subsequent broadcasting subtraction
\texttt{x\_data\ -\ max\_val} will fail or incorrectly broadcast across
the batch dimension instead of feature columns. Always retain
dimensional rank during reduction steps.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Framework Mechanics: Stateless Activations vs.~Stateful
Layers}\label{framework-mechanics-stateless-activations-vs.-stateful-layers}

In TinyTorch, deep learning components adhere to a unified structural
protocol:

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Component Category   Trainable Parameters?  Example        │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Stateless Layer      No (parameters() -> []) ReLU, GELU    │
│  Stateful Layer       Yes (weights, biases)  Linear, Conv2d │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

Because activation layers contain no internal state or learnable
weights, they can be shared or invoked multiple times across a forward
pass without side effects. In contrast, parameterized layers (such as
\texttt{Linear}, which we build in the next chapter) encapsulate weight
matrices and bias vectors that must be registered and tracked by the
framework.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Systems Reality: Memory Bandwidth and Forward
Footprint}\label{systems-reality-memory-bandwidth-and-forward-footprint}

\subsection{The Memory-Bound Nature of Element-Wise
Kernels}\label{the-memory-bound-nature-of-element-wise-kernels}

While matrix multiplications (\(\mathbf{x} \mathbf{W}\)) have high
arithmetic intensity (\(O(N^3)\) operations on \(O(N^2)\) data),
activation functions are \textbf{strictly memory-bandwidth bound}.

For a tensor of \(N\) elements executing ReLU in 32-bit float:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Read 4 bytes per element from DRAM into CPU/GPU registers.
\item
  Perform 1 comparison operation in the ALU.
\item
  Write 4 bytes per element back to the DRAM memory buffer.
\end{itemize}

\[\text{Arithmetic Intensity} = \frac{1\text{ FLOP}}{8\text{ Bytes}} = 0.125\text{ FLOPs/Byte}\]

Consider a modern accelerator with \(312\text{ TFLOPs}\) of peak FP32
compute and \(2.0\text{ TB/s}\) of High Bandwidth Memory (HBM)
throughput. When executing a standalone element-wise activation kernel:

\[\text{Achievable Compute} = 2.0\text{ TB/s} \times 0.125\text{ FLOPs/Byte} = 250\text{ GFLOPs}\]

The hardware achieves \textbf{less than 0.1\% of its peak ALU
throughput}, spending over 99.9\% of its execution time stalled waiting
on memory bus transfers. This bottleneck provides the core motivation
for \textbf{kernel fusion} (combining \texttt{Linear\ +\ Activation}
into a single fused GPU kernel).

\subsection{Intermediate Forward Activation
Allocations}\label{intermediate-forward-activation-allocations}

When a batch of inputs flows through a deep network of \(L\) layers,
each activation function allocates a fresh tensor buffer to store the
transformed output:

\[\mathbf{x}_0 \xrightarrow{\text{Linear}_1} \mathbf{z}_1 \xrightarrow{\text{ReLU}} \mathbf{a}_1 \xrightarrow{\text{Linear}_2} \mathbf{z}_2 \xrightarrow{\text{ReLU}} \mathbf{a}_2 \dots \xrightarrow{\text{Linear}_L} \mathbf{y}\]

For a network processing batch size \(B\) with hidden dimension \(D\),
each intermediate activation tensor consumes
\(B \times D \times 4\text{ bytes}\) of memory.

\section{Check Your Understanding: Forward Activation Buffer
Memory}\label{check-your-understanding-forward-activation-buffer-memory}

Suppose we evaluate a 12-layer multi-layer forward network processing a
batch of \(B = 64\) samples with hidden dimension \(D = 1024\) in
standard \texttt{float32} (4 bytes per element).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  What is the memory allocated by a single intermediate activation
  tensor?
\item
  What is the total memory allocated across all 12 intermediate layer
  activations during the forward pass?
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Solution:} - Single activation tensor:
\(64 \times 1024 \times 4\text{ bytes} = 262,144\text{ bytes} = 256\text{ KB}\).
- Total across 12 layers: \(12 \times 256\text{ KB} = 3.072\text{ MB}\).

While modest for single forward passes on small MLPs, this allocation
pattern scales directly with batch size and sequence length in
large-scale architectures.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Production Reality: How PyTorch Optimizes Activations in
C++}\label{production-reality-how-pytorch-optimizes-activations-in-c}

In production frameworks like PyTorch, element-wise activation functions
are optimized to avoid the memory-bandwidth bottlenecks of standalone
kernels.\footnote{In PyTorch ATen
  (\texttt{aten/src/ATen/native/cpu/ActivationKernel.cpp}),
  \texttt{relu\_kernel} and \texttt{gelu\_kernel} dispatch to vectorized
  CPU templates that unroll loops across 512-bit registers. On GPU
  devices, compiler backends like Triton and PyTorch Inductor
  automatically fuse element-wise activations directly into epilogues of
  preceding GEMM matrix multiplications.}

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PyTorch ATen Activation Dispatch Architecture              │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. Vectorized CPU Backend: at::vec::Vectorized<float>      │
│     • Loads 8 floats (AVX2, 256-bit) or 16 floats (AVX-512) │
│     • Vectorized polynomial math in registers               │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  2. CUDA Kernel Backend: at::native::gpu_kernel             │
│     • 128-bit vector memory reads (float4 / 4 floats/thread)│
│     • Fast math hardware intrinsics (__expf, __fdividef)    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  3. Compiler Kernel Fusion: PyTorch Inductor & Triton        │
│     • Fuses Linear GEMM + Activation into a single kernel   │
│     • Eliminates intermediate HBM roundtrip allocations     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{CPU Vectorization
  (\texttt{at::vec::Vectorized\textless{}float\textgreater{}})}: On x86
  and ARM CPUs, PyTorch ATen evaluates activations using wide SIMD
  intrinsics (\texttt{\_mm256\_max\_ps}, \texttt{\_mm512\_max\_ps}),
  processing 8 or 16 floating-point numbers per instruction cycle.
\item
  \textbf{GPU Coalesced Vector Loads}: In CUDA, activation kernels pack
  4 consecutive \texttt{float32} elements into a 128-bit \texttt{float4}
  vector load per thread, maximizing GPU memory bus utilization.
\item
  \textbf{Kernel Fusion in TorchDynamo \& Inductor}: Instead of
  launching a separate GPU kernel for matrix multiplication followed by
  another kernel for activation, deep learning compilers generate a
  single fused kernel (\texttt{addmm\_relu} or \texttt{addmm\_gelu}).
  Intermediate pre-activation values remain in high-speed on-chip SRAM
  registers and are activated in-place before writing the final result
  back to DRAM.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-2}

In this chapter, we laid the second architectural tier of TinyTorch by
introducing non-linear activation functions. We mathematically proved
why stacking linear transformations without activations collapses into a
single layer, establishing the absolute necessity of non-linear mappings
for deep neural network representation.

We built five essential activation classes---\texttt{ReLU},
\texttt{Sigmoid}, \texttt{Tanh}, \texttt{GELU}, and
\texttt{Softmax}---and implemented the shift-invariance numerical
stabilization property to protect exponential operations against
floating-point register overflow. Finally, we analyzed the
memory-bandwidth characteristics of element-wise operators
(\(0.125\text{ FLOPs/byte}\)), introduced the systems rationale for
kernel fusion, and established the framework contract distinguishing
stateless activation transformations from stateful parameterized layers.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Breaking Linearity}: Without non-linear activation functions,
  multi-layer networks collapse into a single composite linear
  transformation with zero effective depth.
\item
  \textbf{Activation Profiles}: ReLU provides fast ALU computation;
  Sigmoid and Tanh offer bounded ranges; GELU provides smooth
  probabilistic gating standard in modern LLMs.
\item
  \textbf{IEEE 754 Floating-Point Safety}: Unconstrained exponentials
  overflow \texttt{float32} registers at \(x > 88.72\); branch-stable
  Sigmoid and shift-invariant Softmax (\(z_i - \max(\mathbf{z})\))
  eliminate overflow and NaN corruption.
\item
  \textbf{Memory-Bandwidth Bound}: Element-wise activations exhibit low
  arithmetic intensity (\(0.125\text{ FLOPs/byte}\)), making standalone
  execution memory-bound and motivating compiler kernel fusion.
\item
  \textbf{Stateless Framework Contract}: Activation functions contain no
  internal parameters (\texttt{parameters()\ -\textgreater{}\ {[}{]}}),
  allowing them to be shared and reused across network layers.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Encapsulating Weights and
Parameters}\label{chapter-connection-encapsulating-weights-and-parameters}

Now that we have non-linear activation functions, how do we encapsulate
learnable weights, biases, and regularization? In \textbf{Layers,
Regularization, and Parameter Management}, we build the stateful
\texttt{Linear} layer, implement \texttt{Dropout} for regularization,
and create the container abstractions that allow complex multi-layer
networks to be composed and executed.

\chapter{Layers, Regularization, and Parameter
Management}\label{layers-regularization-and-parameter-management}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does an uncalibrated weight matrix destroy signal propagation
before training even begins?}

When an input vector passes through a deep sequence of linear
transformations, the variance of the intermediate activations is
multiplied by the layer width at every step. If weight matrices are
initialized with uncalibrated random values, the magnitude of activation
values will either explode exponentially towards infinity or collapse
exponentially towards zero within a few layers, saturating numerical
registers and destroying the signal before any learning can occur.
Beyond the mathematics of linear projections, real deep learning
frameworks cannot treat layers as simple mathematical functions; layers
are stateful software containers that encapsulate trainable weight and
bias buffers, manage random number generator states, and dynamically
alter behavior between stochastic training and deterministic evaluation.
In a framework runtime, layer abstractions are the physical bridge that
binds raw tensor memory buffers into structured, executable neural
network architectures.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Construct} the foundational \texttt{Layer} base class and
  stateful \texttt{Linear} layer container encapsulating trainable
  \texttt{Tensor} parameter buffers.
\item
  \textbf{Derive} the variance conservation equations governing LeCun
  and He weight initialization (\(1/\sqrt{\text{fan\_in}}\) vs
  \(\sqrt{2/\text{fan\_in}}\)) to stabilize forward signal propagation
  across deep stacks.
\item
  \textbf{Implement} stochastic \texttt{Dropout} regularization using
  the inverted scaling invariant (\(\frac{1}{1-p}\)) to guarantee zero
  computational overhead during evaluation.
\item
  \textbf{Build} state-aware execution pipelines with \texttt{.train()}
  and \texttt{.eval()} mode switching.
\item
  \textbf{Compose} multi-layer neural networks using the
  \texttt{Sequential} container and calculate the exact physical memory
  footprint of model parameters.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Physics of Weight
Initialization}\label{the-physics-of-weight-initialization}

Consider a single linear transformation without bias computing the
output vector \(\mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_{\text{out}}}\) from an
input vector \(\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_{\text{in}}}\):

\[y_j = \sum_{i=1}^{d_{\text{in}}} x_i W_{i, j}\]

Assume the input features \(x_i\) are independent and identically
distributed with zero mean (\(\mathbb{E}[x_i] = 0\)) and unit variance
(\(\text{Var}(x_i) = \sigma_x^2 = 1\)). If we initialize the weight
matrix elements \(W_{i, j}\) from a standard normal distribution
\(\mathcal{N}(0, \sigma_W^2)\) with \(\mathbb{E}[W_{i, j}] = 0\), the
variance of each output element \(y_j\) is given by:

\[\text{Var}(y_j) = \text{Var}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d_{\text{in}}} x_i W_{i, j}\right) = \sum_{i=1}^{d_{\text{in}}} \text{Var}(x_i W_{i, j})\]

Since \(x_i\) and \(W_{i, j}\) are independent zero-mean random
variables:

\[\text{Var}(x_i W_{i, j}) = \mathbb{E}[(x_i W_{i, j})^2] - (\mathbb{E}[x_i W_{i, j}])^2 = \mathbb{E}[x_i^2]\mathbb{E}[W_{i, j}^2] - 0 = \text{Var}(x_i) \cdot \text{Var}(W_{i, j}) = \sigma_x^2 \sigma_W^2\]

Summing across all \(d_{\text{in}}\) incoming connections yields:

\[\text{Var}(y_j) = d_{\text{in}} \cdot \sigma_x^2 \cdot \sigma_W^2\]

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  The Variance Explosion Trap (Unscaled Normal Noise)        │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Layer 0: Input Variance = 1.0                              │
│  Layer 1 (d_in = 512): Output Variance = 512                │
│  Layer 2 (d_in = 512): Output Variance = 512 * 512 = 262,144│
│  Layer 3 (d_in = 512): Output Variance = 1.34 * 10^8        │
│  Layer 5 (d_in = 512): Output Variance = 3.52 * 10^13       │
│  => Activations overflow IEEE 754 float32 in ~6 layers!     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

If an architecture has hidden dimension \(d_{\text{in}} = 512\) and uses
unscaled \(\mathcal{N}(0, 1)\) weights, passing an input through just
three layers causes activation variance to explode by a factor of
\(512^3 \approx 1.34 \times 10^8\). In six layers, values exceed the
dynamic range of IEEE 754 floating-point numbers
(\(> 3.4 \times 10^{38}\)), crashing execution with overflow.\footnote{When
  activation magnitudes explode, hardware registers saturate to
  \(\pm \infty\). When they vanish (\(< 1.17 \times 10^{-38}\)),
  floating-point hardware switches to subnormal representation, forcing
  CPU arithmetic pipelines into multi-cycle microcode traps that degrade
  compute throughput by up to 100× before collapsing to \texttt{0.0}.}

\section{The Law of Variance
Conservation}\label{the-law-of-variance-conservation}

To preserve signal magnitude across consecutive linear layers
(\(\text{Var}(y) = \text{Var}(x) = 1\)), the variance of the weight
initialization must be inversely proportional to the number of input
connections (\(\text{fan\_in}\)):

\[\text{Var}(W) = \frac{1}{\text{fan\_in}} \implies \sigma_W = \sqrt{\frac{1}{\text{fan\_in}}}\]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{LeCun Initialization}:
  \(\sigma = \sqrt{\frac{1}{d_{\text{in}}}}\) (optimal for linear
  projections and zero-centered activations like Tanh).
\item
  \textbf{He (Kaiming) Initialization}:
  \(\sigma = \sqrt{\frac{2}{d_{\text{in}}}}\). Because ReLU zeroes out
  the negative half-plane (\(P(x \le 0) = 0.5\)), it halves the
  activation variance:
  \(\mathbb{E}[\text{ReLU}(y)^2] = \frac{1}{2}\text{Var}(y)\). To
  preserve unit variance across ReLU layers, the weight variance must be
  doubled to \(\text{Var}(W) = \frac{2}{d_{\text{in}}}\).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Stateful Linear Layer}\label{the-stateful-linear-layer}

In TinyTorch, all neural network components inherit from a common
\texttt{Layer} base class that establishes the framework's execution and
parameter tracking protocol.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ List}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Layer:}
    \CommentTok{"""Base class for all neural network layers in TinyTorch."""}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{"""Compute the forward transformation."""}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{NotImplementedError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Subclasses must implement forward()"}\NormalTok{)}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_call\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor, }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{args, }\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{kwargs) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{"""Allow layer instances to be called directly as functions."""}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.forward(x, }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{args, }\OperatorTok{**}\NormalTok{kwargs)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[Tensor]:}
        \CommentTok{"""Return a list of all trainable parameter Tensors."""}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ []}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_repr\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \SpecialStringTok{f"}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\VariableTok{self}\SpecialCharTok{.}\VariableTok{\_\_class\_\_}\SpecialCharTok{.}\VariableTok{\_\_name\_\_}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{()"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{Linear} layer (also termed a \emph{Dense} or \emph{Fully
Connected} layer) encapsulates two trainable parameter tensors:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Weight Matrix (\(\mathbf{W}\))} of shape
  \((d_{\text{in}}, d_{\text{out}})\), initialized with LeCun scaled
  normal noise.
\item
  \textbf{Bias Vector (\(\mathbf{b}\))} of shape \((d_{\text{out}},)\),
  initialized to zeros.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Linear(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Fully connected linear transformation: y = x @ W + b.}

\CommentTok{    Encapsulates trainable weight and bias parameter Tensors.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        in\_features: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        out\_features: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        bias: }\BuiltInTok{bool} \OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        dtype: np.dtype }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.float32}
\NormalTok{    ) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.in\_features }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ in\_features}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.out\_features }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ out\_features}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.use\_bias }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ bias}

        \CommentTok{\# LeCun weight initialization: std = sqrt(1 / in\_features)}
\NormalTok{        std }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.sqrt(}\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ in\_features)}
\NormalTok{        weight\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.random.normal(}\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, std, (in\_features, out\_features)).astype(dtype)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(weight\_data, requires\_grad}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ bias:}
\NormalTok{            bias\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.zeros(out\_features, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{dtype)}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(bias\_data, requires\_grad}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{None}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{        Compute affine projection: y = x @ W + b.}

\CommentTok{        Args:}
\CommentTok{            x: Input Tensor of shape (batch\_size, in\_features)}
\CommentTok{        Returns:}
\CommentTok{            Output Tensor of shape (batch\_size, out\_features)}
\CommentTok{        """}
        \CommentTok{\# Matrix multiplication: (B, in\_features) @ (in\_features, out\_features) {-}\textgreater{} (B, out\_features)}
\NormalTok{        out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.matmul(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight)}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
            \CommentTok{\# Broadcasting addition: (B, out\_features) + (out\_features,)}
\NormalTok{            out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ out }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ out}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[Tensor]:}
        \CommentTok{"""Return encapsulated trainable parameters."""}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias]}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight]}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_repr\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \SpecialStringTok{f"Linear(in\_features=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\VariableTok{self}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{in\_features}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{, out\_features=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\VariableTok{self}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{out\_features}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{, bias=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\VariableTok{self}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{use\_bias}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{)"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Regularization via Inverted
Dropout}\label{regularization-via-inverted-dropout}

Deep neural networks with millions of parameters are susceptible to
\textbf{co-adaptation}, where individual neurons rely on the specific
presence of other neurons to make predictions, leading to overfitting on
training samples.

\textbf{Dropout} mitigates co-adaptation by randomly setting a fraction
\(p \in [0, 1)\) of activation units to zero during forward execution.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/03_layers-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-dropout-architecture}Standard vs.~Inverted Dropout
Execution. During training, a random binary mask zeroes activations with
probability \(p\). Inverted Dropout scales surviving units by
\(\frac{1}{1-p}\), ensuring test-time evaluation requires zero
arithmetic scaling.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{The Inverted Dropout Systems
Invariant}\label{the-inverted-dropout-systems-invariant}

In classical dropout (Hinton et al., 2012), activations were multiplied
by a Bernoulli mask during training, which reduced the expected
activation sum by a factor of \((1 - p)\). To compensate, test-time
inference required multiplying all model weights or activations by
\((1 - p)\).

In production ML systems, modifying weights or executing scaling
operations during inference creates severe systems overhead:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Quantization Invalidation}: Multiplying pre-quantized INT8
  weights by arbitrary floating-point factors destroys quantization
  scales.
\item
  \textbf{Inference Latency}: Launching an element-wise scaling kernel
  at every layer wastes memory bandwidth on latency-critical edge
  devices.
\end{enumerate}

Modern frameworks resolve this by using \textbf{Inverted Dropout}: by
scaling surviving activations by \(\frac{1}{1 - p}\) \emph{during
training}, the mathematical expectation is preserved immediately:

\[\mathbb{E}[y_{\text{train}}] = \mathbb{E}\left[\frac{x \cdot \text{mask}}{1 - p}\right] = \frac{x \cdot (1 - p)}{1 - p} = x = \mathbb{E}[y_{\text{eval}}]\]

During inference, evaluation mode becomes a \textbf{pure cost-free
identity pass} (\(y = x\)), requiring zero kernel launches and zero
memory operations.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Dropout(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Inverted Dropout regularization layer.}

\CommentTok{    Randomly zeroes elements with probability p during training.}
\CommentTok{    Scales surviving elements by 1 / (1 {-} p) to preserve expectation.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, p: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not} \FloatTok{0.0} \OperatorTok{\textless{}=}\NormalTok{ p }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{1.0}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{ValueError}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Dropout probability must be in [0, 1), got }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{p}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.p }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ p}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.training }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{True}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ train(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Enable training mode (stochastic dropout active)."""}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.training }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{True}

    \KeywordTok{def} \BuiltInTok{eval}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Enable evaluation mode (deterministic identity pass)."""}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.training }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{False}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{\# In evaluation mode or when p=0, dropout is a pure no{-}op identity}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.training }\KeywordTok{or} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.p }\OperatorTok{==} \FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ x}

        \CommentTok{\# Generate binary mask: True with probability (1 {-} p)}
\NormalTok{        mask }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (np.random.random(x.shape) }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.p).astype(x.dtype)}
        \CommentTok{\# Scale surviving elements by 1 / (1 {-} p)}
\NormalTok{        scale }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{{-}} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.p)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(x.data }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ mask }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ scale)}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_repr\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \SpecialStringTok{f"Dropout(p=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\VariableTok{self}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{p}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{, training=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\VariableTok{self}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{training}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{)"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Pitfall: Forgetting to Toggle \texttt{.eval()}
During
Inference}{Pitfall: Forgetting to Toggle .eval() During Inference}}\label{pitfall-forgetting-to-toggle-.eval-during-inference}

If a model is evaluated in \texttt{.train()} mode, randomly dropping
50\% of activations introduces stochastic noise into test predictions,
severely degrading benchmark accuracy. Always toggle
\texttt{model.eval()} before running inference.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Composition: The Sequential
Container}\label{composition-the-sequential-container}

With \texttt{Linear}, \texttt{Dropout}, and our activation suite
(\texttt{ReLU}, \texttt{GELU}, \texttt{Softmax} from the preceding
chapter), we can now assemble multi-layer neural network architectures.

The \texttt{Sequential} container encapsulates an ordered list of
layers, executing them in sequence:

\[\mathbf{x} \longrightarrow \text{Layer}_1 \longrightarrow \text{Layer}_2 \longrightarrow \dots \longrightarrow \text{Layer}_K \longrightarrow \mathbf{y}\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Sequential(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""Sequential container executing layers in linear succession."""}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{layers: Layer) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.layers }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{list}\NormalTok{(layers)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ train(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Set all sub{-}layers to training mode."""}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ layer }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.layers:}
            \ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{hasattr}\NormalTok{(layer, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}train\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{                layer.train()}

    \KeywordTok{def} \BuiltInTok{eval}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Set all sub{-}layers to evaluation mode."""}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ layer }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.layers:}
            \ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{hasattr}\NormalTok{(layer, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}eval\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{                layer.}\BuiltInTok{eval}\NormalTok{()}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{"""Sequentially pass input through all layers."""}
\NormalTok{        out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ layer }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.layers:}
\NormalTok{            out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ layer(out)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ out}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[Tensor]:}
        \CommentTok{"""Recursively collect parameters across all sub{-}layers."""}
\NormalTok{        params }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ layer }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.layers:}
\NormalTok{            params.extend(layer.parameters())}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ params}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_repr\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        layer\_strs }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{"}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{  "}\NormalTok{.join(}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{(layer) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ layer }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.layers)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \SpecialStringTok{f"Sequential(}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\SpecialStringTok{  }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{layer\_strs}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\SpecialStringTok{)"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Running a Complete Multi-Layer Forward
Pass}\label{running-a-complete-multi-layer-forward-pass}

We can now construct a 3-layer Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) for digit
classification (such as MNIST: 784 input pixels, 128 hidden units, 10
output classes) and execute a forward pass:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.activations }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ ReLU, Softmax}

\CommentTok{\# Construct the multi{-}layer architecture}
\NormalTok{model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Sequential(}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{784}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    Dropout(p}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    Softmax(axis}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Generate a synthetic batch of 16 images}
\NormalTok{batch\_x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.random.randn(}\DecValTok{16}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{784}\NormalTok{).astype(np.float32))}

\CommentTok{\# Execute forward pass}
\NormalTok{predictions }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ model(batch\_x)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Output shape: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{predictions}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{shape}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{\# (16, 10)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Total parameter tensors: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(model.parameters())}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{\# 6 (3 weights, 3 biases)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Systems Reality: Parameter Counting and Storage
Footprint}\label{systems-reality-parameter-counting-and-storage-footprint}

Every parameter in a neural network is a float in memory. When designing
frameworks, we must calculate the exact physical memory required to
store model weights:

\[\text{Params}_{\text{Linear}} = (d_{\text{in}} \times d_{\text{out}}) + d_{\text{out}}\]

\[\text{Memory} = \text{Total Parameters} \times \text{sizeof}(\text{dtype})\]

\section{Check Your Understanding: Calculating Network Weight
Footprint}\label{check-your-understanding-calculating-network-weight-footprint}

Consider the 3-layer MLP constructed above:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Layer 1: \texttt{Linear(784,\ 128)}
\item
  Layer 2: \texttt{Linear(128,\ 64)}
\item
  Layer 3: \texttt{Linear(64,\ 10)}
\end{itemize}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate the total number of learnable parameters in the network.
\item
  How many bytes of RAM are required to store these parameters in 32-bit
  float (\texttt{float32}, 4 bytes per element)?
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Solution:} - Layer 1:
\((784 \times 128) + 128 = 100,352 + 128 = 100,480\text{ params}\). -
Layer 2: \((128 \times 64) + 64 = 8,192 + 64 = 8,256\text{ params}\). -
Layer 3: \((64 \times 10) + 10 = 640 + 10 = 650\text{ params}\). - Total
Parameters: \(100,480 + 8,256 + 650 = 109,386\text{ parameters}\). -
Physical Memory:
\(109,386 \times 4\text{ bytes} = 437,544\text{ bytes} \approx 427.3\text{ KB}\).

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Production Reality: How PyTorch Implements Linear Layers and
Fast
Dropout}\label{production-reality-how-pytorch-implements-linear-layers-and-fast-dropout}

In production frameworks like PyTorch, layers and regularization are
mapped directly to high-throughput C++ and CUDA libraries.\footnote{Traditional
  linear congruential PRNGs maintain sequential state in memory,
  serializing parallel GPU threads. The Philox counter-based PRNG
  (Salmon et al., 2011) evaluates a stateless cryptographic bijection
  using only thread IDs and clock cycles as counter inputs, allowing
  millions of CUDA threads to generate independent, un-correlated random
  masks in parallel without memory locks.}

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PyTorch Layer & Regularization Systems Pipeline            │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. nn.Linear Execution: at::linear -> at::addmm            │
│     • CPU: Intel MKL / OpenBLAS cblas_sgemm                 │
│     • GPU: NVIDIA cuBLAS cublasGemmEx / CUTLASS kernels     │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  2. nn.Dropout Vectorization: at::native::dropout_cuda      │
│     • Hardware Philox-4x32-10 PRNG in GPU registers         │
│     • 128-bit vector instructions (float4 mask apply)       │
│     • Test-Time Zero-Overhead Identity Bypass               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{torch.nn.Linear}}: In C++
  (\texttt{aten/src/ATen/native/Linear.cpp}), \texttt{at::linear}
  dispatches directly to \texttt{at::addmm}
  (\(\mathbf{Y} = \beta \mathbf{b} + \alpha \mathbf{X} \mathbf{W}\)). On
  CPU, it invokes multi-threaded BLAS routines (\texttt{cblas\_sgemm});
  on GPU, it calls cuBLAS or CUTLASS to stream matrix blocks across GPU
  Tensor Cores.
\item
  \textbf{Vectorized Dropout with Philox PRNG}: Rather than generating
  random numbers in slow DRAM arrays, PyTorch's CUDA dropout kernel uses
  the \textbf{Philox-4x32-10} counter-based pseudo-random number
  generator. Each thread computes 4 random uniform floats entirely
  within hardware registers, masks 4 \texttt{float32} numbers
  simultaneously using 128-bit vector memory instructions
  (\texttt{float4}), and scales surviving floats by \(\frac{1}{1-p}\) in
  a single clock cycle.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-3}

In this chapter, we built the third tier of TinyTorch: stateful layers,
parameter management, and multi-layer network composition. We proved why
uncalibrated random weight initialization causes exponential activation
explosion, and derived the LeCun and He variance conservation scaling
laws (\(1/\sqrt{\text{fan\_in}}\) vs \(\sqrt{2/\text{fan\_in}}\)) that
stabilize signal flow through deep linear layers.

We implemented the \texttt{Linear} layer encapsulating trainable weight
and bias tensors, constructed \texttt{Dropout} regularization using the
inverted scaling invariant (\(\frac{1}{1-p}\)) to guarantee zero
inference overhead, and built the \texttt{Sequential} container to
compose layers into complete forward pipelines. With tensors,
activations, and layers now functional, we have built a complete
forward-pass neural network runtime.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Variance Conservation}: Initializing weights with standard
  normal noise explodes activation magnitude; scaling variance by
  \(1/\text{fan\_in}\) (LeCun) or \(2/\text{fan\_in}\) (He) keeps signal
  variance constant across layers.
\item
  \textbf{Stateful Layer Encapsulation}: Unlike stateless activations,
  the \texttt{Linear} layer manages trainable \texttt{Tensor} parameter
  buffers registered via the \texttt{parameters()} protocol.
\item
  \textbf{Inverted Dropout Invariant}: Scaling training activations by
  \(\frac{1}{1-p}\) maintains the mathematical expectation
  \(\mathbb{E}[y] = x\), allowing evaluation mode to run as a cost-free
  identity pass.
\item
  \textbf{Execution Mode Switching}: Models must maintain explicit
  \texttt{.train()} and \texttt{.eval()} state flags to toggle
  stochastic behavior between training and evaluation.
\item
  \textbf{Sequential Composition}: The \texttt{Sequential} container
  executes layers in linear order and recursively aggregates parameters
  across all sub-modules.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Measuring Model
Error}\label{chapter-connection-measuring-model-error}

Our framework can now construct and execute multi-layer neural networks,
but how do we quantify how well a network's predictions match
ground-truth labels? In \textbf{Objective Functions and Loss
Formulations}, we build objective loss functions (MSE, Binary
Cross-Entropy, and Softmax Cross-Entropy), analyze numerical stability
in log-space, and measure forward prediction error.

\chapter{Objective Functions and Loss
Formulations}\label{objective-functions-and-loss-formulations}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does computing loss in probability space corrupt numerical
precision compared to log-space?}

A neural network's forward pass maps input data into raw continuous
predictions or unnormalized logit vectors. To steer the network, the
framework must evaluate an objective function that quantifies the
discrepancy between model predictions and ground-truth targets as a
single scalar error value. In production deep learning frameworks, loss
calculation is not merely a mathematical formula; it is a critical
numerical stability boundary. Naive implementations that first convert
raw logits into probability distributions via Softmax and subsequently
compute logarithms (\(\log(\text{Softmax}(\mathbf{z}))\)) suffer from
catastrophic floating-point underflow whenever probabilities approach
zero (\(\log(0) \to -\infty \to \text{NaN}\)). To maintain numerical
precision across extreme dynamic ranges, frameworks fuse activation
normalization and error measurement into a single unified mathematical
invariant: the \textbf{Log-Sum-Exp operator}. In a framework runtime,
loss functions are the physical calibration dials where statistical
likelihood is stabilized against the hardware limits of IEEE 754
arithmetic.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-4}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Construct} modular, reusable loss classes (\texttt{MSELoss},
  \texttt{BCELoss}, \texttt{CrossEntropyLoss}) operating seamlessly over
  multidimensional \texttt{Tensor} containers.
\item
  \textbf{Implement} epsilon-clamping safeguards to prevent \(\log(0)\)
  register crashes and NaN corruption in binary cross-entropy
  evaluation.
\item
  \textbf{Derive} and \textbf{implement} the Log-Sum-Exp numerical
  invariant in \texttt{log\_softmax} to execute categorical
  cross-entropy in a single, overflow-proof pass.
\item
  \textbf{Support} flexible framework target formats, handling both
  sparse integer class indices and dense one-hot probability vectors.
\item
  \textbf{Connect} our multi-layer \texttt{Sequential} architecture
  (built in the preceding chapter) directly to \texttt{CrossEntropyLoss}
  to measure forward prediction error.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Framework Role of Loss
Functions}\label{the-framework-role-of-loss-functions}

In a machine learning runtime, the forward pass produces an output
tensor \(\hat{\mathbf{y}}\) (predictions or logits) corresponding to a
batch of input samples. The loss function takes \(\hat{\mathbf{y}}\) and
the ground-truth target tensor \(\mathbf{y}\), and reduces them to a
single scalar \texttt{Tensor}:

\[\mathcal{L} = \text{Loss}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}, \mathbf{y})\]

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Input Batch (B, D_in)  ──>  Sequential Model               │
│                                    │                        │
│                                    ▼                        │
│  Target Labels (B,)     ──>  Predictions / Logits (B, C)    │
│            │                       │                        │
│            └───────────────┬───────┘                        │
│                            ▼                                │
│                   Loss Function                             │
│                            │                                │
│                            ▼                                │
│                   Scalar Loss L (0D Tensor)                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

The computed loss scalar serves as the definitive numerical score of
prediction quality:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If predictions match targets perfectly, \(\mathcal{L} \to 0\).
\item
  If predictions diverge from targets, \(\mathcal{L}\) grows, providing
  the magnitude of error that will subsequently guide optimization.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Mean Squared Error (MSELoss) for Continuous
Regression}\label{mean-squared-error-mseloss-for-continuous-regression}

For regression tasks (such as predicting continuous physical
measurements, prices, or coordinates), the standard objective is
\textbf{Mean Squared Error (MSE)}.

Given a prediction tensor
\(\hat{\mathbf{y}} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times D}\) and target tensor
\(\mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times D}\), MSE computes the average
squared difference across all \(N = B \times D\) elements:

\[\mathcal{L}_{\text{MSE}}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}, \mathbf{y}) = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N (\hat{y}_i - y_i)^2\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ MSELoss:}
    \CommentTok{"""Mean Squared Error loss for continuous regression."""}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, predictions: Tensor, targets: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{        Compute mean squared error between predictions and targets.}

\CommentTok{        Args:}
\CommentTok{            predictions: Model output Tensor of shape (B, ...)}
\CommentTok{            targets: Ground{-}truth Tensor of identical shape (B, ...)}
\CommentTok{        Returns:}
\CommentTok{            Scalar Tensor containing mean squared error}
\CommentTok{        """}
\NormalTok{        diff }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ predictions.data }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ targets.data}
\NormalTok{        loss\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.mean(diff }\OperatorTok{**} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(loss\_val)}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_call\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, predictions: Tensor, targets: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.forward(predictions, targets)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Properties of MSE}:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Non-negativity}: \(\mathcal{L}_{\text{MSE}} \ge 0\), with
  equality if and only if \(\hat{\mathbf{y}} = \mathbf{y}\).
\item
  \textbf{Outlier Sensitivity}: Because errors are squared, a prediction
  error of \(10.0\) generates a penalty 100× greater than an error of
  \(1.0\), heavily discouraging large deviations.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Binary Cross-Entropy (BCELoss) and Epsilon
Clamping}\label{binary-cross-entropy-bceloss-and-epsilon-clamping}

For binary classification problems (where targets are discrete labels
\(y \in \{0, 1\}\) and predictions are probabilities
\(\hat{y} \in (0, 1)\) produced by a Sigmoid activation), the objective
is \textbf{Binary Cross-Entropy (BCE)}:

\[\mathcal{L}_{\text{BCE}}(\hat{\mathbf{y}}, \mathbf{y}) = -\frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N \left[ y_i \log(\hat{y}_i) + (1 - y_i) \log(1 - \hat{y}_i) \right]\]

\subsection{The Logarithm Hazard in IEEE
754}\label{the-logarithm-hazard-in-ieee-754}

If a model predicts \(\hat{y} = 0.0\) for a sample whose true label is
\(y = 1\), evaluating \(\log(\hat{y}) = \log(0.0)\) produces
\(-\infty\). In subsequent arithmetic, evaluating \(0 \times (-\infty)\)
or subtracting \(-\infty\) produces \texttt{NaN} (Not a Number),
crashing execution.

To guarantee complete numerical robustness, the framework must clamp
predictions to an interior interval \([\epsilon, 1 - \epsilon]\) where
\(\epsilon = 10^{-7}\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{EPSILON }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1e{-}7}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ BCELoss:}
    \CommentTok{"""Binary Cross{-}Entropy loss with numerical epsilon clamping."""}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, eps: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ EPSILON) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ eps}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, predictions: Tensor, targets: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{\# Clamp predictions to [eps, 1 {-} eps] to prevent log(0) {-}\textgreater{} {-}inf}
\NormalTok{        preds\_clamped }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.clip(predictions.data, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps, }\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{{-}} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps)}
\NormalTok{        targets\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ targets.data}

        \CommentTok{\# Compute element{-}wise cross{-}entropy}
\NormalTok{        bce }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(targets\_data }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ np.log(preds\_clamped) }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ targets\_data) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ np.log(}\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ preds\_clamped))}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.mean(bce))}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_call\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, predictions: Tensor, targets: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.forward(predictions, targets)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Categorical Cross-Entropy and the Log-Sum-Exp
Invariant}\label{categorical-cross-entropy-and-the-log-sum-exp-invariant}

For multi-class classification across \(C\) mutually exclusive classes
(such as classifying handwritten digits \(0\dots 9\) or predicting the
next token in a language model vocabulary), the model produces raw
unnormalized scores \(\mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{R}^C\) called
\textbf{logits}.

The mathematical cross-entropy for a sample with target class index
\(y \in \{0, \dots, C-1\}\) is the negative log-probability assigned to
the correct class:

\[\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}(\mathbf{z}, y) = -\log\left(\text{Softmax}(\mathbf{z})_y\right) = -\log\left(\frac{e^{z_y}}{\sum_{j=1}^C e^{z_j}}\right)\]

\subsection{The Underflow Trap of Naive Softmax +
Log}\label{the-underflow-trap-of-naive-softmax-log}

Suppose an implementation naively computes probabilities
\(\mathbf{p} = \text{Softmax}(\mathbf{z})\) in linear space and then
evaluates \(\log(\mathbf{p}_y)\).

If an unnormalized logit \(z_y\) is \(-100.0\) relative to other logits,
the resulting probability \(p_y = e^{-100} \approx 3.7 \times 10^{-44}\)
underflows standard 32-bit floating-point precision
(\(< 1.175 \times 10^{-38}\)), collapsing to \texttt{0.0}. Taking
\(\log(0.0)\) produces \(-\infty\), corrupting the loss with
\texttt{NaN}.

Conversely, if logits are large (\(z_j > 88.72\)), evaluating
\(e^{z_j}\) overflows \texttt{float32} registers to \texttt{+inf},
leading to \texttt{inf\ /\ inf\ =\ NaN}.

\section{The Log-Sum-Exp Invariant}\label{the-log-sum-exp-invariant}

Expanding the logarithm of the Softmax quotient algebraically yields:

\[\log\left(\text{Softmax}(\mathbf{z})_i\right) = \log\left(\frac{e^{z_i}}{\sum_{j=1}^C e^{z_j}}\right) = z_i - \log\left(\sum_{j=1}^C e^{z_j}\right)\]

Applying the shift-invariance property with \(M = \max_j(z_j)\):

\[\sum_{j=1}^C e^{z_j} = \sum_{j=1}^C e^{z_j - M + M} = e^M \sum_{j=1}^C e^{z_j - M}\]

\[\log\left(\sum_{j=1}^C e^{z_j}\right) = M + \log\left(\sum_{j=1}^C e^{z_j - M}\right)\]

Substituting back into the log-softmax formula gives the exact
\textbf{Log-Sum-Exp Invariant}:

\[\log(\text{Softmax}(\mathbf{z})_i) = (z_i - M) - \log\left(\sum_{j=1}^C e^{z_j - M}\right)\]

This formula possesses two vital numerical stability guarantees:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{No Overflow}: Because \(z_j - M \le 0\) for all \(j\), the
  exponential terms \(e^{z_j - M} \in (0, 1]\) \textbf{never exceed
  1.0}.
\item
  \textbf{No Underflow to Zero}: Because the maximum element satisfies
  \(z_{\text{argmax}} - M = 0\), the sum always contains \(e^0 = 1.0\),
  guaranteeing \(\sum_{j=1}^C e^{z_j - M} \ge 1.0\). The logarithm
  argument is strictly bounded away from zero
  (\(\log(\ge 1.0) \ge 0.0\)), making \(\log(0) \to -\infty\)
  mathematically impossible.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ log\_softmax(x: Tensor, dim: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Compute log{-}softmax using the Log{-}Sum{-}Exp numerical stability trick.}

\CommentTok{    Args:}
\CommentTok{        x: Input Tensor of unnormalized logits}
\CommentTok{        dim: Dimension along which to compute log{-}softmax (default: {-}1)}
\CommentTok{    Returns:}
\CommentTok{        Tensor of exact log{-}probabilities}
\CommentTok{    """}
\NormalTok{    x\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.data}
    \CommentTok{\# 1. Compute maximum along target dimension for stability}
\NormalTok{    max\_vals }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(x\_data, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{dim, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
    \CommentTok{\# 2. Subtract maximum to guarantee all exponents \textless{}= 0}
\NormalTok{    shifted }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x\_data }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ max\_vals}
    \CommentTok{\# 3. Compute log{-}sum{-}exp}
\NormalTok{    log\_sum\_exp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.log(np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(np.exp(shifted), axis}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{dim, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{))}
    \CommentTok{\# 4. Return log{-}probabilities: (x {-} max) {-} log(sum(exp(x {-} max)))}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(shifted }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ log\_sum\_exp)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/04_losses-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-log-sum-exp-pipeline}Naive Softmax vs.~Fused
Log-Sum-Exp Pipeline. Computing probabilities in linear space risks
exponent overflow and log underflow. Fusing the operations into the
Log-Sum-Exp invariant operates directly on shifted logits, guaranteeing
stable IEEE 754 precision.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Systems Reality: Kernel Fusion and VRAM Bandwidth
Reduction}\label{systems-reality-kernel-fusion-and-vram-bandwidth-reduction}

Beyond numerical stability, fusing \texttt{log\_softmax} with
cross-entropy creates enormous memory-bandwidth savings on hardware
accelerators.

Consider a large language model with vocabulary size \(C = 128,000\)
processing a batch of \(B = 4096\) tokens:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Naive Non-Fused Softmax + NLL}:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Softmax reads logits \((4096 \times 128,000)\) from DRAM, computes
    probabilities, and writes probabilities
    \((4096 \times 128,000 \times 4\text{ B} \approx 2.1\text{ GB})\)
    back to DRAM.
  \item
    Cross-entropy reads \(2.1\text{ GB}\) of probabilities from DRAM,
    computes logs, indexes target tokens, and writes loss.
  \item
    \textbf{Total DRAM Traffic}: Over \(4.2\text{ GB}\) of high-latency
    memory bus traffic.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Fused Log-Sum-Exp Loss Kernel}:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    The GPU kernel loads logit tiles directly into on-chip SRAM
    registers.
  \item
    Threads compute the maximum logit \(M\) and sum-of-exponentials
    \(\sum e^{z_j - M}\) using fast in-register warp shuffle
    reductions.\footnote{On NVIDIA GPUs, warp shuffle instructions
      (\texttt{\_\_shfl\_xor\_sync}, \texttt{\_\_shfl\_down\_sync})
      allow threads in a 32-lane warp to exchange register values
      directly without writing to shared memory or global DRAM, reducing
      parallel reduction latency to just 1--2 clock cycles.}
  \item
    The kernel indexes the ground-truth target logit directly in
    registers and writes back only \(B\) scalar loss values
    (\(4096 \times 4\text{ B} \approx 16\text{ KB}\)).
  \item
    \textbf{Total DRAM Traffic}: \(16\text{ KB}\) written---a
    \textbf{130,000× reduction in memory traffic}!
  \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsection{Building the Complete CrossEntropyLoss
Layer}\label{building-the-complete-crossentropyloss-layer}

In TinyTorch (as in PyTorch), \texttt{CrossEntropyLoss} takes
\textbf{raw unnormalized logits} directly from the final \texttt{Linear}
layer, bypassing explicit \texttt{Softmax} calls. It supports both 1D
integer class label targets and 2D one-hot probability distributions:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ CrossEntropyLoss:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Cross{-}Entropy loss combining log\_softmax and Negative Log{-}Likelihood (NLL).}

\CommentTok{    Expects unnormalized logits directly from the model.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, logits: Tensor, targets: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{        Compute categorical cross{-}entropy loss.}

\CommentTok{        Args:}
\CommentTok{            logits: Unnormalized logits Tensor of shape (B, C)}
\CommentTok{            targets: Class indices (B,) or one{-}hot vectors (B, C)}
\CommentTok{        Returns:}
\CommentTok{            Scalar Tensor containing average cross{-}entropy loss}
\CommentTok{        """}
\NormalTok{        batch\_size }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ logits.shape[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
        \CommentTok{\# Compute numerically stable log{-}probabilities}
\NormalTok{        log\_probs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ log\_softmax(logits, dim}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{).data}

        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ targets.ndim }\OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}
            \CommentTok{\# Sparse integer class indices: shape (B,)}
\NormalTok{            target\_indices }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ targets.data.astype(}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{)}
            \CommentTok{\# Index correct class log{-}probability for each sample in batch}
\NormalTok{            sample\_losses }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{log\_probs[np.arange(batch\_size), target\_indices]}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
            \CommentTok{\# One{-}hot encoded probability distributions: shape (B, C)}
\NormalTok{            sample\_losses }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(targets.data }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ log\_probs, axis}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}

        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.mean(sample\_losses))}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_call\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, logits: Tensor, targets: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.forward(logits, targets)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Pitfall: Double Softmax
Application}\label{pitfall-double-softmax-application}

A frequent beginner mistake is adding a \texttt{Softmax} layer at the
end of a \texttt{Sequential} network and then passing the output to
\texttt{CrossEntropyLoss}. Because \texttt{CrossEntropyLoss} applies
\texttt{log\_softmax} internally, passing already-softmaxxed
probabilities applies Softmax twice, destroying gradient scaling. Always
output raw unnormalized logits from the final \texttt{Linear} layer when
training with \texttt{CrossEntropyLoss}.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Production Reality: How PyTorch Dispatches Cross-Entropy in
C++}\label{production-reality-how-pytorch-dispatches-cross-entropy-in-c}

In PyTorch, \texttt{torch.nn.CrossEntropyLoss} is implemented in ATen
native C++ (\texttt{aten/src/ATen/native/Loss.cpp} and
\texttt{aten/src/ATen/native/SoftMax.cpp}).\footnote{In PyTorch ATen
  (\texttt{aten/src/ATen/native/Loss.cpp}),
  \texttt{cross\_entropy\_loss} combines log-softmax normalization and
  negative log-likelihood indexing, eliminating intermediate probability
  allocations and guaranteeing IEEE 754 numerical stability across
  arbitrary dynamic ranges.}

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PyTorch CrossEntropyLoss Dispatch Pipeline                 │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. Python API: torch.nn.CrossEntropyLoss()(logits, target) │
│     • Accepts raw unnormalized logits                       │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  2. ATen C++ Dispatcher: at::cross_entropy_loss             │
│     ├── at::_log_softmax (Warp-Level Parallel Reduction)   │
│     └── at::nll_loss_forward (Fused Sparse Gather Indexing) │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  3. CUDA Kernel Architecture                                │
│     • Two-pass parallel reduction via __shfl_sync           │
│     • O(B) DRAM writes instead of O(B * C)                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Dispatcher Routing}: In PyTorch ATen,
  \texttt{cross\_entropy\_loss} dispatches to
  \texttt{at::\_log\_softmax} followed by
  \texttt{at::nll\_loss\_forward}.
\item
  \textbf{Warp-Level Reduction}: In CUDA (\texttt{SoftMax.cu}), the
  \texttt{log\_softmax} kernel assigns each batch row to a CUDA thread
  block or warp. Threads compute the row maximum and the sum of shifted
  exponentials entirely across registers using warp shuffle intrinsics.
\item
  \textbf{Fused Sparse Indexing}: In \texttt{nll\_loss\_forward}, when
  targets are provided as 1D class indices, the kernel directly gathers
  the target log-probability per batch item in parallel without
  allocating a full \((B, C)\) target distribution.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Forward Error Evaluation: Connecting Networks to
Loss}\label{forward-error-evaluation-connecting-networks-to-loss}

We can now connect the entire forward execution pipeline: passing an
input batch through our multi-layer \texttt{Sequential} network (built
in the preceding chapter), generating raw logits, and calculating the
exact forward prediction error using \texttt{CrossEntropyLoss}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Linear, Dropout, Sequential}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.activations }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ ReLU}

\CommentTok{\# 1. Define 3{-}layer MLP for digit classification (outputting raw logits)}
\NormalTok{model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Sequential(}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{784}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    Dropout(p}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{\# No Softmax: outputs raw logits!}
\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# 2. Instantiate CrossEntropyLoss}
\NormalTok{criterion }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ CrossEntropyLoss()}

\CommentTok{\# 3. Create synthetic batch of 8 images and ground{-}truth digit labels}
\NormalTok{batch\_x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.random.randn(}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{784}\NormalTok{).astype(np.float32))}
\NormalTok{batch\_y }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.array([}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{]))  }\CommentTok{\# True digit classes}

\CommentTok{\# 4. Execute forward pass to obtain logits}
\NormalTok{logits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ model(batch\_x)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Logits Shape: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{logits}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{shape}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{\# (8, 10)}

\CommentTok{\# 5. Evaluate scalar forward loss}
\NormalTok{loss }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ criterion(logits, batch\_y)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Forward Evaluation Loss: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{loss}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{data}\SpecialCharTok{:.4f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Check Your Understanding: Initial Random Loss
Benchmark}\label{check-your-understanding-initial-random-loss-benchmark}

For a randomly initialized model with 10 balanced classes (\(C = 10\)),
what is the expected initial cross-entropy loss before any training
occurs?

\emph{Solution:} Before learning begins, a properly initialized model
assigns equal unnormalized logits to all \(C = 10\) classes
(\(p_i \approx \frac{1}{10} = 0.1\)).

\[\mathcal{L}_{\text{expected}} = -\log(0.1) = \log(10) \approx 2.3026\]

Checking that your initial forward loss is close to \(\log(C)\)
(\(\approx 2.30\) for 10-class MNIST or CIFAR-10) is a universal systems
sanity check to verify weight initialization and loss calculations
before starting training.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-4}

In this chapter, we built the fourth tier of TinyTorch: objective
functions and error measurement. We explored the mathematical
formulation of error across continuous regression (\texttt{MSELoss}),
binary classification (\texttt{BCELoss}), and multi-class categorical
classification (\texttt{CrossEntropyLoss}).

We analyzed the critical numerical hazards of computing logarithms on
digital hardware, implementing epsilon-clamping to prevent \(\log(0)\)
crashes and deriving the \textbf{Log-Sum-Exp invariant} to execute
log-softmax in a single, overflow-proof pass. We quantified how kernel
fusion in cross-entropy reduces DRAM write traffic by over 130,000×, and
demonstrated the complete forward evaluation pipeline by connecting our
multi-layer \texttt{Sequential} model directly to
\texttt{CrossEntropyLoss} to measure forward prediction error.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-4}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The Loss Contract}: Loss functions reduce multidimensional
  prediction and target tensors into a single scalar \texttt{Tensor}
  measuring prediction error.
\item
  \textbf{The Logarithm Hazard}: Evaluating \(\log(0)\) produces
  \(-\infty\), which creates NaN values in downstream arithmetic;
  epsilon clamping (\(10^{-7}\)) safeguards binary classification.
\item
  \textbf{Log-Sum-Exp Invariant}: Computing Softmax in linear
  probability space risks exponential overflow and underflow; fusing
  normalization into log-space (\((z_i - M) - \log\sum e^{z_j - M}\))
  guarantees numerical stability.
\item
  \textbf{VRAM Bandwidth Reduction}: Fusing log-softmax with negative
  log-likelihood eliminates gigabytes of intermediate probability matrix
  DRAM allocations.
\item
  \textbf{Raw Logits Convention}: Production frameworks require models
  to output raw unnormalized logits directly to
  \texttt{CrossEntropyLoss}, avoiding redundant and numerically fragile
  double-softmax passes.
\item
  \textbf{Initial Loss Sanity Check}: For balanced \(C\)-class
  classification, the initial forward loss of an untrained network
  should approximate \(\log(C)\).
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Feeding Data at
Scale}\label{chapter-connection-feeding-data-at-scale}

Our framework can now construct multi-layer models and compute
prediction error, but where do batches of training samples come from? In
\textbf{Data Pipelines, Datasets, and Batching}, we build the
\texttt{Dataset} and \texttt{DataLoader} abstractions to manage
shuffling, mini-batch chunking, and memory-efficient data loading
pipelines.

\chapter{Data Pipelines, Datasets, and
Batching}\label{data-pipelines-datasets-and-batching}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why is the data loading pipeline the primary determinant of
whether hardware compute stays saturated?}

Deep learning accelerators and multi-core CPUs are designed to process
dense matrix arithmetic at massive throughput. If training data is fed
to the model as individual single samples, the runtime is trapped in
low-intensity memory stalls, starving arithmetic registers while waiting
for isolated memory fetches. To achieve high arithmetic throughput,
frameworks must decouple physical storage from model execution through a
two-tiered software pipeline: the \textbf{Dataset} abstraction (which
defines sample-level retrieval) and the \textbf{DataLoader} engine
(which orchestrates mini-batch collation, index shuffling without memory
copying, and lazy batch generation). In a framework runtime, the data
pipeline is the physical I/O valve that bridges uncompressed storage on
disk with high-bandwidth memory in compute registers.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-5}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Construct} the \texttt{Dataset} abstract base class and
  \texttt{TensorDataset} container to decouple storage representation
  from downstream execution.
\item
  \textbf{Implement} the \texttt{DataLoader} engine with index
  permutation shuffling, avoiding expensive physical memory movement and
  data duplication.
\item
  \textbf{Analyze} the arithmetic intensity shift when moving from
  single-sample processing (\(B = 1\)) to mini-batch GEMM matrix
  operations (\(B \ge 32\)) using the Roofline model.
\item
  \textbf{Build} a lazy Python generator pipeline yielding batched
  \texttt{Tensor} tuples on-demand to enforce strict working-memory
  bounds.
\item
  \textbf{Execute} an end-to-end dataset evaluation pass, calculating
  running loss and multi-class classification accuracy across an entire
  dataset.
\item
  \textbf{Examine} production systems mechanisms: multi-process worker
  pools, POSIX shared memory IPC, and page-locked (pinned) DMA
  host-to-device transfers.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Systems Rationale for
Mini-Batching}\label{the-systems-rationale-for-mini-batching}

Consider evaluating a linear layer transformation
\(\mathbf{Y} = \mathbf{X} \mathbf{W}\) where weight matrix
\(\mathbf{W} \in \mathbb{R}^{D_{\text{in}} \times D_{\text{out}}}\)
operates on \(N\) input feature vectors of dimension \(D_{\text{in}}\).

\subsection{Arithmetic Intensity and the Roofline
Model}\label{arithmetic-intensity-and-the-roofline-model}

To understand why batch size determines hardware efficiency, we
formalize the \textbf{Arithmetic Intensity} (\(I\)), defined as the
ratio of floating-point operations performed to memory bytes transferred
across the memory bus:

\[I = \frac{\text{Total Floating-Point Operations (FLOPs)}}{\text{Total Memory Traffic (Bytes Transferred)}}\]

Let floating-point values occupy \(4\text{ bytes}\) (standard IEEE 754
\texttt{float32}). For a batch of size \(B\):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Floating-Point Operations}: Multiplying matrix
  \(\mathbf{X} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times D_{\text{in}}}\) by
  \(\mathbf{W} \in \mathbb{R}^{D_{\text{in}} \times D_{\text{out}}}\)
  requires \(B \times D_{\text{out}}\) dot products of length
  \(D_{\text{in}}\). Each dot product executes \(D_{\text{in}}\)
  multiplications and \(D_{\text{in}}\) additions (\(2 D_{\text{in}}\)
  FLOPs):

  \[\text{FLOPs} = 2 \times B \times D_{\text{in}} \times D_{\text{out}}\]
\item
  \textbf{Memory Traffic (DRAM Reads and Writes)}: Assuming cold caches,
  the hardware must read matrix \(\mathbf{X}\), read weight matrix
  \(\mathbf{W}\), and write result
  \(\mathbf{Y} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times D_{\text{out}}}\):

  \[\text{Bytes} = 4 \times \left( B \cdot D_{\text{in}} + D_{\text{in}} \cdot D_{\text{out}} + B \cdot D_{\text{out}} \right)\]
\item
  \textbf{Arithmetic Intensity Formulation}:

  \[I(B) = \frac{2 B D_{\text{in}} D_{\text{out}}}{4 \left( B D_{\text{in}} + D_{\text{in}} D_{\text{out}} + B D_{\text{out}} \right)} = \frac{B}{2 \left( \frac{B}{D_{\text{out}}} + 1 + \frac{B}{D_{\text{in}}} \right)}\]
\end{enumerate}

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Single Sample (B = 1): Vector-Matrix (GEMV)                │
│  • Memory Traffic: Load W for EVERY sample                  │
│  • Arithmetic Intensity: ~0.5 FLOP / Byte (Memory-Bound)    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Mini-Batch (B = 64): Matrix-Matrix (GEMM)                  │
│  • Memory Traffic: Load W ONCE for 64 samples               │
│  • Arithmetic Intensity: ~32 FLOPs / Byte (Compute-Bound)   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

When \(B = 1\) and dimensions are large
(\(D_{\text{in}} = D_{\text{out}} = 4096\)):

\[I(1) = \frac{1}{2 \left( \frac{1}{4096} + 1 + \frac{1}{4096} \right)} \approx 0.5\text{ FLOPs / Byte}\]

Modern GPUs (such as an NVIDIA H100 with \(3.35\text{ TB/s}\) HBM3
bandwidth and \(989\text{ TFLOPs}\) FP32 tensor throughput) have a
hardware machine balance ridge point of
\(\frac{989 \times 10^{12}}{3.35 \times 10^{12}} \approx 295\text{ FLOPs/Byte}\).
At \(I = 0.5\text{ FLOPs/Byte}\), the accelerator achieves less than
\(0.2\%\) of its theoretical peak compute, stalled waiting on memory
fetches from High Bandwidth Memory (HBM).

When batch size scales to \(B = 64\):
