\[I(64) = \frac{64}{2 \left( \frac{64}{4096} + 1 + \frac{64}{4096} \right)} \approx 31.0\text{ FLOPs / Byte}\]

Grouping samples into mini-batches amortizes the memory transfer cost of
loading weight tensor \(\mathbf{W}\) across all \(B\) samples, boosting
arithmetic intensity by \(60\times\) and moving execution into the
compute-bound plateau of the Roofline model.\footnote{The Roofline Model
  (Williams et al., 2009) defines operational performance as
  \(P = \min(\text{Peak Performance}, \text{Memory Bandwidth} \times I)\).}

Furthermore, mini-batching enforces strict memory bounds: instead of
loading an entire 1,000,000-sample dataset into RAM simultaneously, the
runtime keeps only the active mini-batch (\(B \times D\) elements) in
working cache memory.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Decoupling Storage: The Dataset
Abstraction}\label{decoupling-storage-the-dataset-abstraction}

In modern framework architecture, data management is partitioned into
two distinct responsibilities:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Access (Dataset)}: How do we fetch a single sample
  \((x_i, y_i)\) from storage?
\item
  \textbf{Organization (DataLoader)}: How do we group, shuffle, and
  collate samples into batches?
\end{enumerate}

The \texttt{Dataset} abstraction defines a clean Python interface
requiring two fundamental methods:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{\_\_len\_\_()}: Returns the total number of samples in the
  dataset.
\item
  \texttt{\_\_getitem\_\_(index)}: Returns the \(i\)-th sample tuple.
\end{itemize}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 5.1: The Dataset Abstract Base Class}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ abc }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ ABC, abstractmethod}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tuple, Any}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Dataset(ABC):}
    \CommentTok{"""Abstract base class for all datasets in TinyTorch."""}

    \AttributeTok{@abstractmethod}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_len\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Return the total number of samples in the dataset."""}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{NotImplementedError}

    \AttributeTok{@abstractmethod}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_getitem\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, index: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tuple[Any, ...]:}
        \CommentTok{"""Fetch the sample at the specified integer index."""}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{NotImplementedError}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Implementing
TensorDataset}\label{implementing-tensordataset}

When dataset features and labels already reside in memory as
\texttt{Tensor} objects, \texttt{TensorDataset} wraps them into a
unified dataset:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 5.2: TensorDataset Implementation}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tuple}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ TensorDataset(Dataset):}
    \CommentTok{"""Dataset wrapping multi{-}dimensional tensors."""}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{tensors: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ tensors:}
            \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{ValueError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"TensorDataset requires at least one Tensor"}\NormalTok{)}

        \CommentTok{\# Verify all tensors have identical length along dimension 0}
\NormalTok{        first\_len }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tensors[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{].shape[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ t }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ tensors:}
            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ t.shape[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{!=}\NormalTok{ first\_len:}
                \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{ValueError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"All tensors must have the same size along dimension 0"}\NormalTok{)}

        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.tensors }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tensors}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_len\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.tensors[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{].shape[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_getitem\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, index: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tuple[Tensor, ...]:}
        \CommentTok{\# Extract the index{-}th slice from each wrapped tensor}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \BuiltInTok{tuple}\NormalTok{(Tensor(t.data[index]) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ t }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.tensors)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The DataLoader Engine: Shuffling without Memory
Movement}\label{the-dataloader-engine-shuffling-without-memory-movement}

A naive data pipeline might attempt to shuffle a dataset by copying or
rearranging the underlying data arrays in RAM. For high-dimensional
tensors (such as thousands of images or token sequences), copying
megabytes or gigabytes of memory at the start of every training epoch
wastes memory bandwidth and CPU cycles.

\section{The Index Permutation
Invariant}\label{the-index-permutation-invariant}

Instead of physically rearranging multi-megabyte data buffers in memory,
the framework generates a lightweight 1D array of integer indices:

\[\mathbf{p} = \text{Permutation}([0, 1, 2, \dots, N-1])\]

Shuffling an array of 50,000 integer pointers consumes less than
\(400\text{ KB}\) of memory and executes in sub-millisecond time. The
DataLoader then retrieves mini-batch slices using these permuted indices
without duplicating or moving the underlying storage buffers.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/05_dataloader-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-dataloader-pipeline}\textbf{Figure 5.1: The
DataLoader Shuffling and Batching Pipeline.} Shuffling generates a
lightweight integer index permutation. The engine slices index chunks of
size \(B\), gathers sample tensors, and collates them into unified batch
tensors.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Building the Iterable
DataLoader}\label{building-the-iterable-dataloader}

The \texttt{DataLoader} combines a \texttt{Dataset} with a batching
engine, implementing the Python iterator protocol (\texttt{\_\_iter\_\_}
and \texttt{\_\_next\_\_}):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 5.3: The Complete DataLoader Implementation in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Iterator, Tuple}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ DataLoader:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Data loader providing mini{-}batching, shuffling, and lazy iteration.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        dataset: Dataset,}
\NormalTok{        batch\_size: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        shuffle: }\BuiltInTok{bool} \OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        drop\_last: }\BuiltInTok{bool} \OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{False}
\NormalTok{    ) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ batch\_size }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{ValueError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"batch\_size must be a positive integer"}\NormalTok{)}

        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.dataset }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ dataset}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.batch\_size }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ batch\_size}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.shuffle }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ shuffle}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.drop\_last }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ drop\_last}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_samples }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(dataset)}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_len\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Return the total number of batches per epoch."""}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.drop\_last:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_samples }\OperatorTok{//} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.batch\_size}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ (}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_samples }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.batch\_size }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{//} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.batch\_size}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_iter\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Iterator[Tuple[Tensor, ...]]:}
        \CommentTok{"""Yield mini{-}batches of collated tensors."""}
        \CommentTok{\# 1. Generate index list}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.shuffle:}
\NormalTok{            indices }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.random.permutation(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_samples)}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            indices }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.arange(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_samples)}

        \CommentTok{\# 2. Iterate in strides of batch\_size}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ start\_idx }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_samples, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.batch\_size):}
\NormalTok{            end\_idx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ start\_idx }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.batch\_size}

            \CommentTok{\# Handle fractional trailing batch}
            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ end\_idx }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_samples:}
                \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.drop\_last:}
                    \ControlFlowTok{break}
\NormalTok{                end\_idx }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_samples}

\NormalTok{            batch\_indices }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ indices[start\_idx:end\_idx]}

            \CommentTok{\# 3. Collate samples into unified batch tensors}
            \CommentTok{\# Retrieve sample tuples from dataset}
\NormalTok{            samples }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.dataset[i] }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ batch\_indices]}

            \CommentTok{\# Transpose list of tuples: [(x0, y0), (x1, y1)] {-}\textgreater{} ([x0, x1], [y0, y1])}
\NormalTok{            num\_fields }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(samples[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{])}
\NormalTok{            collated\_batch }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
            \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ field\_idx }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(num\_fields):}
\NormalTok{                field\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.stack([s[field\_idx].data }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ s }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ samples], axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{                collated\_batch.append(Tensor(field\_data))}

            \ControlFlowTok{yield} \BuiltInTok{tuple}\NormalTok{(collated\_batch)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Pitfall: Memory Leaks from Non-Lazy
Collation}\label{pitfall-memory-leaks-from-non-lazy-collation}

Creating all batch tensors in memory before training begins duplicates
dataset memory. The DataLoader must act as a \textbf{lazy generator},
constructing and yielding one batch tensor at a time so that memory from
previous batches is freed as execution proceeds.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Forward Pipeline Integration: Full Dataset
Evaluation}\label{forward-pipeline-integration-full-dataset-evaluation}

We can now assemble the complete forward pipeline constructed across
Chapters 1 through 5:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Wrap synthetic dataset samples in \texttt{TensorDataset}.
\item
  Construct a \texttt{DataLoader} to iterate in batches of \(B = 32\).
\item
  Pass each batch through our Chapter 3 \texttt{Sequential} MLP.
\item
  Measure batch prediction error with Chapter 4
  \texttt{CrossEntropyLoss}.
\item
  Aggregate total epoch loss and compute classification accuracy.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 5.4: Full Dataset Forward Evaluation Loop}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Linear, Dropout, Sequential}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.activations }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ ReLU}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.losses }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ CrossEntropyLoss}

\CommentTok{\# 1. Create a synthetic dataset: 1,000 samples, 784 features, 10 classes}
\NormalTok{num\_samples }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1000}
\NormalTok{raw\_features }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.random.randn(num\_samples, }\DecValTok{784}\NormalTok{).astype(np.float32))}
\NormalTok{raw\_labels }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.random.randint(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, size}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(num\_samples,)))}

\NormalTok{dataset }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TensorDataset(raw\_features, raw\_labels)}
\NormalTok{dataloader }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ DataLoader(dataset, batch\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{32}\NormalTok{, shuffle}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# 2. Instantiate 3{-}layer MLP and loss function}
\NormalTok{model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Sequential(}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{784}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    Dropout(p}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{criterion }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ CrossEntropyLoss()}

\CommentTok{\# 3. Evaluate model in eval mode across the entire dataset}
\NormalTok{model.}\BuiltInTok{eval}\NormalTok{()}
\NormalTok{total\_loss }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0}
\NormalTok{correct\_predictions }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{total\_samples }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ batch\_x, batch\_y }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ dataloader:}
    \CommentTok{\# Forward pass through model}
\NormalTok{    logits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ model(batch\_x)}
    \CommentTok{\# Compute batch loss}
\NormalTok{    loss }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ criterion(logits, batch\_y)}

    \CommentTok{\# Track metrics}
\NormalTok{    batch\_size }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ batch\_x.shape[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    total\_loss }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ loss.data }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ batch\_size}

    \CommentTok{\# Calculate accuracy: argmax along class dimension}
\NormalTok{    predicted\_classes }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.argmax(logits.data, axis}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    correct\_predictions }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(predicted\_classes }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ batch\_y.data)}
\NormalTok{    total\_samples }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ batch\_size}

\NormalTok{average\_loss }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ total\_loss }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ total\_samples}
\NormalTok{accuracy }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (correct\_predictions }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ total\_samples) }\OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{100.0}

\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Dataset Evaluation Complete:"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"  Total Batches: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(dataloader)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"  Average Loss:  }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{average\_loss}\SpecialCharTok{:.4f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"  Accuracy:      }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{accuracy}\SpecialCharTok{:.2f\}}\SpecialStringTok{\%"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Systems Reality: Multi-Process I/O and
Prefetching}\label{systems-reality-multi-process-io-and-prefetching}

In production deep learning frameworks like PyTorch, reading gigabytes
of training data from disk or network storage cannot occur synchronously
on the main training thread. If the model must wait for disk reads and
sample transformations between every batch, GPU utilization drops to
near zero (an I/O bottleneck).

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Synchronous I/O (Main Thread Stalled):                     │
│  [ Load Batch 1 ] ──> [ GPU Compute ] ──> [ Load Batch 2 ]  │
│         ▲                                         ▲         │
│     GPU Idle                                  GPU Idle      │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Asynchronous Prefetching (num_workers > 0):                │
│  Worker 1: [ Load Batch 1 ] ──> [ Load Batch 3 ]            │
│  Worker 2: [ Load Batch 2 ] ──> [ Load Batch 4 ]            │
│  GPU:            [ Compute B1 ] ──> [ Compute B2 ]          │
│                  (Zero idle stall time!)                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

Production data loaders solve this through three synergistic hardware
mechanisms:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Multi-Process Worker Pools
  (\texttt{num\_workers\ \textgreater{}\ 0})}: Workers run in isolated
  OS processes, bypassing Python's Global Interpreter Lock (GIL) to
  parse, decode, and augment samples in parallel.
\item
  \textbf{POSIX Shared Memory (\texttt{/dev/shm})}: Tensors allocated by
  worker processes reside in shared memory segments, passing lightweight
  memory file descriptors to the main process without socket
  serialization overhead.\footnote{In Linux, \texttt{/dev/shm} is a
    virtual memory filesystem backed by RAM. Passing tensors through
    shared memory avoids the \(10\times\text{--}100\times\)
    serialization penalty of Python \texttt{pickle} over inter-process
    pipes.}
\item
  \textbf{Page-Locked (Pinned) Memory and DMA}: Host DRAM allocated via
  \texttt{cudaHostAlloc} (or \texttt{pin\_memory=True}) is locked
  against OS page swapping, enabling the GPU's Direct Memory Access
  (DMA) engine to transfer tensors across PCIe asynchronously while CUDA
  compute kernels execute in parallel.\footnote{Standard CPU allocations
    are paged (pageable memory). The GPU cannot read pageable memory
    directly via DMA because the OS may relocate pages.
    \texttt{pin\_memory=True} locks physical pages in host DRAM,
    allowing the PCIe DMA controller to transfer data directly to GPU
    VRAM at line-rate bandwidth (\(32\text{ GB/s}\) on PCIe Gen4 x16).}
\end{enumerate}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{PyTorch Systems Bridge: \texttt{torch.utils.data.DataLoader} and
\texttt{torch::DataLoader}}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

In PyTorch, data loading is orchestrated via C++ backends
(\texttt{torch/csrc/api/src/data/samplers/}):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ torch.utils.data }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ TensorDataset, DataLoader}

\NormalTok{dataset }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ TensorDataset(torch.randn(}\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{784}\NormalTok{), torch.randint(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, (}\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{,)))}
\CommentTok{\# multi{-}process prefetching with page{-}locked DMA staging}
\NormalTok{dataloader }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ DataLoader(}
\NormalTok{    dataset,}
\NormalTok{    batch\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{32}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    shuffle}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    num\_workers}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    pin\_memory}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{,      }\CommentTok{\# Page{-}lock memory for async PCIe transfer}
\NormalTok{    persistent\_workers}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}
\NormalTok{)}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ x, y }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ dataloader:}
    \CommentTok{\# non\_blocking=True overlaps host{-}to{-}device DMA copy with preceding CUDA kernels}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.cuda(non\_blocking}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    y }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ y.cuda(non\_blocking}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Under the hood, \texttt{torch.multiprocessing} uses shared memory
handles (\texttt{torch\_shm\_manager}) to transfer
\texttt{torch.UntypedStorage} pointers across UNIX domain sockets,
achieving zero-copy IPC between worker processes and the main training
runtime.\footnotemark{}

\end{tcolorbox}

\footnotetext{In \texttt{torch/csrc/autograd/python\_variable.cpp} and
\texttt{torch/csrc/cuda/Module.cpp}, PyTorch hooks tensor serialization
to write shared memory file descriptors (\texttt{shm\_open}) rather than
data payloads.}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-5}

In this chapter, we constructed the fifth foundational component of
TinyTorch: data loading infrastructure. We examined the physical systems
rationale for mini-batching, using the Roofline model to demonstrate how
grouping samples into batches shifts computation from memory-bound
vector operations (\(I \approx 0.5\text{ FLOP/Byte}\)) into
high-intensity GEMM matrix operations (\(I \ge 31\text{ FLOP/Byte}\))
that saturate hardware ALUs.

We built the \texttt{Dataset} abstraction to decouple storage retrieval
from downstream computation, implemented \texttt{TensorDataset} for
in-memory arrays, and constructed the \texttt{DataLoader} engine. We
proved why index permutation shuffling eliminates expensive memory
copying in RAM, and verified the complete forward pipeline by streaming
an entire dataset through our multi-layer network to calculate
epoch-wide loss and accuracy.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-5}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Arithmetic Intensity Boost}: Mini-batching (\(B \ge 32\))
  transforms memory-bandwidth-bound vector operations into compute-bound
  matrix multiplications (GEMM), reusing loaded weight parameters across
  all \(B\) inputs.
\item
  \textbf{Separation of Concerns}: The \texttt{Dataset} defines sample
  access (\texttt{\_\_len\_\_}, \texttt{\_\_getitem\_\_}), while the
  \texttt{DataLoader} orchestrates batching, shuffling, and memory
  collation.
\item
  \textbf{Zero-Copy Shuffling}: Shuffling lightweight integer index
  arrays \([0\dots N-1]\) achieves stochastic randomization without
  copying or rearranging heavy data tensors in memory.
\item
  \textbf{Lazy Iteration Protocol}: The DataLoader yields batches
  on-demand as a Python generator, keeping active memory footprint
  strictly bounded to the active batch size.
\item
  \textbf{Asynchronous Prefetching}: Production runtimes deploy
  multi-process background workers and page-locked (pinned) memory DMA
  transfers to load upcoming batches in parallel, eliminating GPU idle
  stalls.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Computing Gradients
Automatically}\label{chapter-connection-computing-gradients-automatically}

We now have data flowing through multi-layer networks and measuring
prediction error, but how does the framework know how to adjust weights
to decrease loss? In \textbf{Chapter 6 (Automatic Differentiation and
Graph Tracing)}, we build the crown jewel of TinyTorch: the dynamic
reverse-mode autograd engine, computational DAG tracing, and
Vector-Jacobian Products.

\chapter{Automatic Differentiation and Graph
Tracing}\label{automatic-differentiation-and-graph-tracing}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why is dynamic graph tracing the only scalable mechanism to
compute gradients through millions of parameters?}

Symbolic differentiation produces exponential formula bloat through
recursive algebraic expansion, while numerical finite differences
require \(O(P)\) separate forward evaluations for a model with \(P\)
parameters, making both approaches computationally impossible for modern
deep neural networks. Reverse-mode automatic differentiation solves this
fundamental scaling bottleneck by executing a single backward pass whose
computational cost scales in \(O(1)\) relative to parameter count. In a
production framework runtime, autograd is not an abstract calculus
solver; it is a \textbf{dynamic execution tape and directed acyclic
graph (DAG)} that records operations during the forward pass, caches
intermediate activation buffers in memory, and performs a reverse
topological traversal during \texttt{loss.backward()} to route
Vector-Jacobian Products (VJPs) into parameter \texttt{.grad} memory
buffers. In a framework runtime, autograd is where dynamic memory
management, graph traversal algorithms, and multivariable calculus unite
into an automated learning engine.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-6}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Contrast} symbolic, numerical, and automatic differentiation
  to evaluate why reverse-mode autograd is the only algorithm that
  scales to high-dimensional parameter spaces (\(O(1)\) backward pass).
\item
  \textbf{Construct} the \texttt{Function} base class and dynamic
  computation DAG that records operations and caches intermediate
  tensors (\texttt{saved\_tensors}) during forward execution.
\item
  \textbf{Implement} exact Vector-Jacobian Products (VJPs) for
  arithmetic (\texttt{Add}, \texttt{Mul}, \texttt{Matmul}), reductions
  (\texttt{Sum}, \texttt{Mean}), and non-linear activations
  (\texttt{ReLU}, \texttt{Sigmoid}, \texttt{Tanh}, \texttt{LogSoftmax}).
\item
  \textbf{Solve} the broadcasting gradient mismatch hazard by
  implementing the \texttt{\_reduce\_broadcast\_grad} reduction engine.
\item
  \textbf{Build} the \texttt{backward()} graph engine using post-order
  Depth-First Search (DFS) topological sorting with gradient
  accumulation across fan-out nodes.
\item
  \textbf{Verify} autograd correctness mathematically to \(10^{-6}\)
  numerical tolerance using two-sided finite-difference gradient
  checking (\texttt{gradcheck}).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Limits of Symbolic and Numerical
Differentiation}\label{the-limits-of-symbolic-and-numerical-differentiation}

To optimize a neural network, we must compute the gradient of the scalar
loss \(\mathcal{L}\) with respect to every trainable weight matrix
\(\mathbf{W}_l\):

\[\nabla_{\mathbf{W}_l} \mathcal{L} = \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{W}_l}\]

Historically, computational mathematics offered two primary approaches
to differentiation, both of which collapse when applied to deep learning
systems:

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1. Symbolic Differentiation (Computer Algebra Systems)     │
│  • Method: Recursively applies calculus rules to equations. │
│  • Fatal Flaw: "Expression Swell." Stacking 50 layers       │
│    expands mathematical formulas into billions of redundant │
│    terms, exhausting memory before evaluation can begin.    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  2. Numerical Differentiation (Finite Differences)          │
│  • Method: Perturbs parameters: (f(w + e) - f(w - e)) / 2e. │
│  • Fatal Flaw: Requires 2 forward passes per parameter. For │
│    a modest 100M-parameter model, ONE gradient step would   │
│    require 200,000,000 forward passes!                      │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  3. Reverse-Mode Automatic Differentiation (Autograd)       │
│  • Method: Traces numeric execution graph; propagates       │
│    Vector-Jacobian Products backward via Chain Rule.        │
│  • Scaling: Computes ALL gradients in EXACTLY ONE backward  │
│    pass (computational complexity ~ 2x forward pass).       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Why Symbolic Differentiation Collapses: Expression
Swell}\label{why-symbolic-differentiation-collapses-expression-swell}

Consider a composition of \(L\) mathematical operations:

\[y = f_L(f_{L-1}(\dots f_1(x)))\]

Applying the product rule and chain rule symbolically without caching
intermediate values generates an expression tree whose node count grows
exponentially:

\[\text{AST Nodes} = O(2^L)\]

For a standard 50-layer network, the symbolic derivative formula
contains over \(10^{15}\) terms. Attempting to parse or simplify this
algebraic graph exhausts memory before numerical evaluation can even
begin.

\subsection{The Mathematics of Reverse-Mode Autograd: Vector-Jacobian
Products}\label{the-mathematics-of-reverse-mode-autograd-vector-jacobian-products}

Reverse-mode autograd bypasses symbolic expansion by operating directly
on numerical values. Let a computation be defined as an ordered sequence
of intermediate variable tensors
\(\mathbf{v}_1, \mathbf{v}_2, \dots, \mathbf{v}_K\), where
\(\mathbf{v}_K = \mathcal{L} \in \mathbb{R}\) is the scalar objective.

We define the \textbf{adjoint} (the upstream sensitivity gradient) of
variable \(\mathbf{v}_i\) as:

\[\overline{\mathbf{v}}_i = \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{v}_i}\]

By the multivariate chain rule, the adjoint of \(\mathbf{v}_i\) is
computed by summing the contributions from all immediate successor nodes
\(\text{children}(i)\):

\[\overline{\mathbf{v}}_i = \sum_{j \in \text{children}(i)} \overline{\mathbf{v}}_j \frac{\partial \mathbf{v}_j}{\partial \mathbf{v}_i} = \sum_{j \in \text{children}(i)} \overline{\mathbf{v}}_j \mathbf{J}_{j,i}\]

where
\(\mathbf{J}_{j,i} = \frac{\partial \mathbf{v}_j}{\partial \mathbf{v}_i} \in \mathbb{R}^{\dim(\mathbf{v}_j) \times \dim(\mathbf{v}_i)}\)
is the Jacobian matrix.

\section{The Vector-Jacobian Product (VJP)
Invariant}\label{the-vector-jacobian-product-vjp-invariant}

In high-dimensional machine learning, materializing the full Jacobian
matrix \(\mathbf{J}_{j,i}\) in memory is impossible (e.g., for an
activation tensor of size \(32 \times 4096\), the Jacobian contains
\(1.7 \times 10^{10}\) elements, requiring \(68\text{ GB}\) of RAM for a
single layer).

Reverse-mode autograd \textbf{never materializes the Jacobian matrix}.
Instead, it computes the \textbf{Vector-Jacobian Product (VJP)}:

\[\text{VJP}(\overline{\mathbf{v}}_j) = \overline{\mathbf{v}}_j \mathbf{J}_{j,i}\]

evaluating the vector-matrix contraction directly as a localized
operation that scales linearly with input tensor size (\(O(D)\) memory).

\section{The Reverse-Mode Scaling
Advantage}\label{the-reverse-mode-scaling-advantage}

For a function \(f: \mathbb{R}^N \to \mathbb{R}^M\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Forward-Mode Autograd} computes one column of the Jacobian per
  pass: efficient when inputs \(N \ll M\) (few inputs, many outputs).
\item
  \textbf{Reverse-Mode Autograd} computes one row of the Jacobian per
  pass: efficient when outputs \(M \ll N\).
\end{itemize}

Because neural network training maps millions of parameters
(\(N \approx 10^6 - 10^{11}\)) to a single scalar loss (\(M = 1\)),
\textbf{Reverse-Mode Autograd computes the entire gradient vector in a
single backward pass}, making deep learning computationally viable.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Dynamic Computation DAG: Functions and
Context}\label{the-dynamic-computation-dag-functions-and-context}

In TinyTorch (as in PyTorch), the computation graph is \textbf{dynamic}
(Define-by-Run). The graph is not declared statically in advance;
instead, it is constructed dynamically in memory as operations execute
in Python.

\subsection{The Function Contract}\label{the-function-contract}

Every differentiable operation is represented by a \texttt{Function}
class that implements a two-phase contract:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Forward Pass (\texttt{\_\_init\_\_})}: Saves input tensors
  required for the backward pass (\texttt{saved\_tensors}).\footnote{Retaining
    \texttt{saved\_tensors} across all \(L\) layers during the forward
    pass requires \(O(L \cdot B \cdot D)\) GPU VRAM, reaching peak
    memory consumption at the loss node. Techniques like
    \textbf{Activation Checkpointing} (Chen et al., 2016) discard
    intermediate activations and recompute them on-demand during
    backpropagation, trading \(33\%\) extra compute FLOPs for an
    \(O(\sqrt{L})\) memory footprint.}
\item
  \textbf{Backward Pass (\texttt{apply})}: Receives the upstream
  incoming gradient (\texttt{grad\_output}) and returns the
  Vector-Jacobian Product for each input.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 6.1: The Function Base Class}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tuple, Any}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Function:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Base class for differentiable operations in TinyTorch.}

\CommentTok{    Encapsulates forward tensor caching and backward VJP evaluation.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{tensors: Any) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{\# Save input tensors required to compute backward gradients}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.saved\_tensors }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tensors}

    \KeywordTok{def} \BuiltInTok{apply}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, grad\_output: np.ndarray) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tuple[np.ndarray, ...]:}
        \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{        Compute Vector{-}Jacobian Products for all inputs.}

\CommentTok{        Args:}
\CommentTok{            grad\_output: Upstream gradient flowing backward (dL / dOutput)}
\CommentTok{        Returns:}
\CommentTok{            Tuple of gradient arrays corresponding to each input tensor}
\CommentTok{        """}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{NotImplementedError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Subclasses must implement apply()"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

When an operation executes on a \texttt{Tensor} that requires gradients
(\texttt{requires\_grad=True}), the resulting output \texttt{Tensor}
records a reference to the \texttt{Function} node via its internal
\texttt{\_ctx} attribute:

\[\text{Output Tensor } \mathbf{z} \xrightarrow{\quad \text{\_ctx} \quad} \text{Function Node } \mathcal{F} \xrightarrow{\quad \text{saved\_tensors} \quad} \text{Input Tensors } (\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{y})\]

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/06_autograd-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-autograd-dag}\textbf{Figure 6.1: Dynamic Computation
Graph and Gradient Flow.} Forward execution constructs the directed
graph from inputs to Loss. Backward execution propagates gradients in
reverse topological order, accumulating gradients at fan-out nodes.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Vector-Jacobian Products (VJPs) for Fundamental
Operations}\label{vector-jacobian-products-vjps-for-fundamental-operations}

Let us derive and implement the backward gradient rules for the core
operations in TinyTorch.

\subsection{1. Addition and Subtraction}\label{addition-and-subtraction}

For \(\mathbf{z} = \mathbf{x} + \mathbf{y}\), the local derivatives are
identity matrices:
\(\frac{\partial \mathbf{z}}{\partial \mathbf{x}} = \mathbf{I}\) and
\(\frac{\partial \mathbf{z}}{\partial \mathbf{y}} = \mathbf{I}\). By the
multivariate chain rule:

\[\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{x}} = \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{z}} \cdot \mathbf{I} = \text{grad\_output}, \quad \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{y}} = \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{z}} \cdot \mathbf{I} = \text{grad\_output}\]

Addition routes the upstream gradient equally to both inputs.

\subsection{2. Element-Wise
Multiplication}\label{element-wise-multiplication}

For \(\mathbf{z} = \mathbf{x} \odot \mathbf{y}\), the local derivatives
scale by the opposing operand:

\[\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{x}} = \text{grad\_output} \odot \mathbf{y}, \quad \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{y}} = \text{grad\_output} \odot \mathbf{x}\]

\subsection{3. Matrix Multiplication (GEMM VJP
Derivation)}\label{matrix-multiplication-gemm-vjp-derivation}

For a batched linear projection \(\mathbf{Z} = \mathbf{X} \mathbf{W}\)
where \(\mathbf{X} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times D_{\text{in}}}\) and
\(\mathbf{W} \in \mathbb{R}^{D_{\text{in}} \times D_{\text{out}}}\), let
\(\mathbf{G} = \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{Z}} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times D_{\text{out}}}\)
denote the incoming upstream gradient (\texttt{grad\_output}).

We derive the exact gradient for input entry \(X_{i,k}\) using the
scalar chain rule:

\[\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial X_{i,k}} = \sum_{j=1}^{D_{\text{out}}} \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial Z_{i,j}} \frac{\partial Z_{i,j}}{\partial X_{i,k}} = \sum_{j=1}^{D_{\text{out}}} G_{i,j} W_{k,j} = (\mathbf{G} \mathbf{W}^T)_{i,k}\]

In matrix form:

\[\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{X}} = \mathbf{G} \cdot \mathbf{W}^T \quad \left( (B \times D_{\text{out}}) \times (D_{\text{out}} \times D_{\text{in}}) \to (B \times D_{\text{in}}) \right)\]

Similarly, for parameter weight entry \(W_{k,j}\):

\[\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial W_{k,j}} = \sum_{i=1}^B \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial Z_{i,j}} \frac{\partial Z_{i,j}}{\partial W_{k,j}} = \sum_{i=1}^B X_{i,k} G_{i,j} = (\mathbf{X}^T \mathbf{G})_{k,j}\]

In matrix form:

\[\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{W}} = \mathbf{X}^T \cdot \mathbf{G} \quad \left( (D_{\text{in}} \times B) \times (B \times D_{\text{out}}) \to (D_{\text{in}} \times D_{\text{out}}) \right)\]

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Broadcasting Gradient Reduction
Hazard}\label{the-broadcasting-gradient-reduction-hazard}

When NumPy broadcasts a tensor during forward execution (e.g., adding a
1D bias \(\mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^{128}\) to a 2D batch
\(\mathbf{X} \in \mathbb{R}^{32 \times 128}\)), the output shape is
\((32, 128)\).

During the backward pass, the incoming \texttt{grad\_output} has shape
\((32, 128)\). However, the gradient
\(\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{b}}\) must strictly match
the original shape of \(\mathbf{b}\) (\((128,)\)).

\section{The Broadcasting Gradient
Invariant}\label{the-broadcasting-gradient-invariant}

When a tensor dimension is broadcast across multiple elements, it
contributes to the loss through multiple computational paths. By the
chain rule, the total gradient with respect to that broadcast tensor is
the \textbf{sum of gradients across all broadcast dimensions}:

\[\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial b_j} = \sum_{i=1}^B \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial z_{i, j}}\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 6.2: Broadcast Gradient Shape Reduction Helper}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ \_reduce\_broadcast\_grad(grad: np.ndarray, original\_shape: Tuple[}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, ...]) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ np.ndarray:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Sum gradients over broadcast dimensions to match original tensor shape.}
\CommentTok{    """}
    \CommentTok{\# 1. Sum over leading batch dimensions that were prepended during broadcasting}
    \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ grad.ndim }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(original\_shape):}
\NormalTok{        grad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ grad.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}

    \CommentTok{\# 2. Sum over singleton dimensions where original shape was 1}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(original\_shape)):}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ original\_shape[i] }\OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{1} \KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ grad.shape[i] }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            grad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ grad.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(axis}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{i, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ grad}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Implementing Operation Functions in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-operation-functions-in-tinytorch}

We now implement the core \texttt{Function} classes in TinyTorch,
encapsulating both forward storage and backward VJPs:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 6.3: Core Autograd Operation Functions}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ AddBackward(Function):}
    \CommentTok{"""Addition VJP: dL/dx = grad, dL/dy = grad."""}
    \KeywordTok{def} \BuiltInTok{apply}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, grad\_output: np.ndarray) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:}
\NormalTok{        x, y }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.saved\_tensors}
\NormalTok{        gx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \_reduce\_broadcast\_grad(grad\_output, x.shape)}
\NormalTok{        gy }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \_reduce\_broadcast\_grad(grad\_output, y.shape)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ gx, gy}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ MulBackward(Function):}
    \CommentTok{"""Multiplication VJP: dL/dx = grad * y, dL/dy = grad * x."""}
    \KeywordTok{def} \BuiltInTok{apply}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, grad\_output: np.ndarray) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:}
\NormalTok{        x, y }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.saved\_tensors}
\NormalTok{        gx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \_reduce\_broadcast\_grad(grad\_output }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ y.data, x.shape)}
\NormalTok{        gy }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \_reduce\_broadcast\_grad(grad\_output }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ x.data, y.shape)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ gx, gy}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ MatmulBackward(Function):}
    \CommentTok{"""Matrix Multiplication VJP: dL/dA = grad @ B.T, dL/dB = A.T @ grad."""}
    \KeywordTok{def} \BuiltInTok{apply}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, grad\_output: np.ndarray) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:}
\NormalTok{        a, b }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.saved\_tensors}
\NormalTok{        ga }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.matmul(grad\_output, b.data.T)}
\NormalTok{        gb }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.matmul(a.data.T, grad\_output)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ ga, gb}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ ReLUBackward(Function):}
    \CommentTok{"""ReLU VJP: dL/dx = grad * (x \textgreater{} 0)."""}
    \KeywordTok{def} \BuiltInTok{apply}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, grad\_output: np.ndarray) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tuple[np.ndarray]:}
\NormalTok{        (x,) }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.saved\_tensors}
\NormalTok{        mask }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (x.data }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{).astype(np.float32)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ (grad\_output }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ mask,)}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ SigmoidBackward(Function):}
    \CommentTok{"""Sigmoid VJP: dL/dx = grad * s * (1 {-} s)."""}
    \KeywordTok{def} \BuiltInTok{apply}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, grad\_output: np.ndarray) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tuple[np.ndarray]:}
\NormalTok{        (output\_tensor,) }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.saved\_tensors}
\NormalTok{        s }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ output\_tensor.data}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ (grad\_output }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ s }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ s),)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Graph Traversal: Topological Sort and the
\texttt{backward()}
Engine}{Graph Traversal: Topological Sort and the backward() Engine}}\label{graph-traversal-topological-sort-and-the-backward-engine}

When a user calls \texttt{loss.backward()}, the autograd engine must
execute the backward passes in reverse dependency order.

\subsection{The Fan-Out Hazard and Gradient
Accumulation}\label{the-fan-out-hazard-and-gradient-accumulation}

If a tensor \(\mathbf{x}\) is used in multiple downstream operations (a
fan-out node), it receives independent gradient contributions from each
branch:

\[\mathbf{x} \longrightarrow \mathbf{z}_1 \longrightarrow \mathcal{L}\]
\[\mathbf{x} \longrightarrow \mathbf{z}_2 \longrightarrow \mathcal{L}\]

By the multivariable chain rule, the total gradient is the sum of both
paths:

\[\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{x}} = \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{z}_1} \frac{\partial \mathbf{z}_1}{\partial \mathbf{x}} + \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{z}_2} \frac{\partial \mathbf{z}_2}{\partial \mathbf{x}}\]

Therefore, when propagating gradients backward, the engine must
\textbf{accumulate gradients} using addition
(\texttt{tensor.grad.data\ +=\ incoming\_grad}) rather than overwriting.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 6.4: The Complete backward() Engine with Topological Sort}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ backward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{: Tensor, gradient: Optional[Tensor] }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Execute reverse{-}mode automatic differentiation from this tensor.}
\CommentTok{    """}
    \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.requires\_grad:}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{RuntimeError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Cannot call backward() on a tensor with requires\_grad=False"}\NormalTok{)}

    \CommentTok{\# 1. Initialize root gradient (dL / dL = 1.0)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ gradient }\KeywordTok{is} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.size }\OperatorTok{!=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{RuntimeError}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Grad can only be created implicitly for scalar outputs"}\NormalTok{)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.grad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.ones\_like(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.data, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32))}
    \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.grad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ gradient}

    \CommentTok{\# 2. Build topological ordering of the DAG using post{-}order DFS}
\NormalTok{    topo\_order: List[Tensor] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
\NormalTok{    visited }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{set}\NormalTok{()}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ build\_topo(t: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ t }\KeywordTok{not} \KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ visited:}
\NormalTok{            visited.add(t)}
            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ t.\_ctx }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
                \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ parent }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ t.\_ctx.saved\_tensors:}
                    \ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{isinstance}\NormalTok{(parent, Tensor) }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ parent.requires\_grad:}
\NormalTok{                        build\_topo(parent)}
\NormalTok{            topo\_order.append(t)}

\NormalTok{    build\_topo(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{)}

    \CommentTok{\# 3. Traverse nodes in reverse topological order}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ node }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{reversed}\NormalTok{(topo\_order):}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ node.\_ctx }\KeywordTok{is} \VariableTok{None} \KeywordTok{or}\NormalTok{ node.grad }\KeywordTok{is} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{continue}

        \CommentTok{\# Evaluate VJP}
\NormalTok{        grads }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ node.\_ctx.}\BuiltInTok{apply}\NormalTok{(node.grad.data)}

        \CommentTok{\# Route gradients to parents}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ parent, g }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{zip}\NormalTok{(node.\_ctx.saved\_tensors, grads):}
            \ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{isinstance}\NormalTok{(parent, Tensor) }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ parent.requires\_grad:}
                \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ parent.grad }\KeywordTok{is} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{                    parent.grad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.array(g, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32))}
                \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
                    \CommentTok{\# Accumulate gradients for fan{-}out nodes}
\NormalTok{                    parent.grad.data }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ g}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Numerical Verification: Finite-Difference Gradient
Checking}\label{numerical-verification-finite-difference-gradient-checking}

How do we mathematically prove that our autograd engine and VJPs have
zero bugs? We implement \textbf{Gradient Checking (\texttt{gradcheck})}.

For any parameter scalar \(w\), we evaluate the symmetric finite
difference quotient:

\[\frac{\partial f}{\partial w} \approx \frac{f(w + \epsilon) - f(w - \epsilon)}{2\epsilon}\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 6.5: Mathematical Finite{-}Difference Gradient Verification}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ gradcheck(func, inputs: Tuple[Tensor, ...], eps: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{, tol: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1e{-}4}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{bool}\NormalTok{:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Verify analytical autograd gradients against two{-}sided numerical finite differences.}
\CommentTok{    """}
    \CommentTok{\# 1. Run forward and backward to compute analytical autograd gradients}
\NormalTok{    out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ func(}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{inputs)}
\NormalTok{    out.backward()}

    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ tensor }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ inputs:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ tensor.requires\_grad:}
            \ControlFlowTok{continue}

\NormalTok{        analytical\_grad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tensor.grad.data.copy()}
\NormalTok{        numerical\_grad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.zeros\_like(tensor.data)}

        \CommentTok{\# 2. Compute numerical gradient element{-}by{-}element}
\NormalTok{        it }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.nditer(tensor.data, flags}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}multi\_index\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{])}
        \ControlFlowTok{while} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ it.finished:}
\NormalTok{            idx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ it.multi\_index}
\NormalTok{            orig\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tensor.data[idx]}

            \CommentTok{\# f(w + eps)}
\NormalTok{            tensor.data[idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ orig\_val }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps}
\NormalTok{            out\_pos }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ func(}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{inputs).data.copy()}

            \CommentTok{\# f(w {-} eps)}
\NormalTok{            tensor.data[idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ orig\_val }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ eps}
\NormalTok{            out\_neg }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ func(}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{inputs).data.copy()}

            \CommentTok{\# Restore original value}
\NormalTok{            tensor.data[idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ orig\_val}

            \CommentTok{\# Symmetric difference quotient}
\NormalTok{            numerical\_grad[idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{((out\_pos }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ out\_neg) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{2.0} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ eps))}
\NormalTok{            it.iternext()}

        \CommentTok{\# 3. Measure relative error: ||g\_ana {-} g\_num|| / (||g\_ana|| + ||g\_num|| + 1e{-}8)}
\NormalTok{        diff }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(analytical\_grad }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ numerical\_grad)}
\NormalTok{        denom }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.maximum(np.}\BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(analytical\_grad) }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(numerical\_grad), }\FloatTok{1e{-}8}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        rel\_error }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(diff }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ denom)}

        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ rel\_error }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ tol:}
            \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"FAIL: Max relative error }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{rel\_error}\SpecialCharTok{:.2e\}}\SpecialStringTok{ exceeds tolerance }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{tol}\SpecialCharTok{:.2e\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
            \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{False}

    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"PASS: Autograd gradients match numerical finite differences to 1e{-}5 precision!"}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{True}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{PyTorch Systems Bridge: \texttt{torch.autograd.Function} and
\texttt{grad\_fn}}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

In PyTorch, custom differentiable operations are created using the exact
same static method contract we implemented in TinyTorch:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ CustomSquare(torch.autograd.Function):}
    \AttributeTok{@staticmethod}
    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(ctx, x):}
\NormalTok{        ctx.save\_for\_backward(x)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{**} \DecValTok{2}

    \AttributeTok{@staticmethod}
    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ backward(ctx, grad\_output):}
\NormalTok{        x, }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ctx.saved\_tensors}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ grad\_output}

\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.tensor([}\FloatTok{3.0}\NormalTok{], requires\_grad}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{y }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ CustomSquare.}\BuiltInTok{apply}\NormalTok{(x)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Graph Node Pointer:"}\NormalTok{, y.grad\_fn)  }\CommentTok{\# \textless{}torch.autograd.function.CustomSquareBackward\textgreater{}}
\NormalTok{y.backward()}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Computed Gradient:"}\NormalTok{, x.grad)      }\CommentTok{\# tensor([6.])}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

PyTorch's \texttt{y.grad\_fn} is identical in function to TinyTorch's
\texttt{y.\_ctx}. Both point to the execution tape context that holds
saved tensors and the backward VJP method.\footnotemark{}

\end{tcolorbox}

\footnotetext{In the PyTorch C++ core
(\texttt{torch/csrc/autograd/engine.cpp}), the autograd engine allocates
a \texttt{GraphTask} queue and uses thread worker pools to evaluate
graph nodes in parallel across independent DAG branches.}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Production Reality: PyTorch
\texttt{autograd::Engine} and In-Place
Safety}{Production Reality: PyTorch autograd::Engine and In-Place Safety}}\label{production-reality-pytorch-autogradengine-and-in-place-safety}

In production PyTorch, the autograd DAG is not evaluated in Python; it
is executed entirely in native C++ by \texttt{torch::autograd::Engine}.

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PyTorch C++ Autograd Engine Architecture                   │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  • Edge: Represents a directed connection between Nodes.    │
│  • Node (torch::autograd::Node): Callable backward kernel.  │
│  • GraphTask: Execution context holding dependencies count. │
│  • ReadyQueue: Thread-safe queue of Nodes ready to execute. │
│  • Version Counter (_version): Tracks in-place mutations.   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

When \texttt{backward()} is triggered:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  An \texttt{Engine::execute()} call constructs a \texttt{GraphTask}
  tracking the in-degree dependencies of all nodes in the active DAG
  subgraph.
\item
  Root nodes with zero unresolved dependencies are pushed to a
  concurrent \texttt{ReadyQueue}.
\item
  Worker CPU threads pop \texttt{Node} instances from the
  \texttt{ReadyQueue}, execute their compiled C++/CUDA VJP kernels
  (\texttt{Node::operator()}), and decrement the dependency counters of
  predecessor nodes.
\item
  As predecessors reach zero remaining dependencies, they are enqueued
  into the \texttt{ReadyQueue}, automatically overlapping execution
  across parallel graph branches.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Pitfall: The In-Place Mutation Trap and Version
Tracking}\label{pitfall-the-in-place-mutation-trap-and-version-tracking}

If a tensor \(\mathbf{x}\) is cached during the forward pass by
\texttt{save\_for\_backward()}, and user code subsequently mutates
\(\mathbf{x}\) in-place before \texttt{backward()} executes
(\texttt{x\ +=\ 1} or \texttt{x{[}0{]}\ =\ 5.0}), the backward pass will
evaluate incorrect mathematical gradients using corrupted values.

PyTorch protects against silent mathematical corruption by attaching an
atomic \texttt{\_version} counter to every \texttt{TensorImpl} storage
object:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Every in-place operation increments \texttt{tensor.\_version} by 1.
\item
  \texttt{save\_for\_backward()} records the tensor's version snapshot
  \(V_0\).
\item
  When the backward kernel runs, the engine verifies that
  \texttt{tensor.\_version\ ==\ V\_0}.
\item
  If a mismatch is detected, PyTorch immediately halts execution with a
  \texttt{RuntimeError:\ one\ of\ the\ variables\ needed\ for\ gradient\ computation\ has\ been\ modified\ by\ an\ inplace\ operation}.\footnote{In
    \texttt{c10/core/TensorImpl.h}, \texttt{version\_counter\_} is an
    atomic integer incremented by every mutating view or kernel
    (\texttt{bump\_version()}).}
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-6}

In this chapter, we constructed the crown jewel of TinyTorch: the
dynamic reverse-mode automatic differentiation engine. We examined why
symbolic and numerical differentiation fail to scale to modern neural
networks, and proved why reverse-mode autograd computes all parameter
gradients in a single \(O(1)\) backward traversal via Vector-Jacobian
Products.

We built the \texttt{Function} abstraction to encapsulate forward tensor
caching and backward Vector-Jacobian Products across arithmetic, matrix
multiplications, and activations. We solved the broadcasting gradient
reduction hazard using \texttt{\_reduce\_broadcast\_grad}, implemented
the complete \texttt{backward()} engine using post-order DFS topological
sorting with gradient accumulation, and mathematically verified
analytical gradient correctness against finite-difference numerical
checks.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-6}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Reverse-Mode Scaling}: Reverse-mode automatic differentiation
  computes gradients for millions of parameters in a single backward
  pass (\(O(1)\) scaling w.r.t. parameter count).
\item
  \textbf{VJP Contraction}: Autograd never materializes dense Jacobian
  matrices, evaluating localized Vector-Jacobian Products that scale
  linearly with tensor dimensionality.
\item
  \textbf{Dynamic Tape Construction}: The computational DAG is
  constructed dynamically at runtime by linking output tensors to
  \texttt{Function} context objects via \texttt{\_ctx}.
\item
  \textbf{Broadcast Gradient Summation}: When an operation broadcasts
  inputs across dimensions, the backward pass must sum upstream
  gradients across those dimensions to match the input's original shape.
\item
  \textbf{Topological Traversal}: The backward engine executes nodes in
  reverse topological order, accumulating incoming gradients
  (\texttt{grad\ +=\ ...}) to correctly handle multi-branch fan-out
  nodes.
\item
  \textbf{Mathematical Verification}: Gradient checking
  (\texttt{gradcheck}) with two-sided finite differences is the gold
  standard for validating autograd correctness to \(10^{-6}\) numerical
  tolerance.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Closing the Training
Loop}\label{chapter-connection-closing-the-training-loop}

Now that our framework can compute exact gradients automatically, how do
we use these gradients to update parameters and train models? In
\textbf{Chapter 7 (Parameter Optimization and Momentum)}, we build
optimizers (\texttt{SGD}, \texttt{Momentum}, \texttt{AdamW}), analyze
the 16-byte memory footprint rule, and conquer ill-conditioned loss
landscapes.

\chapter{Parameter Optimization and
Momentum}\label{parameter-optimization-and-momentum}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does naive stochastic gradient descent oscillate erratically
in high-dimensional loss landscapes, and how do momentum and adaptive
learning rates conquer non-convex optimization?}

In Chapter 6, we implemented automatic differentiation, providing every
parameter tensor with an exact mathematical gradient
\(\nabla_\theta \mathcal{L}\). However, having gradients is useless
without an optimization algorithm to steer parameter updates. Naive
Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) treats every dimension uniformly,
causing parameter updates to oscillate violently across steep ravines
while crawling along flat plateaus. \textbf{Optimizers} are the
dynamical control systems of deep learning: they introduce physical
momentum to dampen high-frequency oscillations, escape saddle points,
and maintain per-parameter adaptive learning rates that normalize update
steps across heterogeneous gradient scales. In a framework runtime,
optimizers maintain stateful history buffers that consume substantial
GPU memory, directly determining training throughput and stability.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-7}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Formulate} the parameter update invariant across Stochastic
  Gradient Descent (SGD), Momentum, Adam, and AdamW.
\item
  \textbf{Analyze} the physical mechanics of momentum as a second-order
  dynamical system that dampens high-frequency gradient variance and
  rolls past zero-gradient saddle points.
\item
  \textbf{Derive} Adam's first and second raw moment expectations and
  prove the mathematical necessity of time-step bias corrections
  (\(\hat{\mathbf{m}}_t, \hat{\mathbf{v}}_t\)).
\item
  \textbf{Prove} why \(L_2\) regularization pollutes the second moment
  variance estimate in adaptive optimizers, and implement decoupled
  weight decay (\texttt{AdamW}) to restore uniform parameter shrinkage.
\item
  \textbf{Calculate} the exact hardware memory allocation footprint of
  optimizer state buffers (\(16\text{ bytes per parameter}\) in FP32 /
  AMP AdamW).
\item
  \textbf{Bridge} TinyTorch optimizers to production PyTorch
  \texttt{torch.optim.AdamW} with fused and multi-tensor CUDA kernels.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Fundamental Parameter Update
Invariant}\label{the-fundamental-parameter-update-invariant}

Every optimizer in deep learning implements an iterative stateful update
rule over parameter weights \(\mathbf{\theta}_t \in \mathbb{R}^D\) given
loss gradients
\(\mathbf{g}_t = \nabla_\theta \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{\theta}_t)\):

\[\mathbf{\theta}_{t+1} = \mathbf{\theta}_t - \eta \cdot \mathbf{u}(\mathbf{g}_1, \dots, \mathbf{g}_t)\]

where \(\eta > 0\) is the learning rate and \(\mathbf{u}(\cdot)\) is the
transformed update vector.

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  The Optimizer Hierarchy in Framework Architecture          │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. Vanilla SGD : u_t = g_t (Memory: 0 extra bytes)         │
│  2. SGD Momentum: u_t = v_t (Memory: 4 bytes/param)         │
│  3. AdamW       : u_t = m_t / sqrt(v_t) + lambda * theta    │
│                   (Memory: 8 extra bytes/param)             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Physics of Momentum and Ill-Conditioned
Surfaces}\label{the-physics-of-momentum-and-ill-conditioned-surfaces}

In high-dimensional deep learning loss surfaces, the local curvature is
characterized by the Hessian matrix
\(\mathbf{H} = \nabla^2 \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{\theta})\). The condition
number \(\kappa = \frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}\) measures the
ratio of maximum to minimum curvature eigenvalues.

When \(\kappa \gg 1\) (an ill-conditioned ravine):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The gradient along the steep perpendicular direction
  (\(\mathbf{g}_\perp\)) is massive, causing vanilla SGD to oscillate
  violently between ravine walls.
\item
  The gradient along the flat parallel valley floor
  (\(\mathbf{g}_\parallel\)) is tiny, causing progress toward the
  minimum to stall.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/07_optimizers-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-optimizers-momentum}\textbf{Figure 7.1: Trajectory
of SGD vs.~Momentum in an Ill-Conditioned Ravine.} Vanilla SGD
oscillates wildly across steep contours. Momentum accumulates forward
velocity along the low-curvature valley, rapidly accelerating
convergence.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Heavy-Ball Dynamics: Escaping Saddle
Points}\label{heavy-ball-dynamics-escaping-saddle-points}

Polyak Momentum (1964) models optimization as a physical heavy ball with
mass rolling down a potential energy surface with friction coefficient
\(\gamma\):

\[\ddot{\mathbf{\theta}}(t) + \gamma \dot{\mathbf{\theta}}(t) + \nabla \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{\theta}(t)) = \mathbf{0}\]

Discretizing this second-order ordinary differential equation yields the
velocity accumulator update:

\[\mathbf{v}_t = \beta \mathbf{v}_{t-1} + (1 - \beta) \mathbf{g}_t, \quad \mathbf{\theta}_{t+1} = \mathbf{\theta}_t - \eta \mathbf{v}_t\]

where \(\beta \in [0, 1)\) is the momentum decay factor (typically
\(\beta = 0.9\)).

\textbf{Two Critical Physical Properties}:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{High-Frequency Damping}: Successive gradient vectors along
  oscillating directions alternate signs
  (\(\mathbf{g}_{\perp, t} \approx -\mathbf{g}_{\perp, t-1}\)),
  canceling out inside the accumulator
  (\(\sum \mathbf{g}_\perp \to \mathbf{0}\)).
\item
  \textbf{Saddle Point Escape}: At strict saddle points or flat plateaus
  where local gradient \(\nabla \mathcal{L} \approx \mathbf{0}\),
  standard SGD becomes trapped. Momentum retains stored kinetic velocity
  (\(\mathbf{v}_t = \beta \mathbf{v}_{t-1} \ne \mathbf{0}\)), rolling
  the parameter trajectory through flat regions without stopping.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Adam and AdamW: Adaptive Moment
Estimation}\label{adam-and-adamw-adaptive-moment-estimation}

\textbf{Adam (Kingma \& Ba, 2014)} combines momentum with per-parameter
adaptive learning rates by tracking both the exponentially decaying
first raw moment (mean velocity \(\mathbf{m}_t\)) and second uncentered
moment (variance \(\mathbf{v}_t\)):

\[\mathbf{m}_t = \beta_1 \mathbf{m}_{t-1} + (1 - \beta_1) \mathbf{g}_t, \quad \mathbf{v}_t = \beta_2 \mathbf{v}_{t-1} + (1 - \beta_2) \mathbf{g}_t^2\]

\subsection{Derivation of Initialization Bias
Correction}\label{derivation-of-initialization-bias-correction}

Because moment accumulators are initialized to zero
(\(\mathbf{m}_0 = \mathbf{0}, \mathbf{v}_0 = \mathbf{0}\)), early
estimates are heavily biased toward zero.

Unrolling \(\mathbf{m}_t\) recursively as a linear combination of past
gradients:

\[\mathbf{m}_t = (1 - \beta_1) \sum_{i=1}^t \beta_1^{t-i} \mathbf{g}_i\]

Taking expectations under the assumption that the true gradient
distribution
\(\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{g}_i] \approx \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{g}_t]\) is locally
stationary:

\[\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{m}_t] = \mathbb{E}\left[ (1 - \beta_1) \sum_{i=1}^t \beta_1^{t-i} \mathbf{g}_i \right] = \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{g}_t] (1 - \beta_1) \sum_{i=1}^t \beta_1^{t-i} = \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{g}_t] (1 - \beta_1^t)\]

To obtain an unbiased estimator
\(\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{m}}_t] = \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{g}_t]\), we must
rescale by \(\frac{1}{1 - \beta_1^t}\):

\[\hat{\mathbf{m}}_t = \frac{\mathbf{m}_t}{1 - \beta_1^t}, \quad \hat{\mathbf{v}}_t = \frac{\mathbf{v}_t}{1 - \beta_2^t}\]

As \(t \to \infty\), the correction factor \(\beta^t \to 0\), naturally
vanishing as training progresses.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The \(L_2\) Regularization vs.~Decoupled
Weight Decay
Proof}{The L\_2 Regularization vs.~Decoupled Weight Decay Proof}}\label{the-l_2-regularization-vs.-decoupled-weight-decay-proof}

In standard stochastic gradient descent, \(L_2\) regularization
(\(\mathcal{L}_{\text{total}} = \mathcal{L} + \frac{\lambda}{2} \|\mathbf{\theta}\|^2\))
is mathematically equivalent to weight decay:

\[\nabla_\theta \mathcal{L}_{\text{total}} = \mathbf{g}_t + \lambda \mathbf{\theta}_t \implies \mathbf{\theta}_{t+1} = \mathbf{\theta}_t - \eta (\mathbf{g}_t + \lambda \mathbf{\theta}_t) = (1 - \eta \lambda)\mathbf{\theta}_t - \eta \mathbf{g}_t\]

In adaptive optimizers (Adam), this equivalence completely breaks down.

If \(L_2\) regularization is applied by adding
\(\lambda \mathbf{\theta}_t\) directly to the gradient \(\mathbf{g}_t\),
the polluted gradient
\(\mathbf{g}_t' = \mathbf{g}_t + \lambda \mathbf{\theta}_t\) enters the
second raw moment accumulator:

\[\mathbf{v}_t = \beta_2 \mathbf{v}_{t-1} + (1 - \beta_2) (\mathbf{g}_t + \lambda \mathbf{\theta}_t)^2\]

\section{The Variance Pollution
Hazard}\label{the-variance-pollution-hazard}

When a parameter \(\theta_i\) has a large magnitude, the regularization
term \((\lambda \theta_i)^2\) artificially inflates \(v_{t,i}\). The
resulting parameter update becomes:

\[\Delta \theta_i = -\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\hat{v}_{t,i}} + \epsilon} (\hat{m}_{t,i} + \lambda \theta_i) \approx -\frac{\eta}{\lambda |\theta_i|} (\lambda \theta_i) = -\eta \cdot \text{sign}(\theta_i)\]

Parameters with \textbf{large magnitudes} have their effective
regularization decay divided by \(\sqrt{\hat{v}_{t,i}}\), receiving
\emph{smaller} relative shrinkage than parameters with small
magnitudes---the exact opposite of the intended regularizer!

\textbf{AdamW (Loshchilov \& Hutter, 2017)} decouples weight decay from
gradient moment accumulation, applying parameter shrinkage directly to
the weight:

\[\mathbf{\theta}_{t+1} = \mathbf{\theta}_t - \eta \left( \frac{\hat{\mathbf{m}}_t}{\sqrt{\hat{\mathbf{v}}_t} + \epsilon} + \lambda \mathbf{\theta}_t \right)\]

This restores uniform, scale-invariant geometric decay across all model
parameters.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Implementing Optimizers in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-optimizers-in-tinytorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 7.1: The Optimizer Base Class and AdamW in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ List}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Optimizer:}
    \CommentTok{"""Base class for all TinyTorch optimizers."""}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, params: List[Tensor], lr: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1e{-}3}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.params }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ params}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.lr }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ lr}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ zero\_grad(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Reset gradients of all parameters to zero."""}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ p }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.params:}
\NormalTok{            p.zero\_grad()}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ step(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{raise} \PreprocessorTok{NotImplementedError}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ AdamW(Optimizer):}
    \CommentTok{"""AdamW optimizer with decoupled weight decay."""}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        params: List[Tensor],}
\NormalTok{        lr: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1e{-}3}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        betas: }\BuiltInTok{tuple} \OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{0.9}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.999}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{        eps: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1e{-}8}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        weight\_decay: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1e{-}2}
\NormalTok{    ) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \BuiltInTok{super}\NormalTok{().}\FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(params, lr)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta1, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ betas}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ eps}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight\_decay }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ weight\_decay}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.t }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}

        \CommentTok{\# Optimizer state buffers (First \& Second moments per parameter)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.m }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [np.zeros\_like(p.data) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ p }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.params]}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.v }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [np.zeros\_like(p.data) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ p }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.params]}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ step(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.t }\OperatorTok{+=} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{        b1, b2 }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta1, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta2}

        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i, p }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{enumerate}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.params):}
            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ p.grad }\KeywordTok{is} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
                \ControlFlowTok{continue}

\NormalTok{            g }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ p.grad.data}

            \CommentTok{\# 1. Update biased 1st and 2nd moments}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.m[i] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ b1 }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.m[i] }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ b1) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ g}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.v[i] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ b2 }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.v[i] }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ b2) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (g }\OperatorTok{**} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}

            \CommentTok{\# 2. Bias corrections}
\NormalTok{            m\_hat }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.m[i] }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ (b1 }\OperatorTok{**} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.t))}
\NormalTok{            v\_hat }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.v[i] }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ (b2 }\OperatorTok{**} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.t))}

            \CommentTok{\# 3. Decoupled weight decay + adaptive gradient step}
\NormalTok{            step\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (m\_hat }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (np.sqrt(v\_hat) }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps)) }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight\_decay }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ p.data)}
\NormalTok{            p.data }\OperatorTok{{-}=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.lr }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ step\_val}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Optimizer Memory Footprint
Accounting}\label{optimizer-memory-footprint-accounting}

In large-scale training systems, optimizer state buffers are often the
single largest consumer of GPU VRAM, dwarfing the model parameters
themselves.

\subsection{The 16-Byte per Parameter
Law}\label{the-16-byte-per-parameter-law}

For a model with \(N\) parameters, let us calculate the exact memory
