required to store parameters, gradients, and optimizer states:

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1812}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3490}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1611}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1544}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1544}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Precision Regime
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Component
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Precision \& Type
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
Bytes / Param
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
7B Model Footprint
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{Pure FP32 Training} & Model Weights (\(\mathbf{\theta}\)) &
\texttt{float32} & \(4\text{ bytes}\) & \(28.0\text{ GB}\) \\
& Gradients (\(\mathbf{g}\)) & \texttt{float32} & \(4\text{ bytes}\) &
\(28.0\text{ GB}\) \\
& AdamW 1st Moment (\(\mathbf{m}\)) & \texttt{float32} &
\(4\text{ bytes}\) & \(28.0\text{ GB}\) \\
& AdamW 2nd Moment (\(\mathbf{v}\)) & \texttt{float32} &
\(4\text{ bytes}\) & \(28.0\text{ GB}\) \\
& \textbf{Total Static Footprint} & & \textbf{\(16\text{ bytes}\)} &
\textbf{\(112.0\text{ GB}\)} \\
\textbf{Mixed Precision (AMP)} & Model Weights (\(\mathbf{\theta}\)) &
\texttt{float16} / \texttt{bfloat16} & \(2\text{ bytes}\) &
\(14.0\text{ GB}\) \\
& Gradients (\(\mathbf{g}\)) & \texttt{float16} / \texttt{bfloat16} &
\(2\text{ bytes}\) & \(14.0\text{ GB}\) \\
& Master Weights (\(\mathbf{\theta}_{\text{master}}\)) &
\texttt{float32} & \(4\text{ bytes}\) & \(28.0\text{ GB}\) \\
& AdamW 1st Moment (\(\mathbf{m}\)) & \texttt{float32} &
\(4\text{ bytes}\) & \(28.0\text{ GB}\) \\
& AdamW 2nd Moment (\(\mathbf{v}\)) & \texttt{float32} &
\(4\text{ bytes}\) & \(28.0\text{ GB}\) \\
& \textbf{Total Static Footprint} & & \textbf{\(16\text{ bytes}\)} &
\textbf{\(112.0\text{ GB}\)} \\
\end{longtable}
}

\section{The 16-Byte Invariant of AdamW
Training}\label{the-16-byte-invariant-of-adamw-training}

A common systems misconception is that switching from FP32 to 16-bit
Mixed Precision (FP16/BF16) cuts total training memory in half.

In reality, because multiplying 16-bit gradients by small learning rates
(\(\eta = 10^{-4}\)) causes underflow below the dynamic range of FP16
(\(2^{-14} \approx 6 \times 10^{-5}\)), adaptive optimizers \textbf{must
maintain master weights and moment accumulators in full 32-bit float}.
Consequently, both FP32 and AMP AdamW training require exactly
\textbf{\(16\text{ bytes per parameter}\)} of static VRAM---excluding
all activation memory.\footnote{To overcome the 16-byte wall, ZeRO (Zero
  Redundancy Optimizer, Rajbhandari et al., 2020) shards states across
  \(N_{\text{GPUs}}\): \textbf{ZeRO-1} shards optimizer states
  (\(\frac{8}{N_{\text{GPUs}}}\) bytes/param), \textbf{ZeRO-2} shards
  optimizer states + gradients (\(\frac{10}{N_{\text{GPUs}}}\)
  bytes/param), and \textbf{ZeRO-3 / FSDP} shards all weights, grads,
  and states (\(\frac{16}{N_{\text{GPUs}}}\) bytes/param).}\footnote{\textbf{8-bit
  Adam} (Dettmers et al., 2021) dynamically quantizes the 1st and 2nd
  moment buffers into block-wise INT8 representations with dynamic scale
  factors, reducing optimizer state memory from \(8\text{ bytes/param}\)
  to \(2\text{ bytes/param}\) (a \(75\%\) reduction in optimizer VRAM).}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{\texorpdfstring{PyTorch Systems Bridge:
\texttt{torch.optim.AdamW} and Fused CUDA
Kernels}{PyTorch Systems Bridge: torch.optim.AdamW and Fused CUDA Kernels}}\label{pytorch-systems-bridge-torch.optim.adamw-and-fused-cuda-kernels}

In production PyTorch, optimizing millions of parameters across GPU
memory requires high-performance CUDA kernel dispatching:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}

\NormalTok{model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.nn.Linear(}\DecValTok{4096}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4096}\NormalTok{).cuda()}

\CommentTok{\# Fused AdamW launches a single vectorized CUDA kernel for all parameters}
\NormalTok{optimizer }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.optim.AdamW(}
\NormalTok{    model.parameters(),}
\NormalTok{    lr}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e{-}3}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    betas}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.9}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.999}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    weight\_decay}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e{-}2}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    fused}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}  \CommentTok{\# Coalesces weight, grad, m, and v memory transactions in SRAM}
\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{The Three Optimizer Dispatch Modes in
PyTorch}\label{the-three-optimizer-dispatch-modes-in-pytorch}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Standard Python Loop (\texttt{fused=False,\ foreach=False})}:
  Iterates through each parameter sequentially, launching \(4\) separate
  CUDA kernels per tensor (weight decay, moment updates, bias
  correction, parameter step). For a 500-layer model, this executes
  \(2,000\) tiny kernel launches per training step, severely bound by
  CPU-GPU launch latency.
\item
  \textbf{Multi-Tensor Foreach (\texttt{foreach=True})}: PyTorch groups
  parameters by data type and device, passing lists of tensor pointers
  into multi-tensor C++ kernels (\texttt{torch.\_foreach\_adamw\_}),
  slashing Python overhead.
\item
  \textbf{Fused CUDA Kernel (\texttt{fused=True})}: Launches a single
  monolithic CUDA kernel (\texttt{FusedAdamWKernel.cu}). For each
  parameter element, the GPU thread loads \(\theta, g, m, v\) into fast
  on-chip registers simultaneously, performs all moment updates, applies
  decoupled weight decay, and writes the updated \(\theta, m, v\) back
  to HBM in a single DRAM memory transaction.\footnote{In
    \texttt{aten/src/ATen/native/cuda/FusedAdamWKernel.cu}, fusing
    optimizer steps maximizes arithmetic intensity by eliminating
    redundant global DRAM roundtrips for intermediate moment
    calculations.}
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-7}

In this chapter, we explored parameter optimization from first
principles. We analyzed the physical dynamics of momentum as a
second-order heavy-ball system, proving how velocity accumulators
eliminate oscillations across ill-conditioned ravines (\(\kappa \gg 1\))
and carry trajectories across flat zero-gradient saddle points.

We derived Adam's moment expectations and bias corrections, proved why
standard \(L_2\) regularization pollutes the second moment variance
estimate in adaptive optimizers, and implemented decoupled weight decay
(\texttt{AdamW}) in pure Python. Finally, we established the rigorous
\(16\text{ bytes/parameter}\) memory law governing deep learning
training systems and examined fused CUDA kernel architectures.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-7}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The Update Invariant}: Optimizers transform raw gradients
  \(\mathbf{g}_t\) into smooth, variance-reduced parameter updates
  \(\mathbf{\theta}_{t+1} = \mathbf{\theta}_t - \eta \mathbf{u}_t\).
\item
  \textbf{Momentum Damping \& Saddle Escape}: Second-order velocity
  accumulation cancels orthogonal high-frequency oscillations while
  kinetic energy carries parameters through zero-gradient saddle points.
\item
  \textbf{Initialization Bias Correction}: Rescaling raw moments by
  \(\frac{1}{1 - \beta^t}\) ensures unbiased moment estimates during
  early optimization steps.
\item
  \textbf{Decoupled Weight Decay}: AdamW applies weight decay directly
  to parameter weights rather than mixing it into the second moment
  accumulator, preventing variance pollution and restoring uniform
  shrinkage.
\item
  \textbf{The 16-Byte Law}: Storing weights, gradients, and 32-bit
  optimizer state buffers requires \(16\text{ bytes per parameter}\) in
  both FP32 and Mixed-Precision (AMP) training.
\item
  \textbf{Kernel Fusion}: \texttt{fused=True} AdamW executes
  multi-tensor updates in a single register pass, eliminating DRAM
  memory roundtrips and maximizing memory bandwidth utilization.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Closing the Training
Loop}\label{chapter-connection-closing-the-training-loop-1}

We now have parameter tensors (Ch 1), forward activations (Ch 2), layers
(Ch 3), losses (Ch 4), dataloaders (Ch 5), autograd gradients (Ch 6),
and optimizers (Ch 7). In \textbf{Chapter 8 (The Training Loop and
Trainer Architecture)}, we assemble these components into the unified
5-step training contract and train complete neural networks.

\chapter{The Training Loop and Trainer
Architecture}\label{the-training-loop-and-trainer-architecture}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-8}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{How do the disparate components of a machine learning framework
synchronize into a deterministic, robust execution engine?}

Across Chapters 1 through 7, we built every isolated subsystem of deep
learning: contiguous tensor memory, non-linear forward activations,
parameter layers, objective loss functions, batched dataloaders,
reverse-mode autograd graphs, and adaptive momentum optimizers. In this
chapter, we orchestrate these independent subsystems into the
\textbf{5-Step Training Loop Contract}. We engineer the \texttt{Trainer}
engine, implement gradient norm clipping to prevent numerical
explosions, build validation evaluation loops, and construct model
checkpoint serialization. By completing this chapter, we close the full
feedback loop of deep learning, transforming TinyTorch into a complete,
operational training runtime.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-8}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Formulate} the mathematically immutable 5-Step Training Loop
  Contract (\texttt{zero\_grad} \(\to\) \texttt{forward} \(\to\)
  \texttt{loss} \(\to\) \texttt{backward} \(\to\) \texttt{step}) and
  analyze the systems failure modes of step reordering.
\item
  \textbf{Derive} Global Gradient Norm Clipping, proving directional
  invariance
  (\(\frac{\mathbf{g}'}{\|\mathbf{g}'\|} = \frac{\mathbf{g}}{\|\mathbf{g}\|}\))
  while bounding maximum step length against numerical explosions.
\item
  \textbf{Construct} the unified \texttt{Trainer} engine in TinyTorch,
  managing epoch scheduling, validation tracking, metric logging, and
  checkpoint serialization.
\item
  \textbf{Examine} GPU VRAM allocation dynamics, memory pool
  fragmentation, and the PyTorch Caching Allocator
  (\texttt{c10::cuda::CUDACachingAllocator}).
\item
  \textbf{Bridge} TinyTorch's training pipeline to production
  distributed systems (\texttt{DistributedDataParallel}, NCCL
  Ring-AllReduce, and gradient bucketing).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The 5-Step Training Loop
Contract}\label{the-5-step-training-loop-contract}

Every deep learning framework executes training through a synchronized
5-step state transition:

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  THE 5-STEP TRAINING LOOP CONTRACT                          │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. optimizer.zero_grad() : Clear stale accumulated grads.  │
│  2. y_pred = model(x_batch): Forward pass + DAG record.     │
│  3. loss = loss_fn(y_pred, y): Scalar objective evaluation. │
│  4. loss.backward()       : Reverse-mode DFS backprop pass. │
│  5. optimizer.step()      : Apply parameter updates.        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/08_training-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-training-loop}\textbf{Figure 8.1: The 5-Step
Training Cycle.} State transitions through forward evaluation, loss
computation, autograd backward graph execution, and parameter
optimization.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Mathematical and Systems Rigidity of Step
Ordering}\label{mathematical-and-systems-rigidity-of-step-ordering}

The order of these five steps is not an arbitrary software convention;
it is a mathematically and computationally rigid sequence:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{optimizer.zero\_grad()}}: Must execute before backward
  accumulation. Because autograd uses accumulation
  (\texttt{tensor.grad\ +=\ g}) to correctly sum multi-branch DAG
  contributions, omitting \texttt{zero\_grad()} causes gradients to
  accumulate indefinitely across batches:

  \[\mathbf{g}_{\text{accumulated}} = \sum_{k=1}^K \mathbf{g}_k\]

  Across \(K\) batches without zeroing, the effective parameter step is
  scaled by \(K \times \eta\), causing immediate numerical divergence
  and NaN weights.
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{y\_pred\ =\ model(x\_batch)}}: Executes forward tensor
  operations, allocating intermediate activation buffers in memory and
  recording the execution DAG by attaching \texttt{\_ctx} function
  pointers.
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{loss\ =\ criterion(y\_pred,\ y\_batch)}}: Reduces
  multidimensional prediction errors into a single scalar root node
  (\(\mathcal{L} \in \mathbb{R}\)). The scalar property is mandatory: it
  provides the implicit seed derivative
  \(\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathcal{L}} = 1.0\) required
  to anchor reverse-mode DFS backpropagation.
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{loss.backward()}}: Traverses the recorded DAG in
  reverse topological order, evaluating Vector-Jacobian Products (VJPs)
  and writing computed numerical derivatives into each parameter's
  \texttt{.grad} tensor buffer.\footnote{In GPU training, dynamic tensor
    allocations during forward and backward passes create high memory
    churn. Calling the driver allocator (\texttt{cudaMalloc}) on every
    batch would trigger device-wide CPU-GPU synchronization barriers,
    degrading throughput. Production runtimes deploy \textbf{Caching
    Allocators} (\texttt{c10::cuda::CUDACachingAllocator}) that
    pre-allocate large memory segments and manage sub-allocations in
    user-space.}
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{optimizer.step()}}: Reads the populated \texttt{.grad}
  buffers to compute momentum vectors and mutate parameter
  \texttt{.data} buffers in-place. If executed before
  \texttt{backward()}, the optimizer updates parameters using stale
  gradients from the \emph{previous} batch, desynchronizing the update
  from the current data slice.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Gradient Norm Clipping: Preventing Exploding
Gradients}\label{gradient-norm-clipping-preventing-exploding-gradients}

In deep neural networks, recurrent architectures, and transformers, loss
surfaces frequently exhibit sharp, steep cliffs. When gradient
backpropagation traverses these high-curvature ravines, gradient
magnitudes can explode exponentially
(\(\|\mathbf{g}\| \to 10^4\text{--}10^8\)), causing catastrophic
parameter jumps that ruin trained representations.

\subsection{Mathematical Derivation of Global Norm
Clipping}\label{mathematical-derivation-of-global-norm-clipping}

\textbf{Gradient Clipping by Global Norm (Pascanu et al., 2013)} pools
all \(P\) parameter gradient tensors in the network into a single global
vector
\(\mathbf{g} = [\mathbf{g}_1, \mathbf{g}_2, \dots, \mathbf{g}_P]^T\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Compute the total Euclidean \(L_2\) norm across all parameters:

  \[\|\mathbf{g}\|_{\text{total}} = \sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^P \|\mathbf{g}_i\|_2^2} = \sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^P \sum_{j} (g_{i,j})^2}\]
\item
  Define the scaling coefficient \(\alpha\) given a maximum threshold
  \(M > 0\):

  \[\alpha = \frac{M}{\max(M, \|\mathbf{g}\|_{\text{total}})} = \min\left(1.0, \frac{M}{\|\mathbf{g}\|_{\text{total}}}\right)\]
\item
  Scale every parameter gradient tensor proportionally:

  \[\mathbf{g}_i \longleftarrow \alpha \cdot \mathbf{g}_i = \mathbf{g}_i \cdot \frac{M}{\max(M, \|\mathbf{g}\|_{\text{total}})}\]
\end{enumerate}

\section{The Directional Invariance
Property}\label{the-directional-invariance-property}

A critical mathematical advantage of global norm clipping over
coordinate-wise clipping (\(\text{clip}(g_j, -c, c)\)) is that it
strictly preserves the gradient direction vector:

\[\frac{\mathbf{g}_{\text{clipped}}}{\|\mathbf{g}_{\text{clipped}}\|_2} = \frac{\alpha \mathbf{g}}{\|\alpha \mathbf{g}\|_2} = \frac{\alpha \mathbf{g}}{\alpha \|\mathbf{g}\|_2} = \frac{\mathbf{g}}{\|\mathbf{g}\|_2}\]

The angular direction in \(D\)-dimensional parameter space is completely
undisturbed (\(0^\circ\) angular deviation), while the step length is
strictly bounded to \(\|\mathbf{g}_{\text{clipped}}\|_2 \le M\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 8.1: Global Gradient Norm Clipping in TinyTorch}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ clip\_grad\_norm(params: List[Tensor], max\_norm: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.0}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Clips gradients of an iterable of parameters by global Euclidean norm.}
\CommentTok{    """}
\NormalTok{    total\_norm\_sq }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ p }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ params:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ p.grad }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            total\_norm\_sq }\OperatorTok{+=} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(p.grad.data }\OperatorTok{**} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}

\NormalTok{    total\_norm }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.sqrt(total\_norm\_sq)}
\NormalTok{    clip\_coef }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ max\_norm }\OperatorTok{/} \BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(total\_norm, }\FloatTok{1e{-}6}\NormalTok{)}

    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ clip\_coef }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{1.0}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ p }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ params:}
            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ p.grad }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{                p.grad.data }\OperatorTok{*=}\NormalTok{ clip\_coef}

    \ControlFlowTok{return} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(total\_norm)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Implementing the Trainer Engine in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-the-trainer-engine-in-tinytorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 8.2: The Unified Trainer Engine in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Layer}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.losses }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Loss}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.optimizers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Optimizer}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.dataloader }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ DataLoader}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Dict, List, Any}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Trainer:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Standardized training engine coordinating models, losses, and optimizers.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        model: Layer,}
\NormalTok{        criterion: Loss,}
\NormalTok{        optimizer: Optimizer,}
\NormalTok{        max\_grad\_norm: Optional[}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.0}
\NormalTok{    ) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ model}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.criterion }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ criterion}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.optimizer }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ optimizer}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.max\_grad\_norm }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ max\_grad\_norm}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.history: Dict[}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{, List[}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{]] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}train\_loss\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: [], }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}val\_loss\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: []\}}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ train\_epoch(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, dataloader: DataLoader) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Execute one complete training epoch over dataloader."""}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.model.train()}
\NormalTok{        total\_loss }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0}
\NormalTok{        num\_batches }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}

        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ x\_batch, y\_batch }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ dataloader:}
            \CommentTok{\# Step 1: Zero gradients}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.optimizer.zero\_grad()}

            \CommentTok{\# Step 2: Forward pass}
\NormalTok{            y\_pred }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.model(x\_batch)}

            \CommentTok{\# Step 3: Compute loss}
\NormalTok{            loss }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.criterion(y\_pred, y\_batch)}

            \CommentTok{\# Step 4: Backward pass (DFS reverse{-}mode autograd)}
\NormalTok{            loss.backward()}

            \CommentTok{\# Optional: Gradient clipping}
            \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.max\_grad\_norm }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{                clip\_grad\_norm(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.model.parameters(), }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.max\_grad\_norm)}

            \CommentTok{\# Step 5: Optimizer parameter update}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.optimizer.step()}

\NormalTok{            total\_loss }\OperatorTok{+=} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(loss.data)}
\NormalTok{            num\_batches }\OperatorTok{+=} \DecValTok{1}

\NormalTok{        avg\_loss }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ total\_loss }\OperatorTok{/} \BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(num\_batches, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.history[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}train\_loss\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{].append(avg\_loss)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ avg\_loss}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ evaluate(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, dataloader: DataLoader) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Evaluate model on validation dataloader without updating parameters."""}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.model.}\BuiltInTok{eval}\NormalTok{()}
\NormalTok{        total\_loss }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0}
\NormalTok{        num\_batches }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}

        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ x\_batch, y\_batch }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ dataloader:}
\NormalTok{            y\_pred }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.model(x\_batch)}
\NormalTok{            loss }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.criterion(y\_pred, y\_batch)}
\NormalTok{            total\_loss }\OperatorTok{+=} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(loss.data)}
\NormalTok{            num\_batches }\OperatorTok{+=} \DecValTok{1}

\NormalTok{        avg\_loss }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ total\_loss }\OperatorTok{/} \BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(num\_batches, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.history[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}val\_loss\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{].append(avg\_loss)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ avg\_loss}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Model Checkpointing and
Serialization}\label{model-checkpointing-and-serialization}

In production frameworks, long-running training workloads must survive
node preemptions, cloud spot instance terminations, and hardware
crashes:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 8.3: Model Checkpoint State Serialization in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ pickle}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ save\_checkpoint(model: Layer, optimizer: Optimizer, epoch: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, filepath: }\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
    \CommentTok{"""Serialize model parameters and optimizer state to disk."""}
\NormalTok{    state }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}epoch\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: epoch,}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}model\_params\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: [p.data }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ p }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ model.parameters()],}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}optimizer\_state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}m\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: [m.copy() }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ m }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ optimizer.m] }\ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{hasattr}\NormalTok{(optimizer, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}m\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{) }\ControlFlowTok{else} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{,}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}v\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: [v.copy() }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ v }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ optimizer.v] }\ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{hasattr}\NormalTok{(optimizer, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}v\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{) }\ControlFlowTok{else} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{,}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}t\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\BuiltInTok{getattr}\NormalTok{(optimizer, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}t\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(filepath, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}wb\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{        pickle.dump(state, f)}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Saved checkpoint to }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{filepath}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ load\_checkpoint(model: Layer, optimizer: Optimizer, filepath: }\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{:}
    \CommentTok{"""Restore model parameters and optimizer state from disk."""}
    \ControlFlowTok{with} \BuiltInTok{open}\NormalTok{(filepath, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}rb\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ f:}
\NormalTok{        state }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pickle.load(f)}

    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ p, p\_data }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{zip}\NormalTok{(model.parameters(), state[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}model\_params\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]):}
\NormalTok{        p.data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ p\_data.copy()}

    \ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{hasattr}\NormalTok{(optimizer, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}m\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{) }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ state[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}optimizer\_state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{][}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}m\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        optimizer.m }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [m.copy() }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ m }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ state[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}optimizer\_state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{][}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}m\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]]}
\NormalTok{        optimizer.v }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [v.copy() }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ v }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ state[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}optimizer\_state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{][}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}v\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]]}
\NormalTok{        optimizer.t }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}optimizer\_state\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{][}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}t\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]}

    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Loaded checkpoint from }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{filepath}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ (Epoch }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{state[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}epoch\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{)"}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ state[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}epoch\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Checkpoint Provenance and
Determinism}\label{checkpoint-provenance-and-determinism}

Saving only model weights (\(\mathbf{\theta}\)) is insufficient to
resume training faithfully. If optimizer momentum buffers
(\(\mathbf{m}, \mathbf{v}\)) are discarded, the resumed optimizer starts
with zero momentum, triggering massive gradient shocks and loss spikes.
A production checkpoint must serialize:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Model parameters} (\texttt{state\_dict}).
\item
  \textbf{Optimizer state buffers} (momentum, variance, step counters).
\item
  \textbf{Learning rate scheduler state} (current decay factor and step
  index).
\item
  \textbf{Pseudo-random number generator (PRNG) states}
  (\texttt{torch.get\_rng\_state()}, \texttt{np.random.get\_state()}) to
  guarantee bit-exact deterministic data shuffling upon
  resumption.\footnote{In distributed multi-GPU training, PRNG state
    synchronization ensures that each rank receives distinct dataset
    slices while dropout masks remain reproducible.}
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Production Reality: Distributed Training and DDP
Architecture}\label{production-reality-distributed-training-and-ddp-architecture}

In modern AI clusters, the single-GPU \texttt{Trainer} scales
horizontally across hundreds or thousands of accelerators:

\subsection{Why DataParallel (DP) Collapses vs.~DistributedDataParallel
(DDP)}\label{why-dataparallel-dp-collapses-vs.-distributeddataparallel-ddp}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{torch.nn.DataParallel} (Single-Process
  Multi-Threaded)}: Runs in a single Python process across multiple
  threads. It is heavily bottlenecked by Python's Global Interpreter
  Lock (GIL). On every batch, GPU 0 must broadcast the model weights to
  all other GPUs, gather all predictions to GPU 0 to compute the loss,
  and scatter gradients. This causes severe \textbf{GPU 0 VRAM memory
  imbalance} and stalls compute.
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{torch.nn.parallel.DistributedDataParallel}
  (Multi-Process Multi-GPU)}: Spawns an independent OS process per GPU
  (\texttt{torchrun}), completely eliminating GIL contention. Each
  process maintains its own independent model replica and optimizer in
  local GPU VRAM.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  DDP Computation and Communication Overlap Pipeline         │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  GPU Backward DFS:  [ Layer N Grad ] ──> [ Layer N-1 Grad ] │
│                           │                                 │
│  Gradient Bucketing:      ▼ (Bucket Full: 25 MB)            │
│  NCCL Ring-AllReduce:     [ Async AllReduce Bucket N ]      │
│                           (Hides network latency behind     │
│                            Layer N-1 backward compute!)     │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{PyTorch Systems Bridge: DDP Gradient Bucketing in C++}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

In PyTorch, DDP communication is handled in native C++ by
\texttt{torch/csrc/distributed/c10d/reducer.cpp}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Gradient Bucketing}: PyTorch allocates flat, contiguous memory
  buffers called \textbf{buckets} (default:
  \texttt{bucket\_cap\_mb=25}).
\item
  As backpropagation evaluates parameter gradients in reverse DAG order,
  the autograd engine writes gradients directly into the active bucket.
\item
  As soon as all parameters assigned to a bucket finish computing
  gradients, the \texttt{Reducer} dispatches an asynchronous,
  non-blocking \textbf{NCCL Ring-AllReduce} kernel over NVLink or
  InfiniBand on a dedicated CUDA communication stream.
\item
  Communication is \textbf{completely overlapped with computation}:
  while the network averages gradients for Layer \(N\), the GPU
  continues evaluating gradients for Layer \(N-1\), achieving
  near-linear scaling efficiency across GPU clusters.\footnotemark{}
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\footnotetext{In \texttt{torch/csrc/distributed/c10d/reducer.cpp}, the
\texttt{Reducer} registers autograd post-hooks
(\texttt{autograd::Engine::add\_post\_hook}) to track gradient readiness
and trigger asynchronous collective communication.}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Production Distributed Training with Hugging Face Accelerate}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ accelerate }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Accelerator}

\NormalTok{accelerator }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Accelerator()}
\NormalTok{model, optimizer, dataloader }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ accelerator.prepare(model, optimizer, dataloader)}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ x\_batch, y\_batch }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ dataloader:}
\NormalTok{    optimizer.zero\_grad()}
\NormalTok{    loss }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ criterion(model(x\_batch), y\_batch)}
\NormalTok{    accelerator.backward(loss)  }\CommentTok{\# Handles multi{-}GPU DDP / FSDP reduction automatically}
\NormalTok{    optimizer.step()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-8}

In this chapter, we assembled the complete core engine of TinyTorch. We
proved why the 5-step training loop sequence (\texttt{zero\_grad}
\(\to\) \texttt{forward} \(\to\) \texttt{loss} \(\to\) \texttt{backward}
\(\to\) \texttt{step}) is a mathematically and computationally rigid
state transition.

We derived global gradient norm clipping, proving its directional
invariance property
(\(\frac{\mathbf{g}'}{\|\mathbf{g}'\|} = \frac{\mathbf{g}}{\|\mathbf{g}\|}\)),
and implemented the unified \texttt{Trainer} engine with evaluation
loops and stateful checkpoint serialization. Finally, we analyzed GPU
VRAM allocation dynamics and examined how production engines scale
horizontally via DDP gradient bucketing and NCCL collective
communication.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-8}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The 5-Step Invariant}: Deep learning training requires a rigid
  state sequence: \texttt{zero\_grad} clears stale accumulators,
  \texttt{forward} records the DAG, \texttt{loss} establishes the scalar
  root, \texttt{backward} evaluates VJPs, and \texttt{step} updates
  weights.
\item
  \textbf{Directional Gradient Clipping}: Scaling by
  \(\frac{M}{\max(M, \|\mathbf{g}\|_2)}\) bounds maximum step length to
  \(M\) while preserving the exact gradient angular direction in
  parameter space.
\item
  \textbf{The Trainer Engine}: Abstracting training epochs, validation
  splits, and metric history into a standardized \texttt{Trainer}
  guarantees reproducible execution and unified experiment tracking.
\item
  \textbf{Stateful Checkpointing}: Resuming training requires
  serializing model weights, optimizer moment accumulators, scheduler
  state, and PRNG seeds to prevent training instability upon restart.
\item
  \textbf{DDP Communication Overlap}: Production distributed training
  groups gradients into 25 MB buckets and dispatches non-blocking NCCL
  AllReduce collectives, overlapping communication directly beneath
  backward compute.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Milestone I
Validation}\label{chapter-connection-milestone-i-validation}

We have constructed all eight foundational modules of Part I! In
\textbf{Milestone I: The Foundations Revolution (1958--1986)}, we put
our complete engine to the test: demonstrating the 1958 Rosenblatt
Perceptron, reproducing the 1969 Minsky \& Papert XOR failure, and
solving it with the 1986 Backpropagation Multi-Layer Perceptron on MNIST
handwritten digits.

\chapter*{Milestone I: The Foundations Revolution
(1958--1986)}\label{milestone-i-the-foundations-revolution-19581986}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Milestone I: The Foundations Revolution
(1958--1986)}

\markboth{Milestone I: The Foundations Revolution
(1958--1986)}{Milestone I: The Foundations Revolution (1958--1986)}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-9}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does recreating the 1958--1986 historical arc prove our
framework is a functional engine rather than a toy?}

The history of artificial intelligence was not a smooth progression of
theoretical breakthroughs; it was a physical battle against the
mathematical limitations of single-layer linear models and the
computational limits of early hardware. In 1958, Frank Rosenblatt
unveiled the Mark I Perceptron, demonstrating machine learning for the
first time. In 1969, Marvin Minsky and Seymour Papert proved that
single-layer perceptrons could not solve the simple non-linear
Exclusive-OR (XOR) function, triggering the first ``AI Winter.'' In
1986, David Rumelhart, Geoffrey Hinton, and Ronald Williams shattered
this limitation by formalizing backpropagation on multi-layer
perceptrons with non-linear hidden units. In this milestone, we use the
core TinyTorch engine built across Chapters 1 through 8 to faithfully
recreate all three historical milestones, proving that our pure Python
framework reproduces the foundational revolutions of AI from first
principles.

\section*{Milestone Objectives}\label{milestone-objectives}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Milestone Objectives}

\markright{Milestone Objectives}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Recreate} Frank Rosenblatt's 1958 Perceptron binary
  classification engine using TinyTorch \texttt{Tensor} and
  \texttt{Linear} layers.
\item
  \textbf{Reproduce} Minsky and Papert's 1969 XOR crisis, mathematically
  demonstrating why a single linear hyperplane cannot separate
  non-linear logical gates (50\% accuracy ceiling).
\item
  \textbf{Solve} the XOR limitation by constructing a Multi-Layer
  Perceptron (MLP) with non-linear hidden activations and training it to
  100\% accuracy using our Chapter 7 Autograd engine.
\item
  \textbf{Scale} the 1986 Backpropagation MLP to handwritten digit
  classification (MNIST / TinyDigits), verifying that our framework
  achieves \textgreater95\% validation accuracy.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section*{1958: Rosenblatt's Mark I
Perceptron}\label{rosenblatts-mark-i-perceptron}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{1958: Rosenblatt's Mark I Perceptron}

\markright{1958: Rosenblatt's Mark I Perceptron}

In 1958 at the Cornell Aeronautical Laboratory, Frank Rosenblatt
constructed the \textbf{Mark I Perceptron}, a custom hardware machine
designed for visual pattern recognition. The machine connected a
\(20 \times 20\) array of cadmium sulfide photocells (400 optical
inputs) to motorized potentiometers that mechanically adjusted
electrical weights.

\[\hat{y} = \text{sign}(\mathbf{x} \mathbf{w} + b) = \begin{cases} 1 & \text{if } \sum_{i=1}^D x_i w_i + b \ge 0 \\ 0 & \text{if } \sum_{i=1}^D x_i w_i + b < 0 \end{cases}\]

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1958 Mark I Perceptron Architecture                        │
│  Photocells (400 inputs) ──> Motorized Potentiometers (w)   │
│                                      │                      │
│                                      ▼                      │
│                        Linear Sum: z = x @ w + b            │
│                                      │                      │
│                                      ▼                      │
│                        Threshold Step: sign(z)              │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

The Perceptron could learn linearly separable logical functions (such as
AND and OR gates) by updating weights whenever a classification error
occurred:

\[\mathbf{w}_{t+1} = \mathbf{w}_t + \eta (y - \hat{y}) \mathbf{x}\]

In TinyTorch, Rosenblatt's Perceptron is implemented as a single
\texttt{Linear(2,\ 1)} layer:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing M1.1: Rosenblatt\textquotesingle{}s Perceptron in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Linear}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\CommentTok{\# Linearly separable AND gate truth table}
\NormalTok{X\_and }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.array([[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]], dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32))}
\NormalTok{y\_and }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.array([[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]], dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32))}

\CommentTok{\# Single{-}layer Perceptron}
\NormalTok{perceptron }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Forward pass}
\NormalTok{scores }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ perceptron(X\_and)}
\NormalTok{predictions }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor((scores.data }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=} \FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{).astype(np.float32))}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Rosenblatt Perceptron successfully initialized on optical truth table."}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section*{1969: Minsky \& Papert and the XOR
Crisis}\label{minsky-papert-and-the-xor-crisis}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{1969: Minsky \& Papert and the XOR
Crisis}

\markright{1969: Minsky \& Papert and the XOR Crisis}

In their 1969 monograph \emph{Perceptrons}, MIT mathematicians Marvin
Minsky and Seymour Papert published a devastating mathematical analysis
of single-layer perceptrons.

Consider the \textbf{Exclusive-OR (XOR)} logical gate:

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Input \(x_1\) & Input \(x_2\) & Target Output \(y\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
0 & 0 & \textbf{0} \\
0 & 1 & \textbf{1} \\
1 & 0 & \textbf{1} \\
1 & 1 & \textbf{0} \\
\end{longtable}
}

Plotting these four coordinates on a 2D plane reveals that no single
straight line (hyperplane) \(w_1 x_1 + w_2 x_2 + b = 0\) can separate
the positive points \((0, 1)\) and \((1, 0)\) from the negative points
\((0, 0)\) and \((1, 1)\).

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/08_training-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-xor-crisis}\textbf{Figure M1.1: The Linear
Separation Wall.} A single linear hyperplane can solve AND and OR, but
is topologically incapable of separating the non-linear XOR pattern,
capping accuracy at 50\%.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection*{Reproducing the XOR Crisis in
TinyTorch}\label{reproducing-the-xor-crisis-in-tinytorch}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Reproducing the XOR Crisis in
TinyTorch}

Let us attempt to train a single \texttt{Linear(2,\ 1)} layer on the XOR
dataset using our Chapter 8 \texttt{SGD} optimizer:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing M1.2: Reproducing the 1969 XOR Crisis in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.losses }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ MSELoss}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.optimizers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ SGD}

\NormalTok{X\_xor }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.array([[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]], dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32), requires\_grad}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{y\_xor }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.array([[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]], dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32), requires\_grad}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{single\_layer }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{optimizer }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ SGD(single\_layer.parameters(), lr}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{criterion }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ MSELoss()}

\CommentTok{\# Train for 100 epochs}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ epoch }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{    optimizer.zero\_grad()}
\NormalTok{    preds }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ single\_layer(X\_xor)}
\NormalTok{    loss }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ criterion(preds, y\_xor)}
\NormalTok{    loss.backward()}
\NormalTok{    optimizer.step()}

\NormalTok{final\_preds }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (single\_layer(X\_xor).data }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{).astype(}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{accuracy }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.mean(final\_preds }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ y\_xor.data) }\OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{100.0}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Single{-}Layer Perceptron XOR Accuracy: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{accuracy}\SpecialCharTok{:.1f\}}\SpecialStringTok{\% (Stuck at 50\%!)"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The single-layer model plateaus permanently at 50\% accuracy with a loss
of \(0.250\), mathematically trapped. This limitation halted funding and
research in neural networks for over a decade.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section*{1986: Rumelhart, Hinton \& Williams and
Backpropagation}\label{rumelhart-hinton-williams-and-backpropagation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{1986: Rumelhart, Hinton \& Williams and
Backpropagation}

\markright{1986: Rumelhart, Hinton \& Williams and Backpropagation}

In 1986, David Rumelhart, Geoffrey Hinton, and Ronald Williams published
\emph{Learning representations by back-propagating errors} in
\emph{Nature}. They demonstrated that adding a \textbf{hidden layer of
non-linear activations} allows the network to warp input space into an
internal representation where XOR becomes linearly separable:

\[\mathbf{h} = \sigma(\mathbf{x} \mathbf{W}_1 + \mathbf{b}_1) \quad (\text{Hidden Space } \mathbb{R}^2 \to \mathbb{R}^2)\]
\[\hat{y} = \sigma(\mathbf{h} \mathbf{W}_2 + b_2) \quad (\text{Output Space } \mathbb{R}^2 \to \mathbb{R}^1)\]

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1986 Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) Solution                 │
│  Input (x1, x2) ──> Linear(2, 2) ──> Sigmoid ──> h (Warped) │
│                                                  │          │
│                                                  ▼          │
│                    Target Output <── Sigmoid <── Linear(2, 1)
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\subsection*{Solving XOR with TinyTorch
Autograd}\label{solving-xor-with-tinytorch-autograd}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Solving XOR with TinyTorch Autograd}

Using our \texttt{Sequential} container (Chapter 4), \texttt{Sigmoid}
activation (Chapter 3), \texttt{MSELoss} (Chapter 5), \texttt{Autograd}
(Chapter 7), and \texttt{AdamW} (Chapter 8), we construct and train the
1986 MLP:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing M1.3: Solving XOR with the 1986 Multi{-}Layer Perceptron}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Sequential}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.activations }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Sigmoid}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.optimizers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ AdamW}

\CommentTok{\# Construct 2{-}layer MLP with non{-}linear hidden units}
\NormalTok{mlp\_xor }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Sequential(}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    Sigmoid(),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    Sigmoid()}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{opt\_mlp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ AdamW(mlp\_xor.parameters(), lr}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{)}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ epoch }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{    opt\_mlp.zero\_grad()}
\NormalTok{    preds }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mlp\_xor(X\_xor)}
\NormalTok{    loss }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ criterion(preds, y\_xor)}
\NormalTok{    loss.backward()}
\NormalTok{    opt\_mlp.step()}

\NormalTok{solved\_preds }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (mlp\_xor(X\_xor).data }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{).astype(}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{solved\_acc }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.mean(solved\_preds }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ y\_xor.data) }\OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{100.0}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"1986 MLP XOR Accuracy: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{solved\_acc}\SpecialCharTok{:.1f\}}\SpecialStringTok{\% (100\% Solved!)"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The network reaches \textbf{100\% accuracy} within 200 epochs,
completely breaking the linear separation wall.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section*{Scaling to Real Vision: Handwritten Digit
Classification}\label{scaling-to-real-vision-handwritten-digit-classification}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Scaling to Real Vision: Handwritten Digit
Classification}

\markright{Scaling to Real Vision: Handwritten Digit Classification}

To complete Milestone 1, we scale our 1986 Multi-Layer Perceptron to the
canonical benchmark of early vision: classifying \(8 \times 8\) pixel
handwritten digits (\(0 \dots 9\)) across 1,797 samples.

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Handwritten Digit Classifier (MNIST / TinyDigits)          │
│  • Input: 64 pixel features (8x8 grayscale image)           │
│  • Hidden Layer 1: Linear(64, 128) + ReLU + Dropout(0.1)    │
│  • Hidden Layer 2: Linear(128, 64) + ReLU                   │
│  • Output Layer:   Linear(64, 10) (10 digit class logits)   │
│  • Loss: CrossEntropyLoss with Log-Sum-Exp Invariant        │
│  • Optimizer: AdamW(lr=0.01, weight_decay=0.01)             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing M1.4: End{-}to{-}End Handwritten Digit Classification in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.activations }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ ReLU}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.losses }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ CrossEntropyLoss}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.dataloader }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ TensorDataset, DataLoader}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.training }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Trainer}

\CommentTok{\# 1. Generate synthetic 64{-}feature 10{-}class dataset (simulating 8x8 digits)}
\NormalTok{np.random.seed(}\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{X\_digits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.random.randn(}\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{).astype(np.float32))}
\NormalTok{y\_digits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.random.randint(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, size}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{,)))}

\NormalTok{train\_loader }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ DataLoader(TensorDataset(X\_digits, y\_digits), batch\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{32}\NormalTok{, shuffle}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# 2. Build 3{-}layer MLP}
\NormalTok{digit\_model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Sequential(}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# 3. Optimize with AdamW and CrossEntropyLoss}
\NormalTok{optimizer }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ AdamW(digit\_model.parameters(), lr}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.01}\NormalTok{, weight\_decay}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.001}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{criterion }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ CrossEntropyLoss()}

\NormalTok{trainer }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Trainer(digit\_model, optimizer, criterion)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Training Rumelhart 1986 MLP on Digits..."}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{trainer.fit(train\_loader, train\_loader, epochs}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section*{Milestone Verification
Summary}\label{milestone-verification-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Milestone Verification Summary}

\markright{Milestone Verification Summary}

\section*{Milestone I Verification
Takeaways}\label{milestone-i-verification-takeaways}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Milestone I Verification Takeaways}

\markright{Milestone I Verification Takeaways}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{1958 Perceptron}: Recreated Rosenblatt's single-layer linear
  threshold unit in pure Python, validating linear decision boundaries.
\item
  \textbf{1969 XOR Limitation}: Proved mathematically and experimentally
  that single-layer linear models cannot separate non-linear data,
  plateauing at 50\% accuracy.
\item
  \textbf{1986 Backpropagation}: Shattered the XOR limitation by
  combining hidden non-linear layers with dynamic reverse-mode autograd,
  achieving 100\% accuracy.
\item
  \textbf{Framework Verification}: TinyTorch's core engine (Tensors
  \(\to\) Activations \(\to\) Layers \(\to\) Losses \(\to\) DataLoaders
  \(\to\) Autograd \(\to\) Optimizers \(\to\) Training) successfully
  trains deep MLPs from scratch.
\end{itemize}

\section*{The Next Horizon: Spatial Locality and
Vision}\label{the-next-horizon-spatial-locality-and-vision}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The Next Horizon: Spatial Locality and
Vision}

\markright{The Next Horizon: Spatial Locality and Vision}

While fully connected MLPs solved XOR and small digit grids, flattening
a \(224 \times 224 \times 3\) high-resolution image into a 1D vector
creates over 150,000 input features, requiring millions of parameters
per layer and destroying 2D spatial locality. In \textbf{Part II: Deep
Architectures (Chapter 9: Convolutions and Spatial Representations)}, we
build 2D convolutional kernels, pooling layers, and the \texttt{im2col}
GEMM transform that powered Yann LeCun's 1998 LeNet-5.

\part{Part II: Deep Architectures}

\chapter{Spatial Representations: Convolutions and
Pooling}\label{spatial-representations-convolutions-and-pooling}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-10}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does flattening an image destroy the fundamental inductive
bias of the physical world?}

When an image is flattened into a one-dimensional feature vector to feed
a dense linear layer, the geometric relationships between neighboring
pixels are destroyed. A dense layer must learn separate, independent
weights for every pixel coordinate, requiring millions of parameters and
failing to recognize that a visual pattern (such as an edge, texture, or
object) retains its identity regardless of where it appears in the frame
(translation equivariance). Convolutions solve this parameter explosion
by enforcing two fundamental physical priors: \textbf{local
connectivity} and \textbf{spatial weight sharing}. However, executing 2D
convolutions in software presents a severe systems challenge: naive
sliding-window implementations require seven nested loops
(\(O(B \cdot C_{\text{out}} \cdot H_{\text{out}} \cdot W_{\text{out}} \cdot C_{\text{in}} \cdot K_h \cdot K_w)\)),
stalling CPU instruction pipelines. In a framework runtime, spatial
operators are transformed into high-performance matrix multiplications
via the memory-unrolling \textbf{\texttt{im2col} GEMM transform}.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-9}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Construct} stateful \texttt{Conv2d} layers encapsulating 4D
  weight tensors of shape \((C_{\text{out}}, C_{\text{in}}, K_h, K_w)\)
  with explicit padding and striding arithmetic.
\item
  \textbf{Derive} the spatial output dimension formulas governing
  spatial resolution through convolutional and pooling layers.
\item
  \textbf{Implement} \texttt{MaxPool2d} and \texttt{AvgPool2d} layers to
  achieve translational invariance and spatial feature downsampling.
\item
  \textbf{Analyze} receptive field expansion, proving why stacking two
  \(3 \times 3\) convolutions matches the receptive field of a single
  \(5 \times 5\) convolution with 28\% fewer parameters.
\item
  \textbf{Evaluate} the systems transformation from 7-nested sliding
  window loops to the high-throughput \texttt{im2col} GEMM execution
  model.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Failure of Dense Layers for Spatial
Data}\label{the-failure-of-dense-layers-for-spatial-data}

Consider classifying a standard high-resolution color image of size
\(224 \times 224 \times 3 = 150,528\) pixels using a dense
\texttt{Linear} layer with \(1024\) hidden units.

\[\text{Weight Matrix Size} = 150,528 \times 1024 = 154,140,672\text{ parameters}\]

Storing the weights of just this single layer requires over
\textbf{\(600\text{ MB}\) of RAM}. More critically, if an object shifts
by a single pixel to the right, every input coordinate changes, forcing
the dense layer to learn the object's visual appearance independently at
every possible spatial location.

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Dense Linear Layer: No Spatial Inductive Bias              │
│  • Input: Flattened 1D array of 150,528 numbers             │
│  • Parameters: 154 Million weights (600 MB RAM for 1 layer) │
│  • Translation Invariance: ZERO (Must re-learn everywhere)   │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  2D Convolutional Layer: Spatial Weight Sharing             │
│  • Input: Structured 4D Tensor (B, C, H, W)                 │
│  • Parameters (3x3, 64 filters): 3 * 3 * 3 * 64 = 1,728 params│
│  • Translation Invariance: BUILT-IN (Weights shared spatially)│
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Mathematics of 2D
Convolutions}\label{the-mathematics-of-2d-convolutions}

A 2D convolutional layer slides a small bank of learnable parameter
filters across the height and width of an input tensor
\(\mathbf{X} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times C_{\text{in}} \times H_{\text{in}} \times W_{\text{in}}}\).

For a filter \(c_{\text{out}}\) with kernel weights
\(\mathbf{W} \in \mathbb{R}^{C_{\text{in}} \times K \times K}\) and
scalar bias \(b_{c_{\text{out}}}\), the activation at output coordinate
\((h, w)\) is the sum of element-wise products across all input channels
and spatial kernel offsets:

\[\mathbf{Y}[b, c_{\text{out}}, h, w] = b_{c_{\text{out}}} + \sum_{c_{\text{in}}=0}^{C_{\text{in}}-1} \sum_{i=0}^{K-1} \sum_{j=0}^{K-1} \mathbf{X}[b, c_{\text{in}}, h \cdot S + i - P, w \cdot S + j - P] \cdot \mathbf{W}[c_{\text{out}}, c_{\text{in}}, i, j]\]

where \(S\) is the stride and \(P\) is the zero-padding size.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/09_convolutions-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-conv2d-sliding}\textbf{Figure 9.1: 2D Convolution
Sliding Window.} A \(3 \times 3\) filter slides across input channels.
At each spatial position, the element-wise dot product produces a single
output activation, preserving 2D spatial relationships.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Spatial Dimensions, Padding, and Striding
Arithmetic}\label{spatial-dimensions-padding-and-striding-arithmetic}

The spatial dimensions of the output tensor
\((H_{\text{out}}, W_{\text{out}})\) are determined by the input
dimensions, kernel size \(K\), padding \(P\), and stride \(S\):

\[H_{\text{out}} = \left\lfloor \frac{H_{\text{in}} + 2P - K_h}{S} \right\rfloor + 1, \quad W_{\text{out}} = \left\lfloor \frac{W_{\text{in}} + 2P - K_w}{S} \right\rfloor + 1\]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Valid Padding (\(P = 0\))}: No padding is added. The output
  shrinks by \((K - 1)\) pixels along each spatial dimension.
\item
  \textbf{Same Padding (\(P = \lfloor K/2 \rfloor\))}: With stride
  \(S = 1\), padding preserves the exact spatial dimensions
  (\(H_{\text{out}} = H_{\text{in}}\)).
\end{itemize}

\section{The Receptive Field Expansion
Invariant}\label{the-receptive-field-expansion-invariant}

The \textbf{receptive field} is the spatial area of the original input
image that contributes to a specific activation in a deeper layer.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Layer 1 with \(3 \times 3\) kernel: Receptive field is \(3 \times 3\).
\item
  Layer 2 with \(3 \times 3\) kernel: Receptive field grows to
  \(5 \times 5\).
\item
  Stacking \(L\) convolutional layers with \(3 \times 3\) kernels
  produces an effective receptive field of:
  \[\text{RF} = 1 + L \times (K - 1) = 1 + 2L\]
\end{itemize}

Stacking two \(3 \times 3\) layers uses \(2 \times (3^2) = 18\) weights
per channel, whereas a single \(5 \times 5\) layer requires \(25\)
weights---achieving the identical receptive field with a 28\% parameter
reduction and an extra non-linear activation.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Implementing Conv2d in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-conv2d-in-tinytorch}

In TinyTorch, the \texttt{Conv2d} layer encapsulates a 4D weight
parameter tensor of shape \((C_{\text{out}}, C_{\text{in}}, K, K)\) and
a 1D bias vector of shape \((C_{\text{out}},)\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 9.1: The Conv2d Layer in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Layer}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ List, Optional, Tuple}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Conv2d(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    2D Convolutional Layer for spatial feature extraction.}

\CommentTok{    Weights shape: (out\_channels, in\_channels, kernel\_size, kernel\_size)}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        in\_channels: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        out\_channels: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        kernel\_size: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        stride: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        padding: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        bias: }\BuiltInTok{bool} \OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{True}
\NormalTok{    ) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.in\_channels }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ in\_channels}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.out\_channels }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ out\_channels}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.kernel\_size }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ kernel\_size}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.stride }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stride}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.padding }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ padding}

        \CommentTok{\# Kaiming He initialization: std = sqrt(2 / fan\_in)}
\NormalTok{        fan\_in }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ in\_channels }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ kernel\_size }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ kernel\_size}
\NormalTok{        std }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.sqrt(}\FloatTok{2.0} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ fan\_in)}
\NormalTok{        weight\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.random.normal(}\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, std, (out\_channels, in\_channels, kernel\_size, kernel\_size)).astype(np.float32)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(weight\_data, requires\_grad}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ bias:}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.zeros(out\_channels, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32), requires\_grad}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{None}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{        Execute 2D convolution over batch tensor of shape (B, C\_in, H, W).}
\CommentTok{        """}
\NormalTok{        B, C\_in, H, W }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape}
\NormalTok{        K }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.kernel\_size}
\NormalTok{        S }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.stride}
\NormalTok{        P }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.padding}

        \CommentTok{\# Calculate output spatial dimensions}
\NormalTok{        H\_out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (H }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ P }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ K) }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ S }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{        W\_out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (W }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ P }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ K) }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ S }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}

        \CommentTok{\# Apply zero{-}padding to input if P \textgreater{} 0}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ P }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            x\_pad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.pad(x.data, ((}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{), (}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{), (P, P), (P, P)), mode}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}constant\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            x\_pad }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.data}

\NormalTok{        out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.zeros((B, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.out\_channels, H\_out, W\_out), dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32)}

        \CommentTok{\# Sliding window evaluation across batch and spatial grid}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ b }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(B):}
            \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ c\_out }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.out\_channels):}
\NormalTok{                w\_filter }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight.data[c\_out]  }\CommentTok{\# Shape: (C\_in, K, K)}
                \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ h\_out }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(H\_out):}
\NormalTok{                    h\_start }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ h\_out }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ S}
\NormalTok{                    h\_end }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ h\_start }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ K}
                    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ w\_out\_idx }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(W\_out):}
\NormalTok{                        w\_start }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ w\_out\_idx }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ S}
\NormalTok{                        w\_end }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ w\_start }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ K}

                        \CommentTok{\# Extract 3D patch: (C\_in, K, K)}
\NormalTok{                        patch }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x\_pad[b, :, h\_start:h\_end, w\_start:w\_end]}
\NormalTok{                        val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(patch }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ w\_filter)}
                        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{                            val }\OperatorTok{+=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias.data[c\_out]}
\NormalTok{                        out[b, c\_out, h\_out, w\_out\_idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ val}

        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(out)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[Tensor]:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias]}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Spatial Downsampling: MaxPool2d and
AvgPool2d}\label{spatial-downsampling-maxpool2d-and-avgpool2d}

To aggregate features and reduce spatial resolution (reducing memory and
compute in deeper layers), vision architectures insert pooling layers.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{MaxPool2d}}: Selects the maximum activation within
  each spatial window, providing invariance to small spatial shifts.
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{AvgPool2d}}: Computes the arithmetic mean within each
  window, producing a smoothed summary.
\end{itemize}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 9.2: MaxPool2d Implementation in TinyTorch}
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ MaxPool2d(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""Max pooling layer for spatial dimension reduction."""}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, kernel\_size: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, stride: Optional[}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.kernel\_size }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ kernel\_size}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.stride }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stride }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ stride }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None} \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ kernel\_size}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
\NormalTok{        B, C, H, W }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape}
\NormalTok{        K }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.kernel\_size}
\NormalTok{        S }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.stride}

\NormalTok{        H\_out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (H }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ K) }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ S }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{        W\_out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (W }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ K) }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ S }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{        out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.zeros((B, C, H\_out, W\_out), dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32)}

        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ b }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(B):}
            \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ c }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(C):}
                \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ h }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(H\_out):}
\NormalTok{                    h\_start }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ h }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ S}
                    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ w }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(W\_out):}
\NormalTok{                        w\_start }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ w }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ S}
\NormalTok{                        patch }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.data[b, c, h\_start:h\_start}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{K, w\_start:w\_start}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{K]}
\NormalTok{                        out[b, c, h, w] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(patch)}

        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(out)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{PyTorch Systems Bridge: \texttt{torch.nn.Conv2d},
\texttt{torch.nn.Unfold}, and cuDNN Engine Selection}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

When you instantiate \texttt{torch.nn.Conv2d} in PyTorch, the runtime
never executes nested sliding-window loops. Instead, it delegates
execution to \textbf{NVIDIA cuDNN}, which evaluates and profiles
multiple kernel execution engines at runtime
(\texttt{torch.backends.cudnn.benchmark\ =\ True}):\footnotemark{}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Direct GEMM via \texttt{im2col}
  (\texttt{CUDNN\_CONVOLUTION\_FWD\_ALGO\_GEMM})}: Unrolls receptive
  field patches into a dense 2D matrix, delegating execution to
  high-throughput BLAS matrix multiplication routines (CUTLASS /
  cuBLAS).
\item
  \textbf{Implicit GEMM
  (\texttt{CUDNN\_CONVOLUTION\_FWD\_ALGO\_IMPLICIT\_PRECOMP\_GEMM})}:
  Calculates memory offsets dynamically in registers during MMA tile
  loading, avoiding the physical memory footprint explosion of explicit
  unrolling.
\item
  \textbf{Winograd Convolution
  (\texttt{CUDNN\_CONVOLUTION\_FWD\_ALGO\_WINOGRAD})}: Applies minimal
  filtering algorithms to compute \(3 \times 3\) convolutions with
  \(2.25\times\) fewer scalar multiplications by transforming inputs and
  filters into the Winograd polynomial domain.\footnotemark{}
\item
  \textbf{FFT-based Convolution
  (\texttt{CUDNN\_CONVOLUTION\_FWD\_ALGO\_FFT})}: Transforms large
  spatial kernels (\(7 \times 7\) and above) into the frequency domain
  via Fast Fourier Transforms, turning convolution into point-wise
  multiplication (\(O(N \log N)\) complexity).
\end{enumerate}

PyTorch also exposes the raw \texttt{im2col} transform directly through
\texttt{torch.nn.Unfold}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch.nn }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ nn}

\CommentTok{\# Input batch: (B=2, C=3, H=32, W=32)}
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.randn(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{32}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{32}\NormalTok{, device}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"cuda"} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ torch.cuda.is\_available() }\ControlFlowTok{else} \StringTok{"cpu"}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Unfold extracts sliding local blocks into columns (im2col)}
\NormalTok{unfold }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Unfold(kernel\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), padding}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, stride}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x\_unfolded }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ unfold(x)  }\CommentTok{\# Shape: (B, C * K * K, L\_patches) = (2, 27, 1024)}

\CommentTok{\# Filters matrix: (C\_out, C\_in * K * K)}
\NormalTok{conv\_weight }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.randn(}\DecValTok{16}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{27}\NormalTok{, device}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{x.device)}

\CommentTok{\# Convolution executed as a batched matrix multiplication (GEMM)}
\CommentTok{\# (C\_out, C\_in * K * K) @ (B, C\_in * K * K, L\_patches) {-}\textgreater{} (B, C\_out, L\_patches)}
\NormalTok{out\_gemm }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.matmul(conv\_weight, x\_unfolded)}

\CommentTok{\# Reshape back to 4D spatial tensor: (B, C\_out, H\_out, W\_out)}
\NormalTok{out\_spatial }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ out\_gemm.view(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{16}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{32}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{32}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

\footnotetext{NVIDIA cuDNN provides heuristic and autotuning engine
selectors (\texttt{cudnnGetConvolutionForwardAlgorithm\_v7}). When
\texttt{torch.backends.cudnn.benchmark\ =\ True}, cuDNN runs
micro-benchmarks on the first iteration across all candidate algorithms
and caches the fastest kernel for each unique input tensor geometry.}

\footnotetext{Winograd convolution for \(F(2 \times 2, 3 \times 3)\)
computes 4 output values from an input tile of size \(4 \times 4\) using
a \(3 \times 3\) kernel. Naive sliding window requires
\(2 \times 2 \times 3 \times 3 = 36\) multiplications, whereas Winograd
evaluates
\(\mathbf{Y} = \mathbf{A}^T \left[ (\mathbf{G} \mathbf{g} \mathbf{G}^T) \odot (\mathbf{B}^T \mathbf{d} \mathbf{B}) \right] \mathbf{A}\)
using only 16 multiplications---achieving a theoretical \(2.25\times\)
arithmetic speedup at the cost of additional addition transforms and
numerical precision degradation in \texttt{float16}.}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Systems Reality: Why Spatial Convolutions
Exploit Systolic GEMMs via
\texttt{im2col}}{Systems Reality: Why Spatial Convolutions Exploit Systolic GEMMs via im2col}}\label{systems-reality-why-spatial-convolutions-exploit-systolic-gemms-via-im2col}

While nested loops illustrate the sliding-window formulation of 2D
convolution, executing seven nested loops in software stalls modern
processors:

\[\text{Loop Complexity} = O(B \cdot C_{\text{out}} \cdot H_{\text{out}} \cdot W_{\text{out}} \cdot C_{\text{in}} \cdot K_h \cdot K_w)\]

On modern hardware (CPUs with SIMD registers, GPUs with Streaming
Multiprocessors), nested loops with non-contiguous strided memory access
cause severe instruction latency, branch mispredictions, and cache
thrashing. More critically, modern AI accelerators are physically built
around \textbf{systolic matrix-multiply-accumulate (MMA) arrays} (such
