as NVIDIA Tensor Cores or Google TPUs). Systolic arrays stream matrix
tiles through a 2D grid of interconnected register processing elements,
achieving peak computational throughput (\(O(N^3)\) multiply-accumulate
operations with only \(O(N^2)\) memory loads from SRAM).

To harness systolic hardware for spatial convolutions, framework
runtimes lower 2D convolutions into a single \textbf{General Matrix
Multiplication (GEMM)} using the \textbf{\texttt{im2col}
(image-to-column)} transformation:

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  The im2col GEMM Transformation Pipeline                                │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. Unroll Spatial Patches (im2col):                                    │
│     Input Tensor: (B, C_in, H_in, W_in)                                 │
│          │                                                              │
│          ▼ [Gather local (C_in, K, K) patches across spatial grid]      │
│     Matrix X_col: (B * H_out * W_out, C_in * K * K)                     │
│                                                                         │
│  2. Reshape Parameter Filters:                                          │
│     Filter Tensor: (C_out, C_in, K, K)                                  │
│          │                                                              │
│          ▼ [Flatten spatial filter weights into rows]                   │
│     Matrix W_row: (C_out, C_in * K * K)                                 │
│                                                                         │
│  3. Execute High-Throughput GEMM on Systolic Hardware:                  │
│     Y_col = X_col @ W_row^T                                             │
│     Dimensions: (B * H_out * W_out, C_in * K * K) @ (C_in * K * K, C_out)│
│     Result: (B * H_out * W_out, C_out)  ──> Near-Peak GPU TFLOPs!       │
│                                                                         │
│  4. Reshape to Output Spatial Tensor (col2im):                          │
│     Matrix Y_col ──> Output Tensor: (B, C_out, H_out, W_out)            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The Memory vs.~Compute Trade-off in
\texttt{im2col}}{The Memory vs.~Compute Trade-off in im2col}}\label{the-memory-vs.-compute-trade-off-in-im2col}

The \texttt{im2col} transformation involves a deliberate systems
trade-off: \textbf{memory redundancy for arithmetic throughput}.

Because neighboring convolutional patches overlap spatially, unrolling
an input image replicates pixel values in memory:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  For a \(3 \times 3\) filter with stride \(S = 1\), each input pixel is
  copied into up to \(K_h \times K_w = 9\) different rows of the
  unfolded matrix \(\mathbf{X}_{\text{col}}\).
\item
  For an input feature map of size \((3, 224, 224)\), explicit unrolling
  expands the memory footprint from \(602\text{ KB}\) to approximately
  \textbf{\(5.4\text{ MB}\)}.
\end{itemize}

In modern computer architecture, this trade-off is overwhelmingly
beneficial: DRAM and High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM) bandwidth is abundant,
whereas systolic GEMM units deliver 10× to 100× higher arithmetic
density than scalar sliding-window iteration. In production runtimes,
\textbf{Implicit GEMM} kernels eliminate even this memory expansion by
performing \texttt{im2col} index arithmetic on-the-fly directly inside
GPU register files during matrix tile loading.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Assembling a Convolutional Neural Network
(CNN)}\label{assembling-a-convolutional-neural-network-cnn}

We can now combine \texttt{Conv2d}, \texttt{ReLU}, \texttt{MaxPool2d},
and \texttt{Linear} into a complete convolutional vision architecture:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 9.3: Assembling a CNN in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Sequential, Linear}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.activations }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ ReLU}

\CommentTok{\# Define LeNet{-}style Vision Architecture}
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Flatten(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""Flattens 4D tensor (B, C, H, W) to 2D matrix (B, C*H*W)."""}
    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
\NormalTok{        B }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(x.data.reshape(B, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}

\NormalTok{cnn\_model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Sequential(}
    \CommentTok{\# Block 1: (B, 1, 28, 28) {-}\textgreater{} (B, 6, 14, 14)}
\NormalTok{    Conv2d(in\_channels}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, out\_channels}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, kernel\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, padding}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    MaxPool2d(kernel\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, stride}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}

    \CommentTok{\# Block 2: (B, 6, 14, 14) {-}\textgreater{} (B, 16, 7, 7)}
\NormalTok{    Conv2d(in\_channels}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, out\_channels}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{16}\NormalTok{, kernel\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, padding}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    MaxPool2d(kernel\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, stride}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}

    \CommentTok{\# Classifier Head: 16 * 7 * 7 = 784 features {-}\textgreater{} 10 classes}
\NormalTok{    Flatten(),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{784}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{120}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{120}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{)}

\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"CNN Model constructed with }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(cnn\_model.parameters())}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ parameter tensors."}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-9}

In this chapter, we expanded TinyTorch into spatial computing by
constructing 2D convolutions and pooling layers. We proved why dense
layers fail on visual data, demonstrating how local connectivity and
spatial weight sharing provide translation equivariance while reducing
parameter counts by orders of magnitude.

We derived the spatial dimension equations governing padding and
striding, proved how stacking small \(3 \times 3\) filters expands
receptive fields with superior parameter efficiency, and built
\texttt{Conv2d} and \texttt{MaxPool2d}. Finally, we analyzed the
\texttt{im2col} transformation that enables production frameworks to
execute convolutions as high-throughput matrix multiplications.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-9}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Spatial Weight Sharing}: Convolutions reuse a small bank of
  \((C_{\text{out}}, C_{\text{in}}, K, K)\) parameter filters across all
  spatial positions, guaranteeing translation equivariance.
\item
  \textbf{Receptive Field Scaling}: Stacking \(L\) layers of
  \(3 \times 3\) convolutions yields a receptive field of \((1 + 2L)\),
  matching larger single kernels with fewer parameters and greater
  non-linear depth.
\item
  \textbf{Padding and Striding}: Output dimensions follow
  \(\lfloor (H + 2P - K)/S \rfloor + 1\); Same Padding
  (\(P = \lfloor K/2 \rfloor\)) preserves spatial resolution when
  \(S = 1\).
\item
  \textbf{Spatial Pooling}: Max pooling selects peak activations within
  local windows, providing robustness against small translations while
  downsampling spatial resolution.
\item
  \textbf{The im2col Transformation}: Modern runtimes unroll overlapping
  image patches into dense matrices, executing spatial convolutions as
  optimized GEMM matrix multiplications.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Moving from Pixels to Natural
Language}\label{chapter-connection-moving-from-pixels-to-natural-language}

We have conquered spatial representations for vision, but how do modern
frameworks process sequential discrete data like human language? In
\textbf{Chapter 10 (Subword Tokenization and Vocabulary Construction)},
we transition into Natural Language Processing by building Byte-Pair
Encoding (BPE) tokenizers from scratch.

\chapter{Subword Tokenization and Vocabulary
Construction}\label{subword-tokenization-and-vocabulary-construction}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-11}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why is the choice of tokenization algorithm the primary bottleneck
on transformer context length and embedding table memory?}

Neural networks are continuous mathematical engines operating on vectors
of floating-point numbers, whereas human language is composed of
discrete, variable-length symbolic strings. Before a language model can
execute a single matrix multiplication, raw text must be converted into
a sequence of discrete integer token identifiers
(\(T \in \{0, 1, \dots, V-1\}^S\)). The choice of tokenization
granularity dictates a fundamental systems trade-off: word-level
tokenization creates massive vocabularies (\(V > 1,000,000\)), causing
embedding tables to consume gigabytes of memory and failing completely
on unseen words (Out-Of-Vocabulary errors); conversely, character-level
tokenization creates tiny vocabularies (\(V \approx 256\)) but expands
sequence length \(S\) by 4× to 5×, which inflates the quadratic
\(O(S^2)\) compute cost of transformer attention by 25×. Subword
\textbf{Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE)} resolves this dilemma by finding the
optimal information-theoretic compression frontier between vocabulary
size and sequence length.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-10}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Construct} the \texttt{Tokenizer} base class and implement
  \texttt{CharTokenizer} and \texttt{BPETokenizer} in pure Python.
\item
  \textbf{Implement} the iterative Byte-Pair Encoding merge algorithm,
  extracting statistical character pair frequencies across training
  corpora.
\item
  \textbf{Analyze} the Pareto trade-off between vocabulary size \(V\)
  (embedding table memory footprint) and tokenized sequence length \(S\)
  (quadratic attention compute cost).
\item
  \textbf{Design} reversible encoding and decoding pipelines with
  dedicated handling for out-of-vocabulary
  (\texttt{\textless{}UNK\textgreater{}}) and boundary tokens
  (\texttt{\textless{}BOS\textgreater{}},
  \texttt{\textless{}EOS\textgreater{}}).
\item
  \textbf{Evaluate} why modern Large Language Models (GPT-4, LLaMA)
  standardized on BPE tokenizers with \(V \approx 32,000 - 100,000\).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Discrete-to-Continuous Bridge in
NLP}\label{the-discrete-to-continuous-bridge-in-nlp}

To process natural language, a machine learning framework must execute a
two-stage translation:

\[\text{Raw Text (String)} \xrightarrow{\quad \text{Tokenizer (Ch 10)} \quad} \text{Token IDs (Integers } \mathbb{Z}^S \text{)} \xrightarrow{\quad \text{Embedding Layer (Ch 11)} \quad} \text{Continuous Vectors } \mathbb{R}^{S \times D}\]

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  The NLP Ingestion Pipeline                                 │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. Raw Text:    "Machine learning systems are fast"        │
│                         │ (Tokenizer)                       │
│                         ▼                                   │
│  2. Token IDs:   [1482, 3819, 2901, 461, 2810]              │
│                         │ (Embedding Lookup)                │
│                         ▼                                   │
│  3. Vectors:     Matrix of shape (5, 768) in float32        │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

The tokenizer's responsibility is to map strings into integer indices
and back, managing a discrete vocabulary dictionary:

\[\text{Vocabulary: } \mathcal{V} = \{ \text{token}_0: 0, \text{token}_1: 1, \dots, \text{token}_{V-1}: V-1 \}\]

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Tokenization Granularity
Dilemma}\label{the-tokenization-granularity-dilemma}

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Granularity Comparison on: "unhappiness"                   │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. Word-Level:                                             │
│     • Tokens: ["unhappiness"] (1 token)                     │
│     • Vocab Size: Huge (1,000,000+ words).                  │
│     • Failure Mode: Out-of-Vocabulary (OOV) on new words.   │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  2. Character-Level:                                        │
│     • Tokens: ['u','n','h','a','p','p','i','n','e','s','s']│
│     • Vocab Size: Tiny (256 ASCII / Unicode bytes).         │
│     • Failure Mode: Sequence length S explodes 11x!         │
│       O(S^2) attention compute increases by 121x!           │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  3. Subword (BPE):                                          │
│     • Tokens: ["un", "happi", "ness"] (3 tokens)            │
│     • Vocab Size: Tunable (e.g., 32,000 tokens).            │
│     • Property: Zero OOV (falls back to characters);        │
│       compact sequence length S.                            │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\section{Information-Theoretic Entropy Maximization in
BPE}\label{information-theoretic-entropy-maximization-in-bpe}

From Claude Shannon's Information Theory, the average information
content (entropy) per token is defined as:

\[H(T) = -\sum_{t \in \mathcal{V}} p(t) \log_2 p(t) \quad \text{bits/token}\]

Natural language is statistically redundant:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Character-level tokenization} (\(V \approx 256\)) produces low
  entropy per token (\(H \approx 2.5\text{ bits}\)), requiring 4--5
  tokens per word. Because transformer self-attention scales
  quadratically with sequence length (\(O(S^2)\) FLOPs and KV cache
  memory), character tokenization wastes \(16\times\) to \(25\times\)
  more attention compute on low-entropy symbol chunks.
\item
  \textbf{Word-level tokenization} (\(V > 1,000,000\)) achieves high
  entropy per token (\(H \approx 11\text{ bits}\)), but follows a heavy
  Zipfian long-tail distribution: the embedding table footprint
  (\(V \times D\)) explodes beyond \(16\text{ GB}\) of VRAM, and unseen
  words trigger fatal Out-Of-Vocabulary (OOV) failures.
\item
  \textbf{Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE)} acts as an iterative Minimum
  Description Length (MDL) compression algorithm. By iteratively merging
  the highest-frequency adjacent symbol pairs, BPE compresses frequent
  character clusters into single tokens while preserving subword
  decomposition for rare words. This flattens the token probability
  distribution \(p(t)\), pushing entropy \(H(T)\) toward theoretical
  maximum capacity (\(\log_2 V\)) and maximizing the semantic
  information density delivered per unit of transformer context window.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE)
Algorithm}\label{the-byte-pair-encoding-bpe-algorithm}

Byte-Pair Encoding (Sennrich et al., 2015; Radford et al., 2019) is a
data-driven compression algorithm that constructs a subword vocabulary
from a training text corpus:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Initialize Vocabulary}: Start with all individual base
  characters (or raw UTF-8 bytes) present in the training text.
\item
  \textbf{Frequency Counting}: Tokenize the text into individual
  characters and count the frequency of all adjacent symbol pairs.
\item
  \textbf{Merge Most Frequent Pair}: Identify the most frequent pair
  \((A, B)\), create a new merged token \(AB\), and add it to the
  vocabulary.
\item
  \textbf{Repeat}: Iterate steps 2 and 3 until the desired vocabulary
  size \(V\) or target number of merges is reached.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/10_tokenization-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-bpe-merging}\textbf{Figure 10.1: Byte-Pair Encoding
Merge Progression.} Base characters are iteratively combined based on
corpus frequency: \texttt{\textquotesingle{}l\textquotesingle{}} +
\texttt{\textquotesingle{}o\textquotesingle{}} \(\to\)
\texttt{\textquotesingle{}lo\textquotesingle{}},
\texttt{\textquotesingle{}l\textquotesingle{}} +
\texttt{\textquotesingle{}o\textquotesingle{}} \(\to\)
\texttt{\textquotesingle{}lo\textquotesingle{}},
\texttt{\textquotesingle{}lo\textquotesingle{}} +
\texttt{\textquotesingle{}w\textquotesingle{}} \(\to\)
\texttt{\textquotesingle{}low\textquotesingle{}}.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Implementing BPETokenizer in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-bpetokenizer-in-tinytorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 10.1: The BPE Tokenizer Engine in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ collections }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Counter}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Dict, List, Tuple, Optional}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ BPETokenizer:}
    \CommentTok{"""Byte{-}Pair Encoding (BPE) tokenizer implemented from scratch."""}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, vocab\_size: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{256}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.target\_vocab\_size }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ vocab\_size}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.merges: List[Tuple[}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{, }\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{]] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.token\_to\_id: Dict[}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{, }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{\}}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.id\_to\_token: Dict[}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, }\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{\}}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.unk\_token }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{"\textless{}UNK\textgreater{}"}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ \_get\_stats(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, splits: List[List[}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{]]) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Counter:}
        \CommentTok{"""Count frequencies of adjacent token pairs across all words."""}
\NormalTok{        pairs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Counter()}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ word }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ splits:}
            \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(word) }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{                pairs[(word[i], word[i }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])] }\OperatorTok{+=} \DecValTok{1}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ pairs}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ train(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, corpus: }\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Train BPE merges on a raw text corpus."""}
        \CommentTok{\# 1. Initialize with unique base characters}
\NormalTok{        words }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ corpus.split()}
\NormalTok{        splits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [[char }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ char }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ word] }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ word }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ words]}

        \CommentTok{\# Build base vocabulary}
\NormalTok{        unique\_chars }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{sorted}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{set}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{""}\NormalTok{.join(corpus.split()))))}
\NormalTok{        base\_vocab }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.unk\_token] }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ unique\_chars}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ idx, token }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{enumerate}\NormalTok{(base\_vocab):}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.token\_to\_id[token] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ idx}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.id\_to\_token[idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ token}

        \CommentTok{\# 2. Iteratively merge most frequent pairs}
\NormalTok{        num\_merges }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.target\_vocab\_size }\OperatorTok{{-}} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.token\_to\_id)}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ \_ }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, num\_merges)):}
\NormalTok{            stats }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.\_get\_stats(splits)}
            \ControlFlowTok{if} \KeywordTok{not}\NormalTok{ stats:}
                \ControlFlowTok{break}

            \CommentTok{\# Find most frequent pair}
\NormalTok{            best\_pair }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stats.most\_common(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{][}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.merges.append(best\_pair)}

            \CommentTok{\# Register new merged token}
\NormalTok{            new\_token }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{""}\NormalTok{.join(best\_pair)}
\NormalTok{            new\_id }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.token\_to\_id)}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.token\_to\_id[new\_token] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ new\_id}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.id\_to\_token[new\_id] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ new\_token}

            \CommentTok{\# Apply merge to active splits}
\NormalTok{            new\_splits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
            \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ word }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ splits:}
\NormalTok{                new\_word }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
\NormalTok{                i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}
                \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(word):}
                    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(word) }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1} \KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ (word[i], word[i }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]) }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ best\_pair:}
\NormalTok{                        new\_word.append(new\_token)}
\NormalTok{                        i }\OperatorTok{+=} \DecValTok{2}
                    \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{                        new\_word.append(word[i])}
\NormalTok{                        i }\OperatorTok{+=} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{                new\_splits.append(new\_word)}
\NormalTok{            splits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ new\_splits}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ encode(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, text: }\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{]:}
        \CommentTok{"""Encode string into list of token integer IDs."""}
\NormalTok{        words }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ text.split()}
\NormalTok{        token\_ids }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}

        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ word }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ words:}
            \CommentTok{\# Start with characters}
\NormalTok{            subwords }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [c }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ c }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ word]}

            \CommentTok{\# Apply learned merges in order of creation}
            \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ pair }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.merges:}
\NormalTok{                merged\_token }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{""}\NormalTok{.join(pair)}
\NormalTok{                new\_subwords }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
\NormalTok{                i }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}
                \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(subwords):}
                    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ i }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(subwords) }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1} \KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ (subwords[i], subwords[i }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]) }\OperatorTok{==}\NormalTok{ pair:}
\NormalTok{                        new\_subwords.append(merged\_token)}
\NormalTok{                        i }\OperatorTok{+=} \DecValTok{2}
                    \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{                        new\_subwords.append(subwords[i])}
\NormalTok{                        i }\OperatorTok{+=} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{                subwords }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ new\_subwords}

            \CommentTok{\# Map to IDs}
            \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ sw }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ subwords:}
\NormalTok{                token\_ids.append(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.token\_to\_id.get(sw, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.token\_to\_id[}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.unk\_token]))}

        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ token\_ids}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ decode(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, token\_ids: List[}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{]) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Decode list of integer IDs back to string."""}
\NormalTok{        tokens }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.id\_to\_token.get(idx, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.unk\_token) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ idx }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ token\_ids]}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \StringTok{" "}\NormalTok{.join(tokens)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Vocabulary Management and Special
Tokens}\label{vocabulary-management-and-special-tokens}

In production language models, tokenizers reserve special non-linguistic
tokens for framework control flow:

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Special Token Architecture                                 │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  • <PAD> (ID: 0) : Pads variable-length sequences in batch. │
│  • <UNK> (ID: 1) : Represents unrecognized characters.      │
│  • <BOS> (ID: 2) : Beginning of sequence marker.            │
│  • <EOS> (ID: 3) : End of sequence / generation stop flag.  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

When batching text of different lengths (e.g., Sentence 1 has 12 tokens,
Sentence 2 has 20 tokens), the data pipeline pads Sentence 1 with eight
\texttt{\textless{}PAD\textgreater{}} tokens to construct a uniform 2D
tensor of shape \((B, S) = (2, 20)\).

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Production Systems Bridge: OpenAI \texttt{tiktoken}, Byte-Level BPE, and
Rust Tokenizers}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

Modern production LLMs (GPT-4, LLaMA-3, Mistral) utilize
\textbf{Byte-Level Byte-Pair Encoding (BBPE)}. Rather than initializing
the vocabulary with Unicode characters, BBPE initializes with the 256
fundamental byte values of UTF-8:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Zero Out-of-Vocabulary (OOV) Fallback}: Any arbitrary string
  in any human language, source code, or binary payload decomposes into
  a sequence of bytes. The \texttt{\textless{}UNK\textgreater{}} token
  is entirely eliminated.
\item
  \textbf{Byte-Fallback Mechanism}: Common multi-byte Unicode glyphs
  (such as emojis or non-Latin scripts) are merged into single tokens if
  frequent, but fall back gracefully to raw bytes if rare.
\end{enumerate}

In production stacks, tokenizers are implemented as multi-threaded Rust
engines (such as OpenAI \texttt{tiktoken} and Hugging Face
\texttt{tokenizers}):\footnotemark{}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ tiktoken}

\CommentTok{\# Cl100k\_base tokenizer used by GPT{-}4 (Vocab Size: 100,277)}
\NormalTok{enc }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tiktoken.get\_encoding(}\StringTok{"cl100k\_base"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{tokens }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ enc.encode(}\StringTok{"Machine learning systems are fast! 🚀"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Token IDs:"}\NormalTok{, tokens)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Decoded:"}\NormalTok{, enc.decode(tokens))}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Bytes per token:"}\NormalTok{, [enc.decode\_single\_token\_bytes(t) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ t }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ tokens])}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

These native tokenizers process text at over \textbf{10--20
Megabytes/second per CPU core}, guaranteeing that token ingestion never
starves GPU execution engines.

\end{tcolorbox}

\footnotetext{OpenAI's \texttt{tiktoken} uses deterministic regex
splitting to partition raw text into independent word-level chunks prior
to BPE merging. This allows multiple CPU worker threads to execute
parallel hash-table pair lookups without global synchronization locks.}

\section{Systems Reality: Host Ingestion Pipelines and Tokenizer
Throughput}\label{systems-reality-host-ingestion-pipelines-and-tokenizer-throughput}

In high-throughput training and inference clusters (such as vLLM or
Hugging Face TGI), tokenization executes on host CPU cores, while matrix
multiplications execute on accelerator GPUs.

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Host CPU Ingestion Pipeline vs. GPU Compute Engine                     │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Host CPU (Rust/C++ Tokenizer)          GPU Accelerator (Matrix Engine) │
│  ┌───────────────────────────┐          ┌─────────────────────────────┐ │
│  │ Raw String Stream         │          │ Transformer Forward Pass    │ │
│  │      │                    │          │                             │ │
│  │      ▼ [Regex Chunking]   │          │                             │ │
│  │ Parallel BPE Merge (SRAM) │  PCIe    │ High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM) │ │
│  │      │                    │ ───────> │ GEMM Matmul on Tensor Cores │ │
│  │      ▼                    │ DMA      │                             │ │
│  │ Pinned Host Buffer (int32)│          │                             │ │
│  └───────────────────────────┘          └─────────────────────────────┘ │
│  Throughput: >20 MB/s per core          Throughput: TeraFLOPs / PFLOPS  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

If a Python-based tokenizer takes \(10\text{ ms}\) to tokenize a prompt
batch, but the GPU forward pass executes in \(2\text{ ms}\), the
training cluster becomes \textbf{tokenizer-bound}, leaving expensive
accelerator hardware stalled on PCIe transfers. High-performance
framework pipelines use asynchronous pinned memory buffers
(\texttt{torch.utils.data.DataLoader(pin\_memory=True,\ num\_workers=8)})
to interleave CPU subword tokenization with GPU kernel execution.

\section{Check Your Understanding: Embedding Memory
Calculation}\label{check-your-understanding-embedding-memory-calculation}

Suppose we configure a language model with hidden dimension
\(D = 4096\).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  What is the memory footprint of the embedding table if we use a
  word-level tokenizer with \(V = 1,000,000\) in \texttt{float32}?
\item
  What is the memory footprint if we use a BPE tokenizer with
  \(V = 32,000\)?
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Solution:} - Word-Level Table:
\(1,000,000 \times 4096 \times 4\text{ bytes} = 16,384,000,000\text{ bytes} \approx 16.38\text{ GB}\).
- BPE Table:
\(32,000 \times 4096 \times 4\text{ bytes} = 524,288,000\text{ bytes} \approx 524.3\text{ MB}\).

Switching from word-level to BPE subwords reduces embedding memory by
\textbf{\(15.8\text{ GB}\)}, freeing crucial GPU VRAM for model weights
and activation caches.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-10}

In this chapter, we constructed the first component of TinyTorch's
Natural Language Processing engine: the tokenizer. We analyzed the
fundamental dilemma between word-level, character-level, and subword
tokenization, proving how BPE optimizes the trade-off between embedding
table memory and quadratic attention sequence length.

We implemented the complete \texttt{BPETokenizer} in pure Python,
extracting pair frequencies and executing iterative subword merges. We
examined special token conventions
(\texttt{\textless{}PAD\textgreater{}},
\texttt{\textless{}UNK\textgreater{}},
\texttt{\textless{}EOS\textgreater{}}) required for batched transformer
execution, and calculated the physical RAM savings achieved by subword
vocabularies.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-10}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Discrete-to-Continuous Bridge}: Tokenizers convert
  variable-length discrete text strings into integer token IDs
  \(\mathbb{Z}^S\) for downstream embedding tables.
\item
  \textbf{The BPE Invariant}: Byte-Pair Encoding merges frequent
  character pairs into subwords, eliminating Out-of-Vocabulary errors
  while maintaining compact sequence lengths.
\item
  \textbf{Embedding vs.~Sequence Cost}: Vocabulary size \(V\) dictates
  embedding table RAM (\(V \times D\)), while sequence length \(S\)
  dictates attention compute (\(O(S^2)\)).
\item
  \textbf{Special Tokens}: \texttt{\textless{}PAD\textgreater{}},
  \texttt{\textless{}BOS\textgreater{}},
  \texttt{\textless{}EOS\textgreater{}}, and
  \texttt{\textless{}UNK\textgreater{}} provide essential control
  signals for mini-batch collation and autoregressive generation
  stopping.
\item
  \textbf{Host Tokenizer Throughput}: High-throughput inference requires
  fast tokenizers to prevent CPU string processing from bottlenecking
  GPU matrix engines.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: From Token IDs to Continuous
Embeddings}\label{chapter-connection-from-token-ids-to-continuous-embeddings}

Now that we can convert text into integer token arrays, how do we map
discrete IDs into dense, continuous vector spaces? In \textbf{Chapter 11
(Representing Sequences: Embeddings and Positional Encoding)}, we
construct embedding lookup tables, sinusoidal positional encodings, and
learned sequence representations.

\chapter{Representing Sequences: Embeddings and Positional
Encoding}\label{representing-sequences-embeddings-and-positional-encoding}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-12}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does a self-attention mechanism treat a shuffled sentence
identically to the original without positional encodings?}

Unlike Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) that process tokens sequentially
step-by-step, transformer architectures process all tokens across an
entire sequence simultaneously in parallel. Because the fundamental
mathematical operation of self-attention consists of pairwise dot
products, the architecture is inherently \textbf{permutation-invariant}:
swapping the order of words in a sentence produces identical attention
scores, destroying syntax and meaning. To represent natural language
sequences, frameworks must perform two orthogonal transformations:
mapping discrete integer token IDs into dense semantic feature spaces
via an \textbf{\texttt{Embedding} lookup table}
(\(\mathbb{R}^{V \times D}\)), and injecting sequence order via
\textbf{Positional Encodings} (\(\mathbb{R}^{S \times D}\)). In a
framework runtime, embedding lookup is a memory-bound gather operation
that converts lightweight integer arrays into high-dimensional
floating-point tensors.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-11}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Construct} the \texttt{Embedding} layer encapsulating a
  learnable \((V, D)\) weight matrix with zero-copy integer index
  slicing.
\item
  \textbf{Prove} why attention mechanisms are permutation-invariant and
  demonstrate why positional encodings are mathematically required.
\item
  \textbf{Derive} and implement Vaswani sinusoidal positional encodings
  using geometric frequency progressions.
\item
  \textbf{Implement} learned positional embedding tables as used in
  autoregressive GPT architectures.
\item
  \textbf{Analyze} the memory bandwidth and cache locality trade-offs of
  sparse embedding gather and scatter operations.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Discrete Lookup Table: Zero-Compute Pointer
Indexing}\label{the-discrete-lookup-table-zero-compute-pointer-indexing}

An embedding layer maps discrete token integers
\(t \in \{0, 1, \dots, V-1\}\) to continuous \(D\)-dimensional vectors
\(\mathbf{e}_t \in \mathbb{R}^D\).

Mathematically, an embedding lookup is formalized as multiplying a 1-hot
row vector \(\mathbf{x}_{\text{one-hot}} \in \{0, 1\}^V\) by a weight
parameter matrix
\(\mathbf{W}_{\text{embed}} \in \mathbb{R}^{V \times D}\):

\[\mathbf{e}_t = \mathbf{x}_{\text{one-hot}} \mathbf{W}_{\text{embed}} = \mathbf{W}_{\text{embed}}[t, :]\]

If executed literally as a matrix multiplication, evaluating
\(\mathbf{x}_{\text{one-hot}} \mathbf{W}_{\text{embed}}\) for a batch of
sequences would require \(B \cdot S \cdot V \cdot D\)
multiply-accumulate operations---where
\(\frac{V-1}{V} \approx 99.999\%\) of arithmetic operations are
multiplying zeros!

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Embedding Lookup: Mathematical Formalism vs. Physical Memory Reality   │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Mathematical Abstraction (Dense Matrix Multiply):                      │
│  [0, 0, ..., 1, ..., 0] @ Matrix(V=32000, D=4096) ──> Vector(D)         │
│  • FLOPs: 2 * 32,000 * 4,096 = 262,144,000 FLOPs (99.997% wasted on 0s)│
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Hardware Execution Reality (Zero-Compute Pointer Gather):              │
│  Index = 42 ──> Pointer: Base_Address + 42 * 4096 * 4 bytes             │
│  • FLOPs: EXACTLY ZERO!                                                 │
│  • Memory Operation: Direct DRAM/HBM Strided Cache Line Fetch           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

In software and hardware execution, deep learning runtimes never
allocate one-hot vectors or execute arithmetic multiplications for
embeddings. Instead, the embedding layer is executed as an
\textbf{\(O(1)\) memory gather operation} (pointer indexing arithmetic):

\[\text{Byte Address}(t) = \text{Base\_Pointer} + t \times D \times \text{sizeof}(\text{dtype})\]

This fundamental architectural insight means embedding layers have an
\textbf{arithmetic intensity of zero FLOPs per byte}: their performance
is entirely bounded by DRAM/HBM memory bandwidth and cache latency.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Implementing the Embedding Layer in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-the-embedding-layer-in-tinytorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 11.1: The Embedding Layer in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Layer}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ List, Optional}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Embedding(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Learnable Embedding lookup table mapping token IDs to dense vectors.}

\CommentTok{    Weights shape: (num\_embeddings, embedding\_dim)}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, num\_embeddings: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, embedding\_dim: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_embeddings }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ num\_embeddings}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.embedding\_dim }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ embedding\_dim}

        \CommentTok{\# Standard normal initialization with variance scaled to embedding\_dim}
\NormalTok{        weight\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.random.normal(}\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ np.sqrt(embedding\_dim), (num\_embeddings, embedding\_dim)).astype(np.float32)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(weight\_data, requires\_grad}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, indices: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{        Lookup embeddings for input token tensor of shape (B, S).}
\CommentTok{        Returns Tensor of shape (B, S, embedding\_dim).}
\CommentTok{        """}
\NormalTok{        idx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ indices.data.astype(}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{)}
        \CommentTok{\# Direct numpy integer fancy indexing (Memory Gather)}
\NormalTok{        out }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight.data[idx]}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(out)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[Tensor]:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Permutation Invariance Problem in
Attention}\label{the-permutation-invariance-problem-in-attention}

Consider two sentences with identical words in reversed order:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \emph{``The dog bit the cat.''}
\item
  \emph{``The cat bit the dog.''}
\end{enumerate}

If these words are converted into token embeddings
\((\mathbf{e}_{\text{dog}}, \mathbf{e}_{\text{bit}}, \dots)\) and fed
into an attention mechanism, the self-attention score between any two
words \(i\) and \(j\) depends purely on their feature dot product:

\[\text{Attention}(\mathbf{e}_i, \mathbf{e}_j) = \frac{\mathbf{e}_i \mathbf{e}_j^T}{\sqrt{D}}\]

Because addition and dot products are commutative, the self-attention
mechanism computes the exact same representation regardless of word
order. The model cannot determine whether the dog or the cat performed
the biting!

\section{The Positional Invariant}\label{the-positional-invariant}

To allow a transformer to perceive word order, we must add a unique
position-dependent vector \(\mathbf{p}_{\text{pos}} \in \mathbb{R}^D\)
directly to the token embedding \(\mathbf{e}_t\):

\[\mathbf{x}_{\text{input}}[b, pos] = \mathbf{e}[b, pos] + \mathbf{p}[pos]\]

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/11_embeddings-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-embeddings-pos}\textbf{Figure 11.1: Combining Token
and Positional Embeddings.} Discrete token IDs are gathered from the
embedding table. Position vectors are added element-wise, creating
position-aware semantic representations.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Vaswani Sinusoidal Positional
Encoding}\label{vaswani-sinusoidal-positional-encoding}

In \emph{Attention Is All You Need} (2017), Vaswani et al.~introduced
\textbf{Sinusoidal Positional Encoding}, using sine and cosine waves of
varying frequencies across the embedding dimensions:

\[PE_{(pos, 2i)} = \sin\left(\frac{pos}{10000^{2i / D}}\right)\]

\[PE_{(pos, 2i+1)} = \cos\left(\frac{pos}{10000^{2i / D}}\right)\]

where \(pos \in [0, S-1]\) is the token position and
\(i \in [0, D/2 - 1]\) indexes the embedding dimension.

\subsection{Mathematical Properties of Sinusoidal
Encodings}\label{mathematical-properties-of-sinusoidal-encodings}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Unique Position Signatures}: Every position \(pos\) receives a
  unique geometric coordinate in \(\mathbb{R}^D\).
\item
  \textbf{Relative Position Linearity}: For any fixed offset \(k\),
  \(PE_{pos+k}\) can be represented as a linear projection of
  \(PE_{pos}\) via trigonometric angle-addition formulas:
\end{enumerate}

\[\sin(\omega (pos + k)) = \sin(\omega \cdot pos)\cos(\omega k) + \cos(\omega \cdot pos)\sin(\omega k)\]

This allows the self-attention mechanism to learn relative distances
between tokens purely through linear projections.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 11.2: Sinusoidal Positional Encoding in TinyTorch}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ create\_sinusoidal\_embeddings(max\_len: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, d\_model: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ np.ndarray:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Generate fixed sinusoidal positional encoding matrix of shape (max\_len, d\_model).}
\CommentTok{    """}
\NormalTok{    pe }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.zeros((max\_len, d\_model), dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32)}
\NormalTok{    position }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.arange(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, max\_len, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32)[:, np.newaxis]}

    \CommentTok{\# Division term: 10000\^{}(2i / d\_model)}
\NormalTok{    div\_term }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.exp(np.arange(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, d\_model, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32) }\OperatorTok{*} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(np.log(}\FloatTok{10000.0}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ d\_model))}

    \CommentTok{\# Apply sin to even indices, cos to odd indices}
\NormalTok{    pe[:, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{::}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.sin(position }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ div\_term)}
\NormalTok{    pe[:, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{::}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.cos(position }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ div\_term)}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ pe}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Learned Positional Embeddings
(GPT-Style)}\label{learned-positional-embeddings-gpt-style}

While sinusoidal encodings are fixed and non-trainable, autoregressive
models (such as GPT-2, GPT-3, and BERT) frequently use \textbf{Learned
Positional Embeddings}.

A learned positional embedding layer is simply a second
\texttt{Embedding} table of shape \((S_{\text{max}}, D)\). During
training, the framework optimizes position representations alongside
token weights via backpropagation:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 11.3: Complete GPT{-}Style Position{-}Aware Embedding Layer}
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ GPTEmbedding(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Combines learned token embeddings and learned positional embeddings.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, vocab\_size: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, max\_seq\_len: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, d\_model: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.token\_embed }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Embedding(num\_embeddings}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{vocab\_size, embedding\_dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{d\_model)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.pos\_embed }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Embedding(num\_embeddings}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{max\_seq\_len, embedding\_dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{d\_model)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ d\_model}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, input\_ids: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
\NormalTok{        B, S }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ input\_ids.shape}

        \CommentTok{\# 1. Lookup token embeddings: (B, S, D)}
\NormalTok{        tok\_vecs }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.token\_embed(input\_ids)}

        \CommentTok{\# 2. Generate position indices: [0, 1, 2, ..., S{-}1]}
\NormalTok{        pos\_indices }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.arange(S, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.int32)[np.newaxis, :])  }\CommentTok{\# Shape: (1, S)}

        \CommentTok{\# 3. Lookup position embeddings: (1, S, D)}
\NormalTok{        pos\_vecs }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.pos\_embed(pos\_indices)}

        \CommentTok{\# 4. Element{-}wise addition (broadcasting batch dimension)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(tok\_vecs.data }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ pos\_vecs.data)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[Tensor]:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.token\_embed.parameters() }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.pos\_embed.parameters()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{PyTorch Systems Bridge: \texttt{torch.nn.Embedding}, Sparse Gradients,
and \texttt{EmbeddingBag}}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

In PyTorch, \texttt{torch.nn.Embedding} wraps high-performance CUDA
memory gather kernels (\texttt{aten/src/ATen/native/cuda/Embedding.cu}):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch.nn }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ nn}

\CommentTok{\# Dense embedding lookup table}
\NormalTok{embed }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Embedding(}
\NormalTok{    num\_embeddings}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{32000}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    embedding\_dim}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{4096}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    padding\_idx}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,      }\CommentTok{\# Gradients for padding index are strictly zeroed}
\NormalTok{    sparse}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}        \CommentTok{\# If True, backward pass yields sparse torch.sparse\_coo gradient}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{token\_ids }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.tensor([[}\DecValTok{101}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2054}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2003}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{102}\NormalTok{], [}\DecValTok{101}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1037}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3899}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{102}\NormalTok{]])}
\NormalTok{vectors }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ embed(token\_ids)  }\CommentTok{\# Shape: (2, 4, 4096)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Dense vs.~Sparse Embedding
Gradients}\label{dense-vs.-sparse-embedding-gradients}

When \texttt{sparse=False} (default), the backward pass allocates a full
dense \((V, D)\) gradient tensor \(\nabla_{\mathbf{W}}\). For
\(V = 1,000,000\) and \(D = 4096\), this allocates \(16.38\text{ GB}\)
of VRAM just to store mostly zero gradients! Setting
\texttt{sparse=True} returns a sparse coordinate
(\texttt{torch.sparse\_coo}) tensor that records only the updated row
indices, combined with \texttt{torch.optim.SparseAdam} to update massive
embedding tables in recommendation engines.\footnotemark{}

In recommendation systems (such as Meta DLRM), PyTorch provides
\texttt{nn.EmbeddingBag}, which fuses the integer table lookup and the
sum/mean pooling into a single CUDA kernel, cutting DRAM bus roundtrips
by over 80\%.\footnotemark{}

\end{tcolorbox}

\footnotetext{In CUDA, the dense backward kernel executes an
\texttt{atomicAdd} across threads if multiple input tokens reference the
same embedding index \(t\), preventing race conditions during gradient
accumulation.}

\footnotetext{In the PyTorch C++ source
(\texttt{aten/src/ATen/native/EmbeddingBag.cpp}), \texttt{EmbeddingBag}
performs parallel reduction across row indices in GPU shared memory
without allocating intermediate \((B, S, D)\) tensor buffers.}

\section{Systems Reality: Memory Bandwidth and the Backward Scatter-Add
Hazard}\label{systems-reality-memory-bandwidth-and-the-backward-scatter-add-hazard}

Embedding layers exhibit an extreme systems imbalance between the
forward gather and the backward scatter:

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Embedding Forward Gather vs. Backward Scatter-Add Pipeline             │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  FORWARD PASS: Strided Memory Gather (Zero FLOPs)                       │
│  Indices [42, 108, 42] ──> Fetch Rows [42, 108, 42] into HBM            │
│  • Latency: DRAM random-access latency (~100-200 ns)                    │
│  • Memory Bandwidth: Uncoalesced reads across 128-byte cache lines      │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  BACKWARD PASS: Atomic Scatter-Add Write Conflict                       │
│  dL/dOutput ──> Accumulate Gradients into Rows [42, 108, 42]            │
│  • Write Hazard: Tokens 0 and 2 BOTH write to Row 42!                   │
│  • Hardware Solution: GPU atomicAdd() locks memory bus lines, causing   │
│    warp serialization when high-frequency stopwords dominate batches. │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

Because token lookups access non-contiguous memory rows across the
table, GPU L2 caches experience high miss rates. In modern LLM
architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral), embedding tables are often tied
with the final output linear projection head
(\texttt{lm\_head.weight\ =\ embed.weight}) to save hundreds of
megabytes of GPU memory, while position representations have evolved
from learned lookup tables to arithmetic \textbf{Rotary Position
Embeddings (RoPE)} that require zero additional memory allocations.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-11}

In this chapter, we constructed the semantic and positional
representation foundation of TinyTorch's NLP engine. We analyzed the
discrete lookup table abstraction, proving how integer fancy indexing
replaces expensive one-hot matrix multiplications with \(O(1)\) memory
gather operations.

We solved the fundamental permutation invariance problem of
self-attention by deriving and implementing both Vaswani sinusoidal
positional encodings and GPT-style learned positional embeddings.
Finally, we analyzed the memory bandwidth bottlenecks inherent in large
embedding tables.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-11}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Memory Gather vs.~Matrix Multiply}: Embedding layers execute
  index-based row slicing rather than one-hot matrix multiplications,
  minimizing arithmetic overhead.
\item
  \textbf{Permutation Invariance}: Self-attention is order-agnostic;
  positional encodings are mandatory to distinguish word order in
  natural language.
\item
  \textbf{Sinusoidal Encodings}: Trigonometric functions across multiple
  frequencies provide relative distance linearity and infinite length
  extrapolation.
\item
  \textbf{Learned Positional Embeddings}: GPT architectures optimize a
  dedicated \((S_{\text{max}}, D)\) positional embedding table directly
  via gradient descent.
\item
  \textbf{Memory-Bound Bottlenecks}: Embedding lookups perform zero
  FLOPs and are strictly bounded by DRAM memory bandwidth and cache
  latency.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: The Heart of the
Transformer}\label{chapter-connection-the-heart-of-the-transformer}

Now that our sequences are embedded into position-aware continuous
matrices \((B, S, D)\), how do tokens dynamically interact with each
other to capture context? In \textbf{Chapter 12 (Scaled Dot-Product
Attention and Multi-Head Mechanisms)}, we build the core engine of
modern deep learning: Multi-Head Self-Attention.

\chapter{Scaled Dot-Product Attention and Multi-Head
Mechanisms}\label{scaled-dot-product-attention-and-multi-head-mechanisms}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-13}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why did scaled dot-product attention replace recurrent memory
cells across the entire landscape of artificial intelligence?}

Prior to the transformer, sequence processing relied on Recurrent Neural
Networks (RNNs, LSTMs, and GRUs) that updated a fixed-size hidden state
vector \(\mathbf{h}_t\) sequentially token-by-token. This sequential
dependency created two fatal bottlenecks: computational unrolling
prohibited hardware parallelism across time steps (\(O(S)\) sequential
latency), and sequential backpropagation compressed long-range context
through an information bottleneck, suffering from vanishing gradients.
Attention shatters this paradigm by establishing direct \(O(1)\) routing
connections between every token pair in a sequence, retrieving context
dynamically via \textbf{Query-Key-Value (QKV)} dot products. In a
framework runtime, attention replaces sequential loops with dense,
parallel matrix multiplications, but introduces a new systems challenge:
the pairwise similarity matrix requires \(O(S^2)\) memory and
arithmetic, creating the infamous \textbf{quadratic attention memory
wall}.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-12}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Formulate} the Query-Key-Value information retrieval mechanism
  and derive the variance stabilization scaling factor
  \(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_k}}\).
\item
  \textbf{Implement} \texttt{scaled\_dot\_product\_attention} in pure
  Python, supporting both bidirectional and causal autoregressive
  masking.
\item
  \textbf{Construct} \texttt{MultiHeadAttention} with parallel head
  projection, 4D tensor reshaping \((B, H, S, d_k)\), and linear output
  recombination.
\item
  \textbf{Calculate} the exact memory allocation footprint of
  intermediate attention score matrices across varying sequence lengths
  (\(S = 512 \to 8192\)).
\item
  \textbf{Evaluate} the systems transition from naive materialization of
  \(S \times S\) attention maps to tile-based IO-aware kernel fusion
  (FlashAttention).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Information Bottleneck of
Recurrence}\label{the-information-bottleneck-of-recurrence}

In an RNN, information from token 1 must pass through \(S-1\) recurrent
state transitions to reach token \(S\):

\[\mathbf{h}_t = \tanh(\mathbf{W}_{hh} \mathbf{h}_{t-1} + \mathbf{W}_{xh} \mathbf{x}_t)\]

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Recurrent Neural Network: Sequential Information Bottleneck│
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Token 1 ──> [Cell 1] ──> [Cell 2] ──> ... ──> [Cell S]     │
│                 │            │                    │         │
│                 ▼            ▼                    ▼         │
│             Hidden h1    Hidden h2            Hidden hS     │
│  • Parallelism across time: ZERO (Must wait for h_{t-1})    │
│  • Maximum path length between tokens: O(S)                 │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Self-Attention: Direct Parallel Interconnect               │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Token 1 <──────────────────────────────────────> Token S   │
│  • Parallelism across time: FULL (Matrix multiplications)   │
│  • Maximum path length between tokens: O(1)                 │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

Attention replaces sequential unrolling with direct pairwise dot
products, reducing the maximum path length between any two tokens in a
document from \(O(S)\) to \(O(1)\).

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Query-Key-Value
Paradigm}\label{the-query-key-value-paradigm}

Attention formulates sequence interaction as a differentiable database
retrieval system:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Query (\(\mathbf{Q} \in \mathbb{R}^{S \times d_k}\))}: What
  the current token is searching for.
\item
  \textbf{Key (\(\mathbf{K} \in \mathbb{R}^{S \times d_k}\))}: What each
  candidate token in the sequence contains.
\item
  \textbf{Value (\(\mathbf{V} \in \mathbb{R}^{S \times d_v}\))}: The
  actual semantic payload retrieved if the Query matches the Key.
\end{itemize}

For an input matrix \(\mathbf{X} \in \mathbb{R}^{S \times D}\), the
three matrices are computed via learned linear projections:

\[\mathbf{Q} = \mathbf{X} \mathbf{W}_Q, \quad \mathbf{K} = \mathbf{X} \mathbf{W}_K, \quad \mathbf{V} = \mathbf{X} \mathbf{W}_V\]

where \(\mathbf{W}_Q, \mathbf{W}_K \in \mathbb{R}^{D \times d_k}\) and
\(\mathbf{W}_V \in \mathbb{R}^{D \times d_v}\).

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/12_attention-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-qkv-attention}\textbf{Figure 12.1: Scaled
Dot-Product Attention Mechanism.} Queries and Keys are multiplied to
produce pairwise affinity logits. After scaling and causal masking,
Softmax normalizes affinities into attention weights that linearly blend
Value vectors.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Scaled Dot-Product
Attention}\label{scaled-dot-product-attention}

The attention weights between all token pairs are computed via the
scaled dot-product formula:

\[\text{Attention}(\mathbf{Q}, \mathbf{K}, \mathbf{V}) = \text{softmax}\left( \frac{\mathbf{Q} \mathbf{K}^T}{\sqrt{d_k}} + \mathbf{M} \right) \mathbf{V}\]

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Why the Scale Factor
\(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_k}}\) is Mandatory: Preventing Softmax Gradient
Extinction}{Why the Scale Factor \textbackslash frac\{1\}\{\textbackslash sqrt\{d\_k\}\} is Mandatory: Preventing Softmax Gradient Extinction}}\label{why-the-scale-factor-frac1sqrtd_k-is-mandatory-preventing-softmax-gradient-extinction}

To understand why the scaling factor \(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_k}}\) is
essential for training stability, consider the statistical behavior of
the raw Query-Key dot product.

Assume the components of query vector
\(\mathbf{q} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_k}\) and key vector
\(\mathbf{k} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_k}\) are independent, zero-mean,
unit-variance random variables:

\[\mathbb{E}[q_i] = 0, \quad \text{Var}(q_i) = 1, \quad \mathbb{E}[k_i] = 0, \quad \text{Var}(k_i) = 1\]

The raw scalar dot product
\(z = \mathbf{q}^T \mathbf{k} = \sum_{i=1}^{d_k} q_i k_i\) has
expectation zero:

\[\mathbb{E}[z] = \sum_{i=1}^{d_k} \mathbb{E}[q_i k_i] = \sum_{i=1}^{d_k} \mathbb{E}[q_i]\mathbb{E}[k_i] = 0\]

Because the terms are mutually independent, the variance of the sum is
the sum of the variances:

\[\text{Var}(z) = \sum_{i=1}^{d_k} \text{Var}(q_i k_i) = \sum_{i=1}^{d_k} \left( \mathbb{E}[q_i^2 k_i^2] - (\mathbb{E}[q_i k_i])^2 \right) = \sum_{i=1}^{d_k} (\text{Var}(q_i)\text{Var}(k_i)) = \sum_{i=1}^{d_k} (1 \cdot 1) = d_k\]

The standard deviation of raw dot products grows with the square root of
the head dimension: \(\sigma_z = \sqrt{d_k}\).

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  The Softmax Gradient Saturation Hazard                                 │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Unscaled Attention (d_k = 128):                                        │
│  • Raw dot-product std dev: sigma = sqrt(128) ≈ 11.31                   │
│  • Logit values range from -30 to +30                                   │
│  • Softmax Exponentiation: exp(30) ≈ 1.07 * 10^13 (Argmax Collapse)     │
│  • Output Distribution: [0.0, 0.0, ..., 1.0, ..., 0.0]                  │
│                                                                         │
│  Softmax Jacobian Gradient Flow:                                        │
│  • Winning index (p_i = 1.0): d(p_i) / d(z_i) = p_i * (1 - p_i) = 0.0   │
│  • Losing indices (p_j = 0.0): d(p_j) / d(z_j) = p_j * (1 - p_j) = 0.0  │
│  • RESULT: Gradient is EXACTLY ZERO everywhere! Model learning halts!   │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Scaled Attention (Scaled by 1 / sqrt(d_k)):                            │
│  • Scaled dot-product std dev: sigma = 1.0 (Unit Variance)              │
│  • Logits remain in the sensitive linear dynamic range of Softmax       │
│  • Non-zero gradients propagate smoothly to W_Q and W_K parameter arrays│
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

When unscaled logits are fed into
\(\mathbf{p} = \text{softmax}(\mathbf{z})\), the exponential function
pushes the largest logit to \(p_{\text{max}} \approx 1.0\) and all
competing logits to \(p_j \approx 0.0\). Evaluating the multivariable
Softmax Jacobian:

\[\frac{\partial p_i}{\partial z_j} = p_i (\delta_{ij} - p_j)\]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  When \(i = j = \text{argmax}\):
  \(p_i (1 - p_i) = 1.0 \times (1.0 - 1.0) = 0\).
\item
  When \(i \ne j\): \(p_i (0 - p_j) = 0.0 \times 0.0 = 0\).
\end{itemize}

The gradient vanishes to zero across all entries, permanently
extinguishing backpropagation through the attention projections.
Dividing by \(\sqrt{d_k}\) enforces
\(\text{Var}\left(\frac{\mathbf{q}^T \mathbf{k}}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right) = 1.0\),
guaranteeing stable, non-vanishing gradient highways regardless of head
dimensionality.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Causal Masking: Enforcing the Arrow of
Time}\label{causal-masking-enforcing-the-arrow-of-time}

In autoregressive language generation (GPT), token \(i\) must not attend
to future tokens \(j > i\).

To enforce this causal constraint without disrupting parallel matrix
multiplications, frameworks apply an upper-triangular \textbf{Causal
Mask} \(\mathbf{M} \in \mathbb{R}^{S \times S}\):

\[\mathbf{M}_{ij} = \begin{cases} 0 & \text{if } j \le i \\ -\infty \quad (\text{or } -10^9) & \text{if } j > i \end{cases}\]

When added to the scaled scores before Softmax:

\[\text{softmax}\left( \frac{\mathbf{Q} \mathbf{K}^T}{\sqrt{d_k}} + \mathbf{M} \right)_{ij} = \frac{e^{z_{ij} + M_{ij}}}{\sum_k e^{z_{ik} + M_{ik}}} = \frac{e^{-\infty}}{\sum \dots} = 0 \quad \forall j > i\]

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Causal Attention Mask Matrix M (4 x 4 Sequence)            │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│               Token 0   Token 1   Token 2   Token 3         │
│  Token 0: [      0,       -inf,     -inf,     -inf   ]      │
│  Token 1: [      0,         0,      -inf,     -inf   ]      │
│  Token 2: [      0,         0,        0,      -inf   ]      │
│  Token 3: [      0,         0,        0,        0    ]      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Multi-Head Attention: Parallel
Subspaces}\label{multi-head-attention-parallel-subspaces}

Rather than performing a single attention function with
\(D\)-dimensional keys and values, \textbf{Multi-Head Attention (MHA)}
projects the queries, keys, and values \(H\) times into
lower-dimensional subspaces of dimension \(d_k = D / H\):

\[\text{head}_h = \text{Attention}(\mathbf{Q} \mathbf{W}_Q^{(h)}, \mathbf{K} \mathbf{W}_K^{(h)}, \mathbf{V} \mathbf{W}_V^{(h)})\]

\[\text{MultiHead}(\mathbf{Q}, \mathbf{K}, \mathbf{V}) = \text{Concat}(\text{head}_1, \text{head}_2, \dots, \text{head}_H) \mathbf{W}_O\]

where \(\mathbf{W}_O \in \mathbb{R}^{D \times D}\) linearly recombines
the outputs of all heads.

Multi-head projections allow the network to jointly attend to
information from different representation subspaces at different
positions (e.g., Head 1 tracks grammatical syntax, Head 2 tracks pronoun
reference, and Head 3 tracks punctuation).

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Implementing MultiHeadAttention in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-multiheadattention-in-tinytorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 12.1: MultiHeadAttention Implementation in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Layer, Linear}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.activations }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Softmax}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ List, Optional, Tuple}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ scaled\_dot\_product\_attention(}
\NormalTok{    q: Tensor,}
\NormalTok{    k: Tensor,}
\NormalTok{    v: Tensor,}
\NormalTok{    mask: Optional[Tensor] }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{None}
\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tuple[Tensor, Tensor]:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Compute Scaled Dot{-}Product Attention over 4D tensors (B, H, S, d\_k).}
\CommentTok{    """}
\NormalTok{    d\_k }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ q.shape[}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    scale }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ np.sqrt(d\_k)}

    \CommentTok{\# 1. Compute raw affinity scores: Q @ K\^{}T {-}\textgreater{} (B, H, S, S)}
    \CommentTok{\# Transpose last two dimensions of K}
\NormalTok{    k\_t }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.swapaxes(k.data, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    scores }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.matmul(q.data, k\_t) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ scale}

    \CommentTok{\# 2. Apply causal or padding mask}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ mask }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        scores }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ mask.data}

    \CommentTok{\# 3. Softmax along last dimension to obtain attention probabilities}
\NormalTok{    softmax }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Softmax(dim}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    attn\_weights }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ softmax(Tensor(scores))}

    \CommentTok{\# 4. Multiply by Values: (B, H, S, S) @ (B, H, S, d\_k) {-}\textgreater{} (B, H, S, d\_k)}
\NormalTok{    output }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.matmul(attn\_weights.data, v.data)}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(output), attn\_weights}


\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ MultiHeadAttention(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Multi{-}Head Attention module for parallel subspace representation.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, d\_model: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, num\_heads: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{assert}\NormalTok{ d\_model }\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ num\_heads }\OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"d\_model must be divisible by num\_heads"}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ d\_model}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_heads }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ num\_heads}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_k }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ d\_model }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ num\_heads}

        \CommentTok{\# Linear projections for Q, K, V and output O}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_q }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(d\_model, d\_model)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_k }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(d\_model, d\_model)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_v }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(d\_model, d\_model)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_o }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(d\_model, d\_model)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, q: Tensor, k: Tensor, v: Tensor, mask: Optional[Tensor] }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
\NormalTok{        B, S\_q, \_ }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ q.shape}
\NormalTok{        B, S\_k, \_ }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ k.shape}

        \CommentTok{\# 1. Linear projections: (B, S, D)}
\NormalTok{        proj\_q }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_q(q)}
\NormalTok{        proj\_k }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_k(k)}
\NormalTok{        proj\_v }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_v(v)}

        \CommentTok{\# 2. Split heads and transpose: (B, S, H, d\_k) {-}\textgreater{} (B, H, S, d\_k)}
\NormalTok{        q\_heads }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ proj\_q.data.reshape(B, S\_q, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_heads, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_k).swapaxes(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        k\_heads }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ proj\_k.data.reshape(B, S\_k, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_heads, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_k).swapaxes(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        v\_heads }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ proj\_v.data.reshape(B, S\_k, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_heads, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_k).swapaxes(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}

        \CommentTok{\# 3. Scaled dot{-}product attention}
\NormalTok{        out\_heads, \_ }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ scaled\_dot\_product\_attention(}
\NormalTok{            Tensor(q\_heads), Tensor(k\_heads), Tensor(v\_heads), mask}
\NormalTok{        )}

        \CommentTok{\# 4. Concatenate heads: (B, H, S\_q, d\_k) {-}\textgreater{} (B, S\_q, H * d\_k)}
\NormalTok{        out\_concat }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ out\_heads.data.swapaxes(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{).reshape(B, S\_q, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_model)}

        \CommentTok{\# 5. Final output projection}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_o(Tensor(out\_concat))}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[Tensor]:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ (}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_q.parameters() }\OperatorTok{+}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_k.parameters() }\OperatorTok{+}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_v.parameters() }\OperatorTok{+}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_o.parameters()}
\NormalTok{        )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{PyTorch Systems Bridge:
\texttt{torch.nn.functional.scaled\_dot\_product\_attention} (SDPA)}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

In modern PyTorch (2.0+), production architectures never manually
execute the sequence
\texttt{(Q\ @\ K.T)\ /\ sqrt(d\_k)\ -\textgreater{}\ Softmax\ -\textgreater{}\ @\ V}.
PyTorch provides
\texttt{torch.nn.functional.scaled\_dot\_product\_attention}, which
dynamically selects the optimal hardware kernel at runtime:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch.nn.functional }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ F}

\CommentTok{\# Query, Key, Value tensors: (Batch=2, Heads=8, SeqLen=1024, HeadDim=64) in fp16}
\NormalTok{q }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.randn(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1024}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{torch.float16, device}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"cuda"} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ torch.cuda.is\_available() }\ControlFlowTok{else} \StringTok{"cpu"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{k }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.randn(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1024}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{torch.float16, device}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"cuda"} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ torch.cuda.is\_available() }\ControlFlowTok{else} \StringTok{"cpu"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{v }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.randn(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1024}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{torch.float16, device}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"cuda"} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ torch.cuda.is\_available() }\ControlFlowTok{else} \StringTok{"cpu"}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Fused FlashAttention{-}2 execution: ZERO intermediate S x S allocations in HBM!}
\NormalTok{out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ F.scaled\_dot\_product\_attention(q, k, v, is\_causal}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{PyTorch Kernel Dispatch
Logic}\label{pytorch-kernel-dispatch-logic}

PyTorch's C++ dispatcher evaluates four candidate backends in priority
order:\footnotemark{}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{FlashAttention-2 (\texttt{enable\_flash=True})}: Triggered for
  half-precision (\texttt{float16}, \texttt{bfloat16}) on NVIDIA Ampere,
  Ada Lovelace, and Hopper GPUs (\texttt{sm\_80+}). Delivers up to
  \(72\%\) of peak hardware TFLOPs.
\item
  \textbf{Memory-Efficient Attention
  (\texttt{enable\_mem\_efficient=True})}: Uses CUTLASS templated
  kernels (Rabe \& Staats, 2021; xFormers), supporting arbitrary head
  dimensions and older architectures (Volta/Turing).
\item
  \textbf{cuDNN SDPA (\texttt{enable\_cudnn=True})}: Hardware-specific
  graph fusion in cuDNN 8.9+.
\item
  \textbf{C++ Math Fallback (\texttt{enable\_math=True})}: Materializes
  explicit intermediate matrices; used for CPU execution or unsupported
  data types.
\end{enumerate}

\end{tcolorbox}

\footnotetext{You can enforce or profile specific dispatch backends in
PyTorch using the context manager
\texttt{torch.backends.cuda.sdp\_kernel(enable\_flash=True,\ enable\_mem\_efficient=False,\ enable\_math=False)}.}

\section{Systems Reality: The Quadratic Memory Wall and
FlashAttention-2}\label{systems-reality-the-quadratic-memory-wall-and-flashattention-2}

The intermediate attention score matrix
\(\mathbf{S} = \mathbf{Q}\mathbf{K}^T\) has shape \((B, H, S, S)\).

\begin{verbatim}
