┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Attention Activation Memory Footprint (B=4, H=32, float16: 2 bytes)    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  • Context Length S = 512    : 4 * 32 * 512^2   * 2B = 67.1 MB          │
│  • Context Length S = 2,048  : 4 * 32 * 2048^2  * 2B = 1.07 GB          │
│  • Context Length S = 8,192  : 4 * 32 * 8192^2  * 2B = 17.18 GB         │
│  • Context Length S = 32,768 : 4 * 32 * 32768^2 * 2B = 274.88 GB        │
│  • Context Length S = 131,072: 4 * 32 * 131072^2* 2B = 4.398 TB (OOM!)  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

At context length \(S = 32,768\), storing a single layer's attention map
in half-precision requires \textbf{\(274.88\text{ GB}\) of GPU
memory}---exceeding the entire physical capacity of an NVIDIA H100 GPU
(\(80\text{ GB}\)).

The introduction of \textbf{FlashAttention-2}\footnote{\textbf{FlashAttention-2
  Algorithm (Dao, 2023)}: Standard attention loads
  \(\mathbf{Q}, \mathbf{K}, \mathbf{V}\) from High-Bandwidth Memory
  (HBM), writes the \(S \times S\) attention matrix to HBM, reads it
  back for Softmax, writes normalized probabilities to HBM, and reads
  them again to multiply by \(\mathbf{V}\)---incurring \(O(S^2)\) memory
  traffic. FlashAttention-2 tiles blocks of
  \(\mathbf{Q}, \mathbf{K}, \mathbf{V}\) directly into on-chip
  \textbf{GPU SRAM (\(192\text{ KB}\) per Streaming Multiprocessor)} and
  executes \textbf{Online Softmax} (Milakov \& Gimelshein, 2018):
  \[m_{\text{new}} = \max(m_{\text{prev}}, \max(\mathbf{S}_{\text{tile}})), \quad l_{\text{new}} = e^{m_{\text{prev}} - m_{\text{new}}} l_{\text{prev}} + \sum e^{\mathbf{S}_{\text{tile}} - m_{\text{new}}}\]
  \[\mathbf{O}_{\text{new}} = \text{diag}\left(e^{m_{\text{prev}} - m_{\text{new}}}\right) \mathbf{O}_{\text{prev}} + e^{\mathbf{S}_{\text{tile}} - m_{\text{new}}} \mathbf{V}_{\text{tile}}\]
  By scaling the output accumulator on-the-fly and never materializing
  the \(S \times S\) matrix in HBM, FlashAttention-2 reduces HBM access
  by \(10\times\) and reduces memory complexity from \(O(S^2)\) to
  strictly \(O(S)\).} shattered this memory wall. By fusing Query-Key
dot products, online Softmax rescaling, and Value accumulation into a
single GPU kernel, attention requires strictly \(O(S)\) activation
storage. Combined with modern \textbf{KV Cache} management strategies
(PagedAttention, GQA)\footnote{\textbf{The KV Cache Memory Footprint}:
  During autoregressive text generation, past key and value vectors are
  stored in GPU memory to prevent redundant recomputation. The exact
  byte footprint of the KV cache across \(L\) layers is:
  \[\text{Memory}_{\text{KV}} = 2 \times L \times H_{\text{kv}} \times d_k \times S \times B \times \text{sizeof(dtype)}\]
  For a LLaMA-2 70B model (\(L=80, H_{\text{kv}}=64, d_k=128\),
  \texttt{float16} = 2 bytes) with context \(S=4096\) and batch size
  \(B=16\):
  \[\text{KV Footprint} = 2 \times 80 \times 64 \times 128 \times 4096 \times 16 \times 2 = 171,798,691,840\text{ bytes} \approx 171.8\text{ GB of VRAM!}\]
  This massive memory overhead inspired \textbf{Grouped-Query Attention
  (GQA)}, which shares 1 key-value head across every 8 query heads
  (\(H_{\text{kv}} = 8\)), reducing the KV cache footprint by
  \(8\times\) to \textbf{\(21.5\text{ GB}\)}.}, foundation models can
scale context windows beyond 1,000,000 tokens.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-12}

In this chapter, we constructed the core attention mechanism of modern
deep learning. We examined how direct \(O(1)\) routing overcomes the
sequential information bottleneck of Recurrent Neural Networks.

We derived the variance-stabilizing scaling factor
\(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_k}}\) that prevents Softmax saturation, implemented
causal autoregressive masking to enforce sequential dependencies, and
built \texttt{MultiHeadAttention} with 4D tensor manipulation. Finally,
we quantified the \(O(S^2)\) quadratic memory wall and examined how
modern IO-aware kernels eliminate intermediate matrix allocation.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-12}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Direct Token Routing}: Self-attention replaces \(O(S)\)
  sequential recurrent transitions with direct \(O(1)\) pairwise
  Query-Key-Value interactions.
\item
  \textbf{Variance Stabilization}: Scaling by \(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_k}}\)
  maintains unit variance in dot products, preventing Softmax gradient
  saturation.
\item
  \textbf{Causal Masking}: Adding \(-\infty\) to upper-triangular
  elements enforces the autoregressive property during parallel
  training.
\item
  \textbf{Multi-Head Subspaces}: Splitting hidden dimension \(D\) across
  \(H\) heads allows the model to attend to distinct semantic relations
  simultaneously.
\item
  \textbf{The Quadratic Memory Wall}: The \(O(S^2)\) attention map
  creates severe memory bottlenecks at long context lengths, solved in
  production by fused IO-aware algorithms (FlashAttention).
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Assembling the Full
Transformer}\label{chapter-connection-assembling-the-full-transformer}

With multi-head attention, positional encodings, and linear projections
complete, how do we combine these components into a stable, deep
architecture capable of generating text? In \textbf{Chapter 13 (The
Modern Transformer and Autoregressive GPT)}, we build Layer
Normalization, residual connections, and complete GPT language models.

\chapter{The Modern Transformer and Autoregressive
GPT}\label{the-modern-transformer-and-autoregressive-gpt}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-14}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does stacking attention layers without normalization and
residual highways immediately collapse gradient flow?}

While Multi-Head Attention enables dynamic context retrieval, simply
stacking attention mechanisms on top of each other creates an unstable
dynamical system: variance compounds exponentially across layers, and
deep gradient paths vanish during backpropagation. To construct stable
deep architectures with dozens or hundreds of layers, modern
transformers rely on two structural stabilizers: \textbf{Layer
Normalization} (which rescales feature distributions across the hidden
dimension independently for each token) and \textbf{Residual
Connections} (which provide uninterrupted identity highways for gradient
flow
\(\frac{\partial}{\partial \mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x} + f(\mathbf{x})) = \mathbf{I} + f'(\mathbf{x})\)).
By combining Token Embeddings, Multi-Head Attention, Pre-LayerNorm
Transformer Blocks, and a Linear Language Modeling Head, we construct
the definitive architecture of modern artificial intelligence: the
\textbf{Autoregressive Generative Pre-trained Transformer (GPT)}.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-13}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Construct} \texttt{LayerNorm} layers encapsulating learnable
  \(\gamma\) scale and \(\beta\) shift parameter tensors.
\item
  \textbf{Implement} the Two-Layer Transformer Feed-Forward Network
  (FFN) with \(4\times\) hidden dimension expansion and GELU
  activations.
\item
  \textbf{Assemble} the Pre-LayerNorm \texttt{TransformerBlock} with
  dual residual identity connections.
\item
  \textbf{Build} the complete \texttt{GPT} architecture and execute
  autoregressive text generation with temperature-scaled sampling.
\item
  \textbf{Derive} the closed-form parameter count formula for
  decoder-only transformers
  (\(N_{\text{params}} \approx 12 \times L \times D^2\)).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Pre-LN vs.~Post-LN: Clean Gradient Highways in Deep
Networks}\label{pre-ln-vs.-post-ln-clean-gradient-highways-in-deep-networks}

The original 2017 Transformer (Vaswani et al.) placed Layer
Normalization \emph{after} the residual addition (\textbf{Post-LN}):

\[\mathbf{x}_{l+1} = \text{LayerNorm}(\mathbf{x}_l + \mathcal{F}_l(\mathbf{x}_l))\]

where \(\mathcal{F}_l\) represents the Multi-Head Attention or
Feed-Forward sub-layer.

\subsection{Mathematical Failure Mode of
Post-LN}\label{mathematical-failure-mode-of-post-ln}

Applying the multivariate chain rule across \(L\) stacked Post-LN blocks
requires multiplying the Jacobians of Layer Normalization at every
single layer interface:

\[\frac{\partial \mathbf{x}_L}{\partial \mathbf{x}_0} = \prod_{l=0}^{L-1} \mathbf{J}_{\text{LN}_l} \left( \mathbf{I} + \mathbf{J}_{\mathcal{F}_l} \right)\]

Because \(\mathbf{J}_{\text{LN}}\) rescales activations by empirical
standard deviations, the product of \(L\) non-linear normalization
Jacobians causes gradient magnitudes near the shallow input layers to
vanish exponentially (or explode). Consequently, deep Post-LN
transformers cannot be trained without delicate learning rate warmup
schedules (Liu et al., 2020; Xiong et al., 2020).

\subsection{The Pre-LN Formulation and Clean Identity Gradient
Flow}\label{the-pre-ln-formulation-and-clean-identity-gradient-flow}

Modern foundation models (GPT-2, GPT-3, GPT-4, LLaMA) standardized on
\textbf{Pre-LayerNorm (Pre-LN)}, applying normalization \emph{before}
each sub-layer:

\[\mathbf{x}' = \mathbf{x}_l + \text{Attention}(\text{LayerNorm}(\mathbf{x}_l))\]
\[\mathbf{x}_{l+1} = \mathbf{x}' + \text{FFN}(\text{LayerNorm}(\mathbf{x}'))\]

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  The Pre-LN Transformer Block: Uninterrupted Residual Identity Highway  │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Input x_l ──┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────[+]──> x'│
│              │                                                  ▲       │
│              ▼                                                  │       │
│         [LayerNorm] ──> [Multi-Head Attention (QKV Matmul)] ───┘       │
│                                                                         │
│  x' ─────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────[+]──> x_│
│              │                                                  ▲     l+1
│              ▼                                                  │       │
│         [LayerNorm] ──> [Feed-Forward Network (GELU MLP)] ──────┘       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

Unrolling the recursive Pre-LN recurrence relation from layer \(0\) to
layer \(L\):

\[\mathbf{x}_L = \mathbf{x}_0 + \sum_{l=0}^{L-1} \mathcal{F}_l(\text{LayerNorm}(\mathbf{x}_l))\]

Taking the total derivative with respect to the input embedding
\(\mathbf{x}_0\):

\[\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{x}_0} = \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{x}_L} \frac{\partial \mathbf{x}_L}{\partial \mathbf{x}_0} = \frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{x}_L} \left( \mathbf{I} + \sum_{l=0}^{L-1} \frac{\partial \mathcal{F}_l(\text{LayerNorm}(\mathbf{x}_l))}{\partial \mathbf{x}_0} \right)\]

Notice the isolated identity matrix \(\mathbf{I}\)! Even if the
sub-layer transformations \(\mathcal{F}_l\) have vanishing derivatives,
the identity term \(\mathbf{I}\) guarantees an uninterrupted, unscaled
\textbf{gradient highway} directly from the final loss to the shallowest
input embeddings, enabling training of 100+ layer foundation models
without warmup instabilities.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Layer Normalization: Feature-Wise
Control}\label{layer-normalization-feature-wise-control}

Unlike BatchNorm (which computes mean and variance across the batch
dimension), \textbf{Layer Normalization} normalizes across the feature
dimension \(D\) independently for each individual token:

\[\mu_t = \frac{1}{D} \sum_{i=1}^D x_{t, i}, \quad \sigma_t^2 = \frac{1}{D} \sum_{i=1}^D (x_{t, i} - \mu_t)^2\]

\[\hat{x}_{t, i} = \frac{x_{t, i} - \mu_t}{\sqrt{\sigma_t^2 + \epsilon}} \cdot \gamma_i + \beta_i\]

where \(\mathbf{\gamma}, \mathbf{\beta} \in \mathbb{R}^D\) are learnable
scale and shift parameter vectors.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 13.1: LayerNorm Implementation in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Layer}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ List}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ LayerNorm(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Layer Normalization over the last feature dimension D.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, d\_model: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, eps: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ eps}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.ones(d\_model, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32), requires\_grad}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.zeros(d\_model, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32), requires\_grad}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{\# Mean and variance across the last dimension (dim={-}1)}
\NormalTok{        mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.mean(x.data, axis}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        var }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.var(x.data, axis}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

        \CommentTok{\# Normalize to zero{-}mean, unit{-}variance}
\NormalTok{        x\_norm }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (x.data }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ np.sqrt(var }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps)}

        \CommentTok{\# Apply learnable affine transform}
\NormalTok{        out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x\_norm }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma.data }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta.data}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(out)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[Tensor]:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Transformer Feed-Forward Network
(FFN)}\label{the-transformer-feed-forward-network-ffn}

While attention mixes information \emph{across different token
positions}, the \textbf{Feed-Forward Network (FFN)} processes each token
position \emph{independently} to expand and refine semantic features.

The standard FFN consists of two linear projections with a \(4\times\)
hidden dimension expansion and a \textbf{GELU (Gaussian Error Linear
Unit)} non-linearity:

\[\text{FFN}(\mathbf{x}) = \text{GELU}(\mathbf{x} \mathbf{W}_1 + \mathbf{b}_1) \mathbf{W}_2 + \mathbf{b}_2\]

where \(\mathbf{W}_1 \in \mathbb{R}^{D \times 4D}\) and
\(\mathbf{W}_2 \in \mathbb{R}^{4D \times D}\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 13.2: Transformer Feed{-}Forward Network in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Linear}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.activations }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ GELU}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ FeedForward(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""Transformer Position{-}Wise Feed{-}Forward Network (FFN)."""}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, d\_model: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, d\_ff: Optional[}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ d\_ff }\KeywordTok{is} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            d\_ff }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{4} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ d\_model}

        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w1 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(d\_model, d\_ff)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.act }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ GELU()}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(d\_ff, d\_model)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{\# (B, S, D) {-}\textgreater{} (B, S, 4D) {-}\textgreater{} GELU {-}\textgreater{} (B, S, D)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w2(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.act(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w1(x)))}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[Tensor]:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w1.parameters() }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w2.parameters()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Complete
TransformerBlock}\label{the-complete-transformerblock}

We can now assemble the unified Pre-LN \texttt{TransformerBlock}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 13.3: The TransformerBlock Module in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.attention }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ MultiHeadAttention}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ TransformerBlock(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Standard Pre{-}LN Transformer Block.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, d\_model: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, num\_heads: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ln1 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ LayerNorm(d\_model)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.attn }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ MultiHeadAttention(d\_model, num\_heads)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ln2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ LayerNorm(d\_model)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ffn }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ FeedForward(d\_model)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor, mask: Optional[Tensor] }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{\# 1. Pre{-}LN Attention with Residual Connection}
\NormalTok{        norm\_x }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ln1(x)}
\NormalTok{        attn\_out }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.attn(norm\_x, norm\_x, norm\_x, mask}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{mask)}
\NormalTok{        x\_mid }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(x.data }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ attn\_out.data)}

        \CommentTok{\# 2. Pre{-}LN FFN with Residual Connection}
\NormalTok{        norm\_x\_mid }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ln2(x\_mid)}
\NormalTok{        ffn\_out }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ffn(norm\_x\_mid)}
\NormalTok{        x\_out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(x\_mid.data }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ ffn\_out.data)}

        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ x\_out}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[Tensor]:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ (}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ln1.parameters() }\OperatorTok{+}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.attn.parameters() }\OperatorTok{+}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ln2.parameters() }\OperatorTok{+}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ffn.parameters()}
\NormalTok{        )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Building the Autoregressive GPT
Model}\label{building-the-autoregressive-gpt-model}

The complete decoder-only GPT architecture stacks \(L\) transformer
blocks on top of token and positional embeddings, terminating in a final
\texttt{LayerNorm} and a linear language modeling projection head:

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/13_transformers-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-gpt-architecture}\textbf{Figure 13.1: Full
Autoregressive GPT Architecture.} Token and position embeddings enter a
stack of \(L\) Pre-LN Transformer Blocks. The final hidden states are
projected to vocabulary logits for next-token prediction.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 13.4: Complete GPT Architecture in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.embeddings }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ GPTEmbedding}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ GPT(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Autoregressive Generative Pre{-}trained Transformer (GPT).}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        vocab\_size: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        max\_seq\_len: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{256}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        d\_model: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        num\_heads: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        num\_layers: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{4}
\NormalTok{    ) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ d\_model}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.max\_seq\_len }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ max\_seq\_len}

        \CommentTok{\# 1. Embeddings}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.embedding }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ GPTEmbedding(vocab\_size, max\_seq\_len, d\_model)}

        \CommentTok{\# 2. Stack of L Transformer Blocks}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.blocks }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [TransformerBlock(d\_model, num\_heads) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ \_ }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(num\_layers)]}

        \CommentTok{\# 3. Final LayerNorm}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ln\_f }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ LayerNorm(d\_model)}

        \CommentTok{\# 4. Language Modeling Head (Linear projection to vocabulary logits)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.lm\_head }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(d\_model, vocab\_size)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, input\_ids: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
\NormalTok{        B, S }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ input\_ids.shape}

        \CommentTok{\# 1. Compute position{-}aware embeddings: (B, S, D)}
\NormalTok{        x }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.embedding(input\_ids)}

        \CommentTok{\# 2. Build causal mask for sequence length S}
\NormalTok{        mask\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.where(np.tril(np.ones((S, S))) }\OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{1e9}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{).astype(np.float32)}
\NormalTok{        mask }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(mask\_data[np.newaxis, np.newaxis, :, :])  }\CommentTok{\# Shape: (1, 1, S, S)}

        \CommentTok{\# 3. Pass through transformer block stack}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ block }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.blocks:}
\NormalTok{            x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ block(x, mask}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{mask)}

        \CommentTok{\# 4. Final normalization}
\NormalTok{        x }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ln\_f(x)}

        \CommentTok{\# 5. Project to vocabulary logits: (B, S, V)}
\NormalTok{        logits }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.lm\_head(x)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ logits}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[Tensor]:}
\NormalTok{        params }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.embedding.parameters()}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ b }\KeywordTok{in} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.blocks:}
\NormalTok{            params.extend(b.parameters())}
\NormalTok{        params.extend(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ln\_f.parameters())}
\NormalTok{        params.extend(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.lm\_head.parameters())}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ params}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Autoregressive Text
Generation}\label{autoregressive-text-generation}

To generate text, GPT operates in an autoregressive loop: feeding the
prompt to predict token \(S+1\), appending the predicted token to the
prompt, and repeating:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 13.5: Autoregressive Text Generation Loop}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ generate(}
\NormalTok{    model: GPT,}
\NormalTok{    prompt\_tokens: List[}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{    max\_new\_tokens: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    temperature: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.0}
\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ List[}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{]:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Generate new tokens autoregressively from a prompt.}
\CommentTok{    """}
\NormalTok{    current\_tokens }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{list}\NormalTok{(prompt\_tokens)}

    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ \_ }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(max\_new\_tokens):}
        \CommentTok{\# Crop context to max\_seq\_len if necessary}
\NormalTok{        context }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ current\_tokens[}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{model.max\_seq\_len:]}
\NormalTok{        input\_tensor }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.array([context], dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.int32))}

        \CommentTok{\# Forward pass to get logits: (1, S, V)}
\NormalTok{        logits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ model(input\_tensor)}

        \CommentTok{\# Extract logits for the very last token: (V,)}
\NormalTok{        next\_token\_logits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ logits.data[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, :] }\OperatorTok{/} \BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(temperature, }\FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{)}

        \CommentTok{\# Numerical Softmax to convert logits to probabilities}
\NormalTok{        exp\_logits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.exp(next\_token\_logits }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(next\_token\_logits))}
\NormalTok{        probs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ exp\_logits }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(exp\_logits)}

        \CommentTok{\# Sample next token from probability distribution}
\NormalTok{        next\_token }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{(np.random.choice(}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(probs), p}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{probs))}
\NormalTok{        current\_tokens.append(next\_token)}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ current\_tokens}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{PyTorch Systems Bridge: \texttt{torch.nn.LayerNorm},
\texttt{torch.nn.RMSNorm}, and Kernel Fusion}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

In modern deep learning frameworks, normalization is memory-bandwidth
bound. PyTorch provides both standard \texttt{torch.nn.LayerNorm} and
native \texttt{torch.nn.RMSNorm}:\footnotemark{}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch.nn }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ nn}

\CommentTok{\# Standard LayerNorm (Computes both mean and variance across feature dimension D)}
\NormalTok{ln }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.LayerNorm(normalized\_shape}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{4096}\NormalTok{, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# LLaMA{-}Style Root Mean Square Normalization (RMSNorm)}
\CommentTok{\# Eliminates mean calculation, reducing memory loads and register pressure}
\ControlFlowTok{try}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{    rmsnorm }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.RMSNorm(normalized\_shape}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{4096}\NormalTok{, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{)}
\ControlFlowTok{except} \PreprocessorTok{AttributeError}\NormalTok{:}
    \CommentTok{\# Custom fallback for PyTorch versions prior to native RMSNorm addition}
    \KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ RMSNormFallback(nn.Module):}
        \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, dim: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, eps: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{):}
            \BuiltInTok{super}\NormalTok{().}\FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{()}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ eps}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Parameter(torch.ones(dim))}
        \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: torch.Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ torch.Tensor:}
\NormalTok{            rms }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.rsqrt(x.}\BuiltInTok{pow}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{).mean(}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps)}
            \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ rms }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight}
\NormalTok{    rmsnorm }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ RMSNormFallback(}\DecValTok{4096}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.randn(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{512}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4096}\NormalTok{, device}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"cuda"} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ torch.cuda.is\_available() }\ControlFlowTok{else} \StringTok{"cpu"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{norm\_x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rmsnorm(x)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Fused Pre-LN Residual
Kernels}\label{fused-pre-ln-residual-kernels}

In unoptimized execution, computing
\(\mathbf{x}_{\text{next}} = \mathbf{x} + \text{Sublayer}(\text{Norm}(\mathbf{x}))\)
requires two separate HBM memory passes: reading \(\mathbf{x}\) to write
\(\text{Norm}(\mathbf{x})\), and reading \(\mathbf{x}\) again to add the
residual. High-performance inference engines (such as vLLM,
TensorRT-LLM, and FlashInfer) use \textbf{fused residual-normalization
kernels} written in CUDA or Triton that perform the residual addition
and root-mean-square normalization in a single on-chip SRAM pass,
eliminating \(50\%\) of DRAM bandwidth overhead.\footnotemark{}

\end{tcolorbox}

\footnotetext{Zhang \& Sennrich (2019), \emph{Root Mean Square Layer
Normalization}, NeurIPS. RMSNorm enforces
\(\text{RMS}(\mathbf{x}) = \sqrt{\frac{1}{D}\sum x_i^2 + \epsilon}\),
showing that scaling invariance matters more than mean-centering for
transformer stability.}

\footnotetext{\textbf{The LLaMA Foundation Architecture (Touvron et al.,
2023)}: Modern foundation models (LLaMA-2/3, Mistral, Gemma) synthesize
three major transformer innovations:}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Pre-LN with RMSNorm}: Applied at the input of every attention
  and FFN block, guaranteeing gradient highway stability while
  eliminating mean calculations.
\item
  \textbf{SwiGLU Gated Feed-Forward Networks (Shazeer, 2020)}: Replaces
  standard GELU MLPs with Swish-Gated Linear Units:
  \[\text{SwiGLU}(\mathbf{x}) = \left(\text{SiLU}(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{W}_{\text{gate}}) \odot (\mathbf{x}\mathbf{W}_{\text{up}})\right)\mathbf{W}_{\text{down}}\]
  where hidden dimension is scaled to
  \(d_{\text{ff}} = \frac{8}{3}D \approx 2.67D\), yielding superior
  reasoning capacity per FLOP.
\item
  \textbf{Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE, Su et al., 2021)}: Encodes
  token positions by multiplying 2D query/key coordinate pairs by 2D
  orthogonal rotation matrices, providing continuous relative positional
  decay without learnable lookup parameters.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Systems Reality: Parameter Counting, Memory Allocation, and
Scaling
Laws}\label{systems-reality-parameter-counting-memory-allocation-and-scaling-laws}

How many parameters are in a decoder-only transformer? Let us break down
the exact mathematical accounting for a model with \(L\) layers, hidden
dimension \(D\), vocabulary size \(V\), and standard \(4D\) FFN
expansion:

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Decoder-Only Transformer Parameter Breakdown (Per Block)               │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. Multi-Head Attention Sub-layer:                                     │
│     • Query Projection  W_Q : D x D = D^2 weights                       │
│     • Key Projection    W_K : D x D = D^2 weights                       │
│     • Value Projection  W_V : D x D = D^2 weights                       │
│     • Output Projection W_O : D x D = D^2 weights                       │
│     ──> Subtotal Attention  : 4 D^2 weights                             │
│                                                                         │
│  2. Feed-Forward Network (FFN) Sub-layer:                               │
│     • Up-Projection     W_1 : D x 4D = 4 D^2 weights                    │
│     • Down-Projection   W_2 : 4D x D = 4 D^2 weights                    │
│     ──> Subtotal FFN        : 8 D^2 weights                             │
│                                                                         │
│  TOTAL PER TRANSFORMER BLOCK: 4 D^2 + 8 D^2 = 12 D^2 WEIGHTS!           │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

Across an entire \(L\)-layer network, the total parameter count is:

\[N_{\text{weights}} \approx 12 \times L \times D^2 + V \times D + S_{\text{max}} \times D\]

\section{\texorpdfstring{The 12 \(L D^2\) Scaling Law Rule of
Thumb}{The 12 L D\^{}2 Scaling Law Rule of Thumb}}\label{the-12-l-d2-scaling-law-rule-of-thumb}

In any standard decoder-only transformer, the core transformer stack
contains exactly \textbf{\(12 L D^2\) parameter weights}.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{GPT-2 Small} (\(L=12, D=768\)):
  \(12 \times 12 \times 768^2 = 84.9\text{M params} + 39.3\text{M vocab} \approx 124\text{M params}\).
\item
  \textbf{GPT-3} (\(L=96, D=12288\)):
  \(12 \times 96 \times 12288^2 \approx 174.1\text{ Billion parameters}\)
  (\(+ 614\text{M vocab}\)).
\item
  \textbf{LLaMA-3 8B} (\(L=32, D=4096\), SwiGLU
  \(3 \times \frac{8}{3}D^2 = 8D^2\) FFN):
  \(12 \times 32 \times 4096^2 \approx 6.44\text{B} + 1.05\text{B vocab} \approx 8.0\text{ Billion parameters}\).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-13}

In this chapter, we constructed the complete modern Transformer and
autoregressive GPT language model in TinyTorch. We analyzed why
Pre-LayerNorm and residual identity highways are mathematically required
to stabilize deep gradient propagation.

We built \texttt{LayerNorm}, the \(4\times\) expanding
\texttt{FeedForward} block, the unified \texttt{TransformerBlock}, and
assembled the full \texttt{GPT} model. We implemented autoregressive
generation with temperature sampling and derived the closed-form
\(12 L D^2\) parameter scaling law governing modern Large Language
Models.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-13}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Pre-LayerNorm Stability}: Normalizing inputs before attention
  and FFN sub-layers provides clean residual gradients \(\mathbf{I}\),
  enabling training of deep 100+ layer architectures.
\item
  \textbf{LayerNorm vs.~BatchNorm}: LayerNorm computes statistics across
  the feature dimension \(D\) for each token independently, making it
  invariant to batch size.
\item
  \textbf{FFN Feature Expansion}: The \(4\times\) projection
  (\(D \to 4D \to D\)) accounts for two-thirds of the parameters in each
  transformer block (\(8 D^2\) out of \(12 D^2\)).
\item
  \textbf{Autoregressive Generation}: Text generation iterates in a
  loop, predicting logits for the last token position and sampling the
  next token.
\item
  \textbf{The \(12 L D^2\) Rule}: Transformer parameter scaling is
  governed by \(12 \times L \times D^2\) weights across attention and
  FFN projections.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Validating the Architecture
Revolution}\label{chapter-connection-validating-the-architecture-revolution}

We have now built the two dominant architectural paradigms of modern
deep learning: 2D Convolutional Networks (Chapter 9) and Autoregressive
Transformers (Chapters 10--13). In \textbf{Milestone 2 (The Architecture
Revolution: 1998--2017)}, we validate our framework by training Yann
LeCun's 1998 LeNet-5 on computer vision and Vaswani's 2017 Transformer
on sequence modeling.

\chapter*{Milestone II: The Architecture Revolution
(1998--2017)}\label{milestone-ii-the-architecture-revolution-19982017}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Milestone II: The Architecture Revolution
(1998--2017)}

\markboth{Milestone II: The Architecture Revolution
(1998--2017)}{Milestone II: The Architecture Revolution (1998--2017)}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-15}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does validating both LeNet-5 (1998) and the Transformer (2017)
prove that TinyTorch is a universal deep learning framework?}

The history of modern deep learning is defined by two monumental
architectural revolutions: Yann LeCun's 1998 \textbf{LeNet-5}, which
introduced spatial weight sharing and convolutional feature hierarchies
to conquer computer vision; and Vaswani et al.'s 2017
\textbf{Transformer}, which eliminated recurrence in favor of multi-head
self-attention to conquer natural language processing. In this
milestone, we use the spatial operators from Chapter 9 and the
transformer components from Chapters 10 through 13 to construct, train,
and verify both milestone architectures from scratch in TinyTorch. By
achieving convergence on both vision and language benchmarks, we
mathematically and experimentally prove that our framework is a
universal, production-grade deep learning execution runtime.

\section*{Milestone Objectives}\label{milestone-objectives-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Milestone Objectives}

\markright{Milestone Objectives}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Construct and train} Yann LeCun's 1998 LeNet-5 CNN on
  handwritten digit vision benchmarks using TinyTorch \texttt{Conv2d},
  \texttt{MaxPool2d}, and \texttt{CrossEntropyLoss}.
\item
  \textbf{Demonstrate} that LeNet-5 matches or exceeds fully connected
  MLP accuracy while using 85\% fewer trainable parameters due to
  spatial weight sharing.
\item
  \textbf{Build and train} a complete Generative Pre-trained Transformer
  (GPT) language model from scratch using our Chapter 13 \texttt{GPT}
  module.
\item
  \textbf{Execute} autoregressive text generation, validating that
  TinyTorch successfully captures semantic grammar and generates
  coherent token sequences.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section*{1998: LeCun's LeNet-5 for Computer
Vision}\label{lecuns-lenet-5-for-computer-vision}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{1998: LeCun's LeNet-5 for Computer
Vision}

\markright{1998: LeCun's LeNet-5 for Computer Vision}

In 1998, Yann LeCun, Léon Bottou, Yoshua Bengio, and Patrick Haffner
published \emph{Gradient-Based Learning Applied to Document
Recognition}, introducing \textbf{LeNet-5} to read zip codes on bank
checks for the US Postal Service.

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1998 LeNet-5 Architecture Pipeline                         │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Input Image: (1, 28, 28)                                   │
│       │                                                     │
│       ▼                                                     │
│  Conv2d(1 -> 6, K=5, P=2) ──> ReLU ──> MaxPool2d(2, S=2)   │
│       │ (Feature maps: 6 @ 14x14)                           │
│       ▼                                                     │
│  Conv2d(6 -> 16, K=5, P=0) ──> ReLU ──> MaxPool2d(2, S=2)  │
│       │ (Feature maps: 16 @ 5x5 = 400 features)             │
│       ▼                                                     │
│  Flatten() ──> Linear(400, 120) ──> ReLU                    │
│       │                                                     │
│       ▼                                                     │
│  Linear(120, 84) ──> ReLU ──> Linear(84, 10) ──> Logits    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\subsection*{Implementing LeNet-5 in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-lenet-5-in-tinytorch}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Implementing LeNet-5 in TinyTorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing M2.1: LeNet{-}5 Architecture in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Sequential, Linear, Layer}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.spatial }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Conv2d, MaxPool2d}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.activations }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ ReLU}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.losses }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ CrossEntropyLoss}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.optimizers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ AdamW}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.dataloader }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ TensorDataset, DataLoader}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.training }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Trainer}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Flatten(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""Reshape 4D tensor (B, C, H, W) to 2D matrix (B, C*H*W)."""}
    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
\NormalTok{        B }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(x.data.reshape(B, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}

\NormalTok{lenet5 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Sequential(}
    \CommentTok{\# Layer 1: Conv {-}\textgreater{} ReLU {-}\textgreater{} MaxPool}
\NormalTok{    Conv2d(in\_channels}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, out\_channels}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, kernel\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, padding}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    MaxPool2d(kernel\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, stride}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}

    \CommentTok{\# Layer 2: Conv {-}\textgreater{} ReLU {-}\textgreater{} MaxPool}
\NormalTok{    Conv2d(in\_channels}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, out\_channels}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{16}\NormalTok{, kernel\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, padding}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    MaxPool2d(kernel\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, stride}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}

    \CommentTok{\# Dense Classifier}
\NormalTok{    Flatten(),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{16} \OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{5} \OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{120}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{120}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{84}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    ReLU(),}
\NormalTok{    Linear(in\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{84}\NormalTok{, out\_features}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{)}

\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"LeNet{-}5 assembled with }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(p.data.size }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ p }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ lenet5.parameters())}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ total parameters."}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{2017: Vaswani et al.~and the Transformer
Revolution}\label{vaswani-et-al.-and-the-transformer-revolution}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{2017: Vaswani et al.~and the Transformer
Revolution}

\markright{2017: Vaswani et al.~and the Transformer Revolution}

In 2017, Ashish Vaswani et al.~at Google Brain published \emph{Attention
Is All You Need}, introducing the \textbf{Transformer} architecture. By
replacing recurrent gates with multi-head self-attention, transformers
eliminated sequential bottlenecking and unlocked massive parallel
scaling across high-performance GPU clusters.

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  2017 Generative Pre-trained Transformer Pipeline           │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Prompt String: "TinyTorch is the xv6 of"                   │
│       │ (BPETokenizer)                                      │
│       ▼                                                     │
│  Token IDs: [42, 108, 9, 312, 1801]                         │
│       │ (Token + Learned Positional Embeddings)             │
│       ▼                                                     │
│  Embedded Sequence: (1, 5, 128) in float32                  │
│       │                                                     │
│       ▼                                                     │
│  [TransformerBlock 1: Pre-LN MHA (4 heads) + FFN]           │
│       │                                                     │
│       ▼                                                     │
│  [TransformerBlock 2: Pre-LN MHA (4 heads) + FFN]           │
│       │                                                     │
│       ▼                                                     │
│  Final LayerNorm ──> LM Head Linear(128, Vocab) ──> Logits │
│       │ (Autoregressive Sampling)                           │
│       ▼                                                     │
│  Generated Token: "machine learning systems."               │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\subsection*{Training TinyGPT in
TinyTorch}\label{training-tinygpt-in-tinytorch}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Training TinyGPT in TinyTorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing M2.2: Training TinyGPT in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.transformers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ GPT, generate}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tokenization }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ BPETokenizer}

\CommentTok{\# 1. Prepare training corpus and train tokenizer}
\NormalTok{corpus }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{"tinytorch is a deep learning framework built from scratch for learning machine learning systems."}
\NormalTok{tokenizer }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ BPETokenizer(vocab\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{tokenizer.train(corpus)}

\CommentTok{\# 2. Instantiate TinyGPT Language Model}
\NormalTok{tiny\_gpt }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ GPT(}
\NormalTok{    vocab\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(tokenizer.token\_to\_id),}
\NormalTok{    max\_seq\_len}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{32}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    d\_model}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    num\_heads}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    num\_layers}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# 3. Setup language modeling training loop}
\NormalTok{token\_ids }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tokenizer.encode(corpus)}
\CommentTok{\# Create autoregressive pairs: x = tokens[0:S{-}1], y = tokens[1:S]}
\NormalTok{x\_train }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.array([token\_ids[:}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]], dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.int32))}
\NormalTok{y\_train }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.array(token\_ids[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:], dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.int32))}

\NormalTok{opt\_gpt }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ AdamW(tiny\_gpt.parameters(), lr}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.005}\NormalTok{, weight\_decay}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.01}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{criterion }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ CrossEntropyLoss()}

\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Training TinyGPT on language sequence..."}\NormalTok{)}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ epoch }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{51}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{    opt\_gpt.zero\_grad()}
\NormalTok{    logits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tiny\_gpt(x\_train)  }\CommentTok{\# Shape: (1, S{-}1, Vocab)}

    \CommentTok{\# Flatten logits and targets for cross{-}entropy loss}
\NormalTok{    loss }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ criterion(Tensor(logits.data.reshape(}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, logits.shape[}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])), y\_train)}
\NormalTok{    loss.backward()}
\NormalTok{    opt\_gpt.step()}

    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ epoch }\OperatorTok{\%} \DecValTok{10} \OperatorTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{:}
        \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Epoch }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{epoch}\SpecialCharTok{:02d\}}\SpecialStringTok{ | Language Modeling Loss: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{loss}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{data}\SpecialCharTok{:.4f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# 4. Generate new tokens autoregressively}
\NormalTok{prompt }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tokenizer.encode(}\StringTok{"tinytorch is"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{output\_tokens }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ generate(tiny\_gpt, prompt, max\_new\_tokens}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, temperature}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.7}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Generated Output:"}\NormalTok{, tokenizer.decode(output\_tokens))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Summary}\label{milestone-ii-verification-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Milestone II Verification Summary}

\markright{Milestone II Verification Summary}

\section*{Milestone II Verification
Takeaways}\label{milestone-ii-verification-takeaways}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Milestone II Verification Takeaways}

\markright{Milestone II Verification Takeaways}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{1998 LeNet-5 Verified}: Successfully constructed and trained
  2D convolutional networks with pooling and dense classifier heads,
  proving spatial inductive bias and parameter reduction (\(74\%\) fewer
  weights than dense MLPs).
\item
  \textbf{2017 Transformer Verified}: Built and trained an
  autoregressive Pre-LN GPT model, validating BPE tokenization entropy
  maximization, multi-head self-attention, LayerNorm, FFN projections,
  and text generation.
\item
  \textbf{MLSys Hardware Convergence}: Proved how both vision and
  language models converge onto systolic General Matrix Multiplication
  (GEMM) hardware engines---via \texttt{im2col} for convolutions and
  FlashAttention-2 tiling for transformers.
\item
  \textbf{Universal Architectural Coverage}: TinyTorch has successfully
  reproduced the two fundamental architectures that power all modern
  artificial intelligence.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Entering Part III: Systems Optimization and
Inference}\label{entering-part-iii-systems-optimization-and-inference}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Entering Part III: Systems Optimization
and Inference}

\markright{Entering Part III: Systems Optimization and Inference}

We have built the models, but can they run efficiently in production? In
\textbf{Part III: Systems \& Acceleration (Chapters 14--20)}, we pivot
from model architecture to systems engineering: performance profiling,
low-precision INT8 quantization, model pruning, kernel acceleration, and
KV caching.

\part{Part III: Systems \& Acceleration}

\chapter{Performance Profiling and Bottleneck
Identification}\label{performance-profiling-and-bottleneck-identification}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-16}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does optimizing an operator without measuring its arithmetic
intensity almost always waste engineering effort?}

In machine learning systems engineering, optimizing code based on
intuition is a recipe for wasted effort: accelerating a matrix
multiplication that accounts for only 2\% of runtime or vectorizing an
operator that is strictly memory bandwidth-bound produces zero
observable speedup. To make disciplined, high-impact performance
optimizations, systems engineers rely on empirical measurement
frameworks. Every deep learning operation is physically constrained by
one of two hardware ceilings: \textbf{Memory Bandwidth} (the rate at
which bytes can be transferred from RAM/HBM to compute registers) or
\textbf{Compute Throughput} (the rate at which ALUs and Tensor Cores can
execute arithmetic instructions). By formulating exact parameter counts,
theoretical FLOP calculations, memory allocation tracking, and latency
profiling into a unified \textbf{Roofline Model}, we provide the
diagnostic foundation that guides all subsequent compression and
acceleration techniques in TinyTorch.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-14}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Construct} the \texttt{Profiler} engine in TinyTorch,
  measuring parameter counts, theoretical FLOPs, peak memory allocation,
  and latency percentiles.
\item
  \textbf{Derive} closed-form FLOP equations for Linear projections,
  Conv2d filters, and Multi-Head Attention blocks.
\item
  \textbf{Formulate} the Williams Roofline Model and compute the
  \textbf{Arithmetic Intensity} (\(I = \text{FLOPs} / \text{Byte}\)) for
  foundational operators.
\item
  \textbf{Execute} statistically rigorous latency profiling with warm-up
  cycles, garbage collection isolation, and percentile reporting (P50,
  P90, P99).
\item
  \textbf{Diagnose} whether a bottleneck requires memory reduction
  (quantization, fusion) or arithmetic acceleration (tiling,
  vectorization).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The First Principle of ML Systems: Measure
First}\label{the-first-principle-of-ml-systems-measure-first}

Before modifying a single line of kernel code, we must profile the
baseline workload across four fundamental systems dimensions:

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  The Four Pillars of ML Performance Profiling               │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. Parameters (P) : Storage footprint & static weight RAM  │
│  2. FLOPs (F)      : Total floating-point arithmetic volume │
│  3. Memory (M)     : Dynamic activation peak buffer size    │
│  4. Latency (T)    : Wall-clock execution time & percentiles│
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Parameter Counting and Theoretical FLOP
Formulations}\label{parameter-counting-and-theoretical-flop-formulations}

A \textbf{FLOP (Floating-Point Operation)} represents a single
arithmetic operation (one addition or one multiplication). A fused
multiply-accumulate (\(y \leftarrow y + a \times b\)) counts as
\textbf{2 FLOPs}.

\subsection{Dense Linear Layer}\label{dense-linear-layer}

For \(\mathbf{Y} = \mathbf{X} \mathbf{W} + \mathbf{b}\) with
\(\mathbf{X} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times D_{\text{in}}}\) and
\(\mathbf{W} \in \mathbb{R}^{D_{\text{in}} \times D_{\text{out}}}\):

\[\text{Parameters} = D_{\text{in}} \times D_{\text{out}} + D_{\text{out}}\]

\[\text{FLOPs} = 2 \times B \times D_{\text{in}} \times D_{\text{out}}\]

\subsection{2D Convolutional Layer}\label{d-convolutional-layer}

For input \((B, C_{\text{in}}, H_{\text{in}}, W_{\text{in}})\) and
filters \((C_{\text{out}}, C_{\text{in}}, K_h, K_w)\):

\[\text{Parameters} = C_{\text{out}} \times C_{\text{in}} \times K_h \times K_w + C_{\text{out}}\]

\[\text{FLOPs} = 2 \times B \times C_{\text{out}} \times H_{\text{out}} \times W_{\text{out}} \times (C_{\text{in}} \cdot K_h \cdot K_w)\]

\subsection{Multi-Head Self-Attention
Block}\label{multi-head-self-attention-block}

For sequence length \(S\), model dimension \(D\), and \(H\) heads
(\(d_k = D/H\)):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Q, K, V, O Linear Projections (\(4 \times \text{Linear}\)):
  \(4 \times (2 B S D^2) = 8 B S D^2\text{ FLOPs}\).
\item
  Attention Logits (\(\mathbf{Q} \mathbf{K}^T\)):
  \(2 B H S^2 d_k = 2 B S^2 D\text{ FLOPs}\).
\item
  Value Aggregation (\(\text{Softmax} \times \mathbf{V}\)):
  \(2 B H S^2 d_k = 2 B S^2 D\text{ FLOPs}\).
\end{itemize}

\[\text{Total Attention FLOPs} = 8 B S D^2 + 4 B S^2 D\]

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Williams Roofline Model}\label{the-williams-roofline-model}

The \textbf{Roofline Model} (Williams et al., 2009) defines the maximum
achievable performance of any hardware system as a function of
\textbf{Arithmetic Intensity (\(I\))}:

\[\text{Arithmetic Intensity } I = \frac{\text{Total Arithmetic FLOPs}}{\text{Total DRAM / HBM Bytes Transferred}} \quad \left( \frac{\text{FLOPs}}{\text{Byte}} \right)\]

\[\text{Attainable Performance } P = \min\left( \text{Peak Compute Throughput } P_{\text{peak}}, \quad I \times \text{Peak Memory Bandwidth } BW_{\text{peak}} \right)\]

The intersection point where compute and memory limits meet is the
\textbf{Ridge Point} (\(I_{\text{ridge}}\)):

\[I_{\text{ridge}} = \frac{P_{\text{peak}}\text{ (FLOP/s)}}{BW_{\text{peak}}\text{ (Bytes/s)}}\]

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/14_profiling-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-roofline-model}\textbf{Figure 14.1: The Roofline
Performance Model.} Operators with arithmetic intensity below the ridge
point (such as LayerNorm, ReLU, and single-token generation) are
memory-bound. Batched GEMMs and large convolutions operate in the
compute-bound plateau.}

\end{figure}%

On modern accelerator hardware, the ridge point is exceptionally high.
For example, an NVIDIA H100 SXM5 GPU delivers
\(\sim 1{,}000\text{ TFLOPS}\) (\(10^{15}\text{ FLOP/s}\)) of FP16
Tensor Core compute against \(3.35\text{ TB/s}\) of High-Bandwidth
Memory (HBM3) bandwidth,\footnote{High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM3/HBM3e)
  achieves multi-terabyte-per-second bandwidth (e.g., 3.35 TB/s on
  NVIDIA H100 SXM5, 4.8 TB/s on H200, and 8.0 TB/s on Blackwell B200) by
  vertically stacking DRAM dies with through-silicon vias (TSVs) over an
  ultra-wide 5120-bit interface, compared to standard dual-channel DDR5
  server memory at \textasciitilde50--100 GB/s across a 128-bit bus.}
resulting in a ridge point:

\[I_{\text{ridge}} = \frac{1{,}000 \times 10^{12}\text{ FLOP/s}}{3.35 \times 10^{12}\text{ Bytes/s}} \approx 298.5\text{ FLOPs/Byte}\]

By contrast, on-chip SRAM caches located directly within each Streaming
Multiprocessor (SM) deliver over \(100\text{ TB/s}\) of aggregate
bandwidth at sub-nanosecond latency,\footnote{Modern GPU Streaming
  Multiprocessors (SMs) allocate 128 KB to 228 KB of ultra-fast on-chip
  SRAM configurable as L1 data cache and shared memory. Across 132 SMs
  on an H100 GPU, this represents \textasciitilde30 MB of aggregate
  on-chip SRAM delivering over 100 TB/s of bandwidth with
  \textasciitilde1--2 ns latency, compared to off-chip HBM3 access at
  3.35 TB/s and \textasciitilde50--100 ns latency.} demonstrating why
keeping intermediate data pinned in registers and L1/SRAM is the central
imperative of kernel optimization.

\section{The Diagnostic Rule}\label{the-diagnostic-rule}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{If \(I < I_{\text{ridge}}\) (Memory-Bound)}: Arithmetic
  pipelines spend the majority of clock cycles stalled waiting for data
  transfers over the memory bus. Optimizing arithmetic instructions
  (e.g., vectorization) will yield \textbf{zero speedup}. You must
  reduce memory traffic via \textbf{Operator Fusion} (Chapter 17) or
  \textbf{Quantization} (Chapter 15).
\item
  \textbf{If \(I > I_{\text{ridge}}\) (Compute-Bound)}: Memory transfers
  are completely hidden behind compute latency; you must maximize
  arithmetic throughput via \textbf{Cache Tiling} and \textbf{Systolic
  Matrix Multiplication} (Chapter 17).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Latency Profiling: Warmups and
Percentiles}\label{latency-profiling-warmups-and-percentiles}

Measuring execution time in modern operating systems is fraught with
noise from CPU frequency scaling, thread context switching, and
background garbage collection.

A production-grade profiling protocol enforces three invariants:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Warmup Cycles}: Run 5--10 iterations before measurement to
  populate CPU L1/L2 caches and stabilize processor clock governors.
\item
  \textbf{Garbage Collection Isolation}: Disable automatic Python
  garbage collection (\texttt{gc.disable()}) during timed loops to
  prevent spurious latency spikes.
\item
  \textbf{Statistical Percentiles}: In real-time inference, reporting
  only mean latency hides long-tail tail latency. Frameworks report
  \textbf{P50 (Median)}, \textbf{P90}, and \textbf{P99 (Worst 1\%)}
  latency.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Implementing the Profiler in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-the-profiler-in-tinytorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 14.1: The Profiler Engine in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ time}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ tracemalloc}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ gc}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Dict, Any, List}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ Profiler:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Comprehensive ML systems profiler for parameters, memory, FLOPs, and latency.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.results: Dict[}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{, Any] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{\}}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ count\_parameters(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, model: Any) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Dict[}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{, }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{]:}
        \CommentTok{"""Count total and trainable parameters in model."""}
\NormalTok{        total\_params }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{        trainable\_params }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}

        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ p }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ model.parameters():}
\NormalTok{            num\_elem }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ p.data.size}
\NormalTok{            total\_params }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ num\_elem}
            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ p.requires\_grad:}
\NormalTok{                trainable\_params }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ num\_elem}

        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ \{}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}total\_parameters\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: total\_params,}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}trainable\_parameters\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: trainable\_params,}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}memory\_bytes\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: total\_params }\OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}memory\_mb\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: (total\_params }\OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1024} \OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{1024}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        \}}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ benchmark\_latency(}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        func: Any,}
        \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{args: Any,}
\NormalTok{        warmup: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        runs: }\BuiltInTok{int} \OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{50}
\NormalTok{    ) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Dict[}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{, }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{]:}
        \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{        Measure execution latency percentiles with warmup and GC isolation.}
\CommentTok{        """}
        \CommentTok{\# 1. Warmup cycles to populate caches}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ \_ }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(warmup):}
\NormalTok{            func(}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{args)}

\NormalTok{        latencies }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
\NormalTok{        gc\_was\_enabled }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ gc.isenabled()}
\NormalTok{        gc.disable()}

        \ControlFlowTok{try}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ \_ }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(runs):}
\NormalTok{                t0 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ time.perf\_counter()}
\NormalTok{                func(}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{args)}
\NormalTok{                t1 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ time.perf\_counter()}
\NormalTok{                latencies.append((t1 }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ t0) }\OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{1000.0}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{\# Milliseconds}
        \ControlFlowTok{finally}\NormalTok{:}
            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ gc\_was\_enabled:}
\NormalTok{                gc.enable()}

\NormalTok{        latencies\_sorted }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.sort(latencies)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ \{}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mean\_ms\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(np.mean(latencies)),}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}std\_ms\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(np.std(latencies)),}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}p50\_ms\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(np.percentile(latencies\_sorted, }\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{)),}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}p90\_ms\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(np.percentile(latencies\_sorted, }\DecValTok{90}\NormalTok{)),}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}p99\_ms\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(np.percentile(latencies\_sorted, }\DecValTok{99}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{        \}}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ measure\_peak\_memory(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, func: Any, }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{args: Any) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Measure peak dynamic memory allocated during execution (MB)."""}
\NormalTok{        tracemalloc.start()}
\NormalTok{        func(}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{args)}
\NormalTok{        current, peak }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tracemalloc.get\_traced\_memory()}
\NormalTok{        tracemalloc.stop()}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ peak }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1024.0} \OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{1024.0}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Profiling a Transformer in
TinyTorch}\label{profiling-a-transformer-in-tinytorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 14.2: Profiling TinyGPT Performance}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.transformers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ GPT}

\NormalTok{gpt }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ GPT(vocab\_size}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, max\_seq\_len}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{, d\_model}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{, num\_heads}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, num\_layers}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{profiler }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Profiler()}

\CommentTok{\# 1. Profile parameters}
\NormalTok{param\_stats }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ profiler.count\_parameters(gpt)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Total Parameters: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{param\_stats[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}total\_parameters\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]}\SpecialCharTok{:,\}}\SpecialStringTok{ (}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{param\_stats[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}memory\_mb\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]}\SpecialCharTok{:.2f\}}\SpecialStringTok{ MB)"}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# 2. Profile latency on batch of shape (4, 64)}
\NormalTok{dummy\_input }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.random.randint(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, size}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{64}\NormalTok{), dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.int32))}
\NormalTok{latency\_stats }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ profiler.benchmark\_latency(gpt, dummy\_input)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Forward Latency: P50 = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{latency\_stats[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}p50\_ms\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]}\SpecialCharTok{:.2f\}}\SpecialStringTok{ ms | P99 = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{latency\_stats[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}p99\_ms\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]}\SpecialCharTok{:.2f\}}\SpecialStringTok{ ms"}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# 3. Peak memory}
\NormalTok{peak\_mem }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ profiler.measure\_peak\_memory(gpt, dummy\_input)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Peak Dynamic Memory Allocation: }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{peak\_mem}\SpecialCharTok{:.2f\}}\SpecialStringTok{ MB"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{PyTorch Systems Bridge: \texttt{torch.profiler} and Graph-Level Cost
Models}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

In production PyTorch pipelines, performance diagnostics are performed
via \texttt{torch.profiler} and the Kineto tracing engine:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ torch.profiler }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ profile, record\_function, ProfilerActivity}

\ControlFlowTok{with}\NormalTok{ profile(}
\NormalTok{    activities}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ProfilerActivity.CPU, ProfilerActivity.CUDA],}
\NormalTok{    record\_shapes}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    profile\_memory}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    with\_flops}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}
\NormalTok{) }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ prof:}
    \ControlFlowTok{with}\NormalTok{ record\_function(}\StringTok{"model\_inference"}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{        model(inputs)}

\CommentTok{\# Export visual timeline trace for Chrome / Perfetto inspection}
\NormalTok{prof.export\_chrome\_trace(}\StringTok{"timeline\_trace.json"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(prof.key\_averages().table(sort\_by}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"cuda\_time\_total"}\NormalTok{, row\_limit}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Under the hood, modern compilers like \textbf{PyTorch 2.0
\texttt{torch.compile} (TorchInductor)} evaluate the Roofline arithmetic
intensity of captured FX graph subgraphs. When an operator chain has low
arithmetic intensity (\(I < I_{\text{ridge}}\)), TorchInductor
automatically fuses the sequence into a unified OpenAI Triton GPU
kernel, eliminating intermediate HBM reads and writes.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-14}

In this chapter, we constructed the diagnostic profiling engine of
TinyTorch. We examined why performance optimization must be grounded in
empirical data, deriving exact mathematical formulas for parameter
storage and theoretical FLOP counts.

We formulated the Williams Roofline Model, defining the critical
boundary between memory-bound and compute-bound operators using
Arithmetic Intensity. We implemented the complete \texttt{Profiler}
engine with latency percentiles and memory allocation tracking,
equipping ourselves with the measurement tools required to guide the
systems optimizations in the upcoming chapters.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-14}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The Roofline Principle}: Operators are either bounded by
  DRAM/HBM memory bandwidth or ALU compute throughput; arithmetic
  intensity (\(I = \text{FLOPs}/\text{Byte}\)) identifies the
  bottleneck.
\item
  \textbf{FLOP Accounting}: Standard Linear projections execute
  \(2 B D_{\text{in}} D_{\text{out}}\) FLOPs; Multi-Head Attention
  executes \(8 B S D^2 + 4 B S^2 D\) FLOPs.
\item
  \textbf{Latency Rigor}: Reliable benchmarking requires cache warmup
  passes, disabling Python garbage collection during timed runs, and
  reporting percentile metrics (P50, P90, P99).
\item
  \textbf{Targeted Optimization}: Memory-bound operators require memory
  traffic reduction (quantization, fusion); compute-bound operators
  require hardware parallelism and cache tiling.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Shrinking Models with Low
Precision}\label{chapter-connection-shrinking-models-with-low-precision}

Our profiler reveals that parameter storage and memory transfers
dominate inference latency for memory-bound workloads. How do we reduce
memory traffic by 4× without retraining our models from scratch? In
\textbf{Chapter 15 (Quantization: Low-Precision Integer Arithmetic)}, we
build INT8 affine and symmetric quantization engines.

\chapter{Low-Precision Integer Arithmetic and
Quantization}\label{low-precision-integer-arithmetic-and-quantization}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-17}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does storing parameters as 32-bit floating-point numbers
represent a 4x waste of memory bandwidth in production inference?}

Deep neural networks require 32-bit floating-point precision (IEEE 754
float32) during training because optimization algorithms must accumulate
infinitesimal parameter updates
(\(\Delta \theta = -\eta \mathbf{g} \approx 10^{-5}\)) across millions
of steps without underflowing. Once training concludes, however, the
model's weight matrices occupy static, bounded dynamic ranges. Running
inference in float32 forces memory buses to transfer 4 bytes for every
weight, throttling memory-bound workloads (such as autoregressive LLM
decoding) at 25\% of theoretical memory throughput. \textbf{INT8
Quantization} maps continuous 32-bit floating-point numbers to discrete
8-bit signed integers (\([-128, 127]\)), reducing model memory
footprints by \textbf{75\% (4× compression)} and enabling integer
systolic arrays to execute matrix arithmetic at 2× to 4× higher
throughput with negligible accuracy degradation (\(<0.5\%\)).

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-15}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Formulate} the mathematical transformations for Symmetric and
  Affine (Asymmetric) INT8 quantization, deriving the scale factor \(S\)
  and zero-point \(Z\).
\item
  \textbf{Implement} \texttt{quantize\_int8} and
  \texttt{dequantize\_int8} in pure Python, enforcing numerical clamping
  and rounding invariants.
\item
  \textbf{Construct} the \texttt{QuantizedLinear} layer, replacing
  32-bit float weight storage with 8-bit integer buffers.
\item
  \textbf{Analyze} the memory bandwidth reduction achieved by INT8
  quantization across memory-bound inference workloads.
\item
  \textbf{Contrast} Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) calibration methods
  against Quantization-Aware Training (QAT) using the Straight-Through
  Estimator (STE).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Numerical Redundancy of 32-Bit
Weights}\label{the-numerical-redundancy-of-32-bit-weights}

A standard IEEE 754 single-precision float (\texttt{float32}) consumes
32 bits (4 bytes) per value:

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  IEEE 754 Float32 Bit Layout (4 Bytes per Parameter)        │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  [ Sign: 1 bit ] [ Exponent: 8 bits ] [ Mantissa: 23 bits ] │
│  • Dynamic Range: ~ 10^(-38) to 10^(+38)                    │
│  • Resolution: 16.7 Million discrete values per decade      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

Trained neural network weights do not span 76 orders of magnitude; they
typically occupy a narrow normal distribution bounded between
\([-2.0, +2.0]\). Using 32 bits to represent this compact interval
wastes substantial memory capacity and bus bandwidth.

By mapping this interval to signed 8-bit integers (\texttt{int8}, 1 byte
per parameter), we represent values within \([-128, 127]\):

\[\text{Memory Reduction} = \frac{4\text{ bytes (float32)} - 1\text{ byte (int8)}}{4\text{ bytes (float32)}} = 75.0\% \quad (4\times\text{ Compression})\]

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Mathematics of
Quantization}\label{the-mathematics-of-quantization}

Quantization is a linear mapping between continuous real numbers
\(x \in \mathbb{R}\) and discrete integers
\(q \in [\text{qmin}, \text{qmax}]\):

\[q = \text{clip}\left( \left\lfloor \frac{x}{S} \right\rceil + Z, \quad \text{qmin}, \quad \text{qmax} \right)\]

\[\hat{x} = S \times (q - Z)\]

where:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(S \in \mathbb{R}^+\) is the positive floating-point \textbf{Scale
  Factor} (the real value represented by a single integer step).
\item
  \(Z \in \mathbb{Z}\) is the integer \textbf{Zero-Point} (the integer
  value corresponding to real value \(0.0\)).
\item
  \(\lfloor \cdot \rceil\) represents rounding to the nearest integer.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/15_quantization-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-quant-mapping}\textbf{Figure 15.1: INT8 Affine
Quantization Mapping.} Continuous floating-point weights spanning
\([x_{\min}, x_{\max}]\) are uniformly mapped onto discrete integer bins
\([-128, 127]\) with zero-point alignment.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Symmetric vs.~Asymmetric (Affine)
Quantization}\label{symmetric-vs.-asymmetric-affine-quantization}

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
