│  1. Symmetric Quantization (Zero-Point Z = 0)               │
│  • Scale: S = max(|x_min|, |x_max|) / 127.0                 │
│  • Mapping: q = clip(round(x / S), -128, 127)               │
│  • Dequantize: x_hat = S * q                                │
│  • Hardware Benefit: Zero-point cancels in matrix multiply! │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  2. Asymmetric / Affine Quantization (Arbitrary Z)          │
│  • Scale: S = (x_max - x_min) / 255.0                       │
│  • Zero-Point: Z = round(-x_min / S) - 128                  │
│  • Mapping: q = clip(round(x / S) + Z, -128, 127)           │
│  • Benefit: Optimal for non-negative activations (ReLU).    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Implementing INT8 Quantization in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-int8-quantization-in-tinytorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 15.1: Pure Python INT8 Quantization Functions}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tuple}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ quantize\_symmetric\_int8(x: np.ndarray) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tuple[np.ndarray, }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{]:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Quantize float32 array to symmetric int8 array with Z = 0.}
\CommentTok{    """}
\NormalTok{    max\_val }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(np.}\BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(x))}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ max\_val }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{1e{-}8}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        scale }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.0}
    \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        scale }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(max\_val }\OperatorTok{/} \FloatTok{127.0}\NormalTok{)}

    \CommentTok{\# Scale, round, and clamp to [{-}128, 127]}
\NormalTok{    q }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.clip(np.}\BuiltInTok{round}\NormalTok{(x }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ scale), }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{128}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{127}\NormalTok{).astype(np.int8)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ q, scale}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ dequantize\_symmetric\_int8(q: np.ndarray, scale: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ np.ndarray:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Dequantize int8 array back to float32 approximation.}
\CommentTok{    """}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ (q.astype(np.float32) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ scale).astype(np.float32)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The QuantizedLinear Layer: 4x Memory
Compression}\label{the-quantizedlinear-layer-4x-memory-compression}

In TinyTorch, the \texttt{QuantizedLinear} layer stores weights
exclusively as 8-bit integers (\texttt{np.int8}), keeping only a
lightweight scalar scale factor in memory:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 15.2: The QuantizedLinear Layer in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Layer, Linear}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ List}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ QuantizedLinear(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Linear layer with INT8 quantized weights (4x memory reduction).}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, float\_layer: Linear) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.in\_features }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ float\_layer.weight.shape[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.out\_features }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ float\_layer.weight.shape[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}

        \CommentTok{\# 1. Quantize float32 weights to INT8}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight\_int8, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight\_scale }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ quantize\_symmetric\_int8(float\_layer.weight.data)}

        \CommentTok{\# 2. Keep bias in float32 (bias is tiny: out\_features \textless{}\textless{} in\_features * out\_features)}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ float\_layer.bias }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias\_data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ float\_layer.bias.data.copy()}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias\_data }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{None}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{        Execute forward pass using dequantized weight matrix.}
\CommentTok{        """}
        \CommentTok{\# Dequantize weights on{-}the{-}fly: (D\_in, D\_out)}
\NormalTok{        w\_float }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ dequantize\_symmetric\_int8(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight\_int8, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight\_scale)}

\NormalTok{        out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.matmul(x.data, w\_float)}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias\_data }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            out }\OperatorTok{+=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias\_data}

        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(out)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ memory\_footprint\_bytes(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Return exact memory consumed by layer weights in RAM."""}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight\_int8.nbytes }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias\_data.nbytes }\ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias\_data }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None} \ControlFlowTok{else} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Quantizing an Entire
Model}\label{quantizing-an-entire-model}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 15.3: Model Quantization and Memory Verification}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ quantize\_model(model: Sequential) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Sequential:}
    \CommentTok{"""Replace all Linear layers in a Sequential model with QuantizedLinear."""}
\NormalTok{    quantized\_layers }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ layer }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ model.layers:}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \BuiltInTok{isinstance}\NormalTok{(layer, Linear):}
\NormalTok{            quantized\_layers.append(QuantizedLinear(layer))}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            quantized\_layers.append(layer)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Sequential(}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{quantized\_layers)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{PTQ vs.~Quantization-Aware Training
(QAT)}\label{ptq-vs.-quantization-aware-training-qat}

How do we minimize accuracy loss when moving to low-precision integer
arithmetic?

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1. Post-Training Quantization (PTQ)                        │
│  • Workflow: Train in FP32 ──> Calibrate on data ──> INT8   │
│  • Overhead: Zero retraining compute.                       │
│  • Best For: Large models where INT8 causes <0.5% drop.     │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  2. Quantization-Aware Training (QAT)                       │
│  • Workflow: Model simulates INT8 rounding during training! │
│  • The Non-Differentiable Rounding Problem:                 │
│    Derivative of round(x) is ZERO everywhere!               │
│  • The Straight-Through Estimator (STE) Solution:           │
│    Forward: q = round(x / S) * S                            │
│    Backward: dL/dx = dL/dq  (Treat rounding as identity I!) │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Systems Reality: Integer Systolic Arrays and DP4A
Instructions}\label{systems-reality-integer-systolic-arrays-and-dp4a-instructions}

Why do INT8 Tensor Cores achieve up to \textbf{4× higher throughput}
than FP32 floating-point units?

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Quadratic Silicon Area \& Energy Scaling}: Digital multiplier
  circuit area scales quadratically with bitwidth \(O(b^2)\). An 8-bit
  integer multiplier requires \(\sim 16\times\) fewer logic gates and
  consumes \(\sim 20\times\) less dynamic energy (\(0.2\text{ pJ}\)
  vs.~\(4.0\text{ pJ}\)) than a 32-bit floating-point multiplier,
  allowing chip designers to pack 4× more integer compute units into the
  same thermal envelope.
\item
  \textbf{SIMD Vector Packing \& DP4A Instructions}: On CPUs, a 512-bit
  vector register packs \textbf{64 INT8 values} compared to only 16 FP32
  values. On NVIDIA GPUs, dedicated \textbf{DP4A} (Dot Product of 4
  8-bit elements with 32-bit Accumulation)\footnote{The NVIDIA
    \texttt{dp4a} hardware instruction computes a 4-element 8-bit
    integer vector dot product with 32-bit integer accumulation in a
    single instruction cycle:
    \(\text{acc} \leftarrow \text{acc} + \sum_{i=0}^3 a_i \cdot b_i\).
    In Tensor Core architectures (Volta through Blackwell), this concept
    is expanded into Matrix Multiply-Accumulate (\texttt{mma.sync})
    instructions executing dense integer matrix tiles directly within
    warp registers.} instructions compute 4-element dot products in a
  single clock cycle.
\item
  \textbf{Systolic Tensor Core Matrix Multiplication}: In Tensor Core
  architectures, INT8 matrix multiply-accumulate (\texttt{mma.sync})
  instructions execute \(16 \times 16 \times 32\) integer matrix tiles
  per warp per cycle, delivering double the math throughput of FP16
  Tensor Cores and 4× the throughput of FP32 ALUs.
\item
  \textbf{Memory Bandwidth Relief}: In memory-bound regimes (such as
  batch-1 LLM generation), memory bus throughput is the absolute
  bottleneck. Transferring 1 byte per weight instead of 4 bytes delivers
  an immediate \textbf{4× speedup in token generation
  throughput}.\footnote{In memory-bandwidth-bound inference regimes
    (such as batch-1 autoregressive decoding with
    \(I \ll I_{\text{ridge}}\)), latency is directly proportional to the
    total bytes transferred over the memory bus:
    \(T = \text{Bytes} / BW_{\text{peak}}\). By reducing parameter
    precision from 4 bytes (FP32) to 1 byte (INT8), quantization cuts
    total HBM3 bus transfers by \(75\%\), delivering a theoretical
    \(4\times\) throughput speedup on memory-bound workloads.}
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{PyTorch Systems Bridge: \texttt{torch.ao.quantization} and NVIDIA
TensorRT}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

In production systems, quantization is automated through compiler
frameworks:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch.ao.quantization }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ quantization}

\CommentTok{\# Post{-}Training Static Quantization in PyTorch}
\NormalTok{model.}\BuiltInTok{eval}\NormalTok{()}
\NormalTok{model.qconfig }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ quantization.get\_default\_qconfig(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}fbgemm\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{\# FBGEMM for x86 CPUs}
\NormalTok{prepared\_model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ quantization.prepare(model)}

\CommentTok{\# Calibrate on representative dataset batch}
\ControlFlowTok{with}\NormalTok{ torch.no\_grad():}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ data, \_ }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ calibration\_loader:}
\NormalTok{        prepared\_model(data)}

\NormalTok{quantized\_model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ quantization.convert(prepared\_model)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

In high-performance GPU deployments, \textbf{NVIDIA TensorRT} and
\textbf{TensorRT-LLM} perform automated INT8 and FP8 (E4M3/E5M2)
calibration using KL-divergence minimization to find the optimal scale
factor \(S\). TensorRT then compiles the model into fused INT8 Tensor
Core execution graphs with zero runtime dequantization overhead.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-15}

In this chapter, we constructed the INT8 quantization engine of
TinyTorch. We analyzed the numerical distribution of trained weights,
proving why 32-bit floats contain significant precision redundancy for
inference.

We derived the mathematical equations for Symmetric and Affine
quantization, implemented \texttt{quantize\_symmetric\_int8}, and built
the \texttt{QuantizedLinear} layer to compress model parameter storage
by exactly 75\%. Finally, we examined Post-Training Quantization
calibration, hardware DP4A instructions, and integer systolic arrays on
modern accelerator architectures.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-15}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{4x Memory Compression}: INT8 quantization reduces parameter
  storage and memory bus traffic by 75\%
  (\(4\text{ bytes} \to 1\text{ byte}\) per weight).
\item
  \textbf{Symmetric vs.~Affine}: Symmetric quantization sets \(Z = 0\),
  simplifying hardware matrix arithmetic; Affine quantization handles
  asymmetric activation distributions.
\item
  \textbf{The Rounding Gradient Barrier}: Rounding has zero derivative;
  Quantization-Aware Training resolves this using the Straight-Through
  Estimator (STE).
\item
  \textbf{Inference Speedup}: For memory-bound workloads (such as LLM
  generation), reducing memory bandwidth consumption by 4× directly
  translates to a 4× speedup in generation latency.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Zeroing Redundant
Weights}\label{chapter-connection-zeroing-redundant-weights}

Quantization shrinks the precision of every weight from 32 bits to 8
bits. Can we go further and eliminate entire weights and neurons
altogether? In \textbf{Chapter 16 (Model Compression: Pruning and
Distillation)}, we explore magnitude weight pruning and student-teacher
knowledge distillation.

\chapter{Model Compression: Pruning and
Distillation}\label{model-compression-pruning-and-distillation}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-18}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does setting 90\% of a weight matrix to zero fail to produce a
10x speedup on modern GPUs?}

Trained deep neural networks are drastically overparameterized:
thousands of weight parameters can be zeroed out without degrading final
model accuracy. However, in systems engineering, not all sparsity is
created equal. Setting individual scalar weights to zero
(\textbf{Unstructured Pruning}) creates irregular, scattered sparse
matrices: while theoretical FLOP counts drop by 90\%, modern GPU
systolic arrays---which depend on dense, coalesced memory reads across
32-thread warps---experience branch divergence and memory bus stalls,
often running \emph{slower} than dense models. To achieve actual
hardware speedups, frameworks use \textbf{Structured Pruning} (which
removes entire rows, columns, or convolution channels, physically
shrinking matrix dimensions) and \textbf{Knowledge Distillation} (which
transfers dark knowledge from large teacher ensembles into compact
student models).

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-16}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Implement} unstructured magnitude pruning with binary sparsity
  masks (\(\mathbf{W}_{\text{pruned}} = \mathbf{W} \odot \mathbf{M}\)).
\item
  \textbf{Construct} structured neuron and channel pruning that
  physically reshapes weight tensors, reducing GEMM dimensions.
\item
  \textbf{Analyze} the hardware execution disparity between unstructured
  random sparsity and structured dense tensor execution.
\item
  \textbf{Build} the Knowledge Distillation loss function, combining
  temperature-scaled soft targets (\(D_{\text{KL}}\)) with hard
  cross-entropy ground truth.
\item
  \textbf{Decompose} dense weight matrices into Low-Rank factorized
  projections (\(\mathbf{W} \approx \mathbf{A} \mathbf{B}^T\)).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Overparameterization and the Lottery Ticket
Hypothesis}\label{overparameterization-and-the-lottery-ticket-hypothesis}

Why can we remove 50\% to 90\% of parameters from a trained model?

In 2018, Frankle and Carbin proposed the \textbf{Lottery Ticket
Hypothesis}: dense, randomly initialized feed-forward networks contain
sparse sub-networks (``winning lottery tickets'') that, when trained in
isolation, match the test accuracy of the original dense network in
comparable training iterations.

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  The Model Compression Landscape                            │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. Unstructured Pruning: Removes individual weights.       │
│     • Fine-grained, preserves high accuracy.                │
│     • Requires sparse matrix libraries (CSR/COO) for speed. │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  2. Structured Pruning: Removes entire channels or neurons. │
│     • Coarse-grained, shrinks physical tensor dimensions.   │
│     • Runs at 100% efficiency on standard dense BLAS/GEMM.  │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  3. Knowledge Distillation: Trains smaller student models.  │
│     • Learns from soft probability distributions.           │
│     • Unlocks architecture redesign (e.g., 12 layers -> 4). │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Unstructured Magnitude
Pruning}\label{unstructured-magnitude-pruning}

In magnitude pruning, we compute the absolute magnitude \(|w_{ij}|\) of
all weights and zero out the lowest \(k\) percentile:

\[\text{Threshold } \tau = \text{Percentile}(|\mathbf{W}|, \text{sparsity}\times 100)\]

\[\mathbf{M}_{ij} = \begin{cases} 1 & \text{if } |w_{ij}| \ge \tau \\ 0 & \text{if } |w_{ij}| < \tau \end{cases}\]

\[\mathbf{W}_{\text{pruned}} = \mathbf{W} \odot \mathbf{M}\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 16.1: Magnitude Pruning in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Linear}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Any}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ magnitude\_prune\_layer(layer: Linear, target\_sparsity: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ np.ndarray:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Apply unstructured magnitude pruning to a Linear layer.}
\CommentTok{    Returns the binary sparsity mask.}
\CommentTok{    """}
    \ControlFlowTok{assert} \FloatTok{0.0} \OperatorTok{\textless{}=}\NormalTok{ target\_sparsity }\OperatorTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{1.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Sparsity must be between 0 and 1"}

\NormalTok{    weights }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ layer.weight.data}
\NormalTok{    abs\_weights }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(weights)}

    \CommentTok{\# Determine magnitude threshold}
\NormalTok{    threshold }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.percentile(abs\_weights, target\_sparsity }\OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{100.0}\NormalTok{)}

    \CommentTok{\# Generate binary mask}
\NormalTok{    mask }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (abs\_weights }\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ threshold).astype(np.float32)}

    \CommentTok{\# Apply mask in{-}place}
\NormalTok{    layer.weight.data }\OperatorTok{*=}\NormalTok{ mask}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ mask}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ measure\_sparsity(model: Any) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{:}
    \CommentTok{"""Compute overall fraction of zero weights across all parameters."""}
\NormalTok{    total\_zeros }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{    total\_elements }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{0}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ p }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ model.parameters():}
\NormalTok{        total\_zeros }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(p.data }\OperatorTok{==} \FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        total\_elements }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ p.data.size}
    \ControlFlowTok{return} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(total\_zeros }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ total\_elements)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/16_compression-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-pruning-types}\textbf{Figure 16.1: Unstructured
vs.~Structured Pruning.} Unstructured pruning creates scattered zero
elements in the matrix. Structured pruning removes complete rows and
columns, physically shrinking matrix dimensions.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Structured Pruning: Physically Shrinking
Layers}\label{structured-pruning-physically-shrinking-layers}

Structured pruning removes entire output neurons from a \texttt{Linear}
layer or feature channels from a \texttt{Conv2d} layer.

For a weight matrix
\(\mathbf{W} \in \mathbb{R}^{D_{\text{in}} \times D_{\text{out}}}\), we
rank each neuron \(j \in [0, D_{\text{out}}-1]\) by its \(L_2\) norm:

\[S_j = \|\mathbf{W}_{:, j}\|_2 = \sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{D_{\text{in}}} W_{i, j}^2}\]

We retain only the top
\((1 - \text{prune\_ratio}) \times D_{\text{out}}\) neurons and
construct a new, physically smaller \texttt{Linear} layer:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 16.2: Structured Neuron Pruning in TinyTorch}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ structured\_prune\_linear(layer: Linear, prune\_ratio: }\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Linear:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Prune neurons with smallest L2 norm, physically reducing layer dimensions.}
\CommentTok{    """}
\NormalTok{    in\_features, out\_features }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ layer.weight.shape}
\NormalTok{    num\_keep }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{(out\_features }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ prune\_ratio))}

    \CommentTok{\# Compute L2 norm for each output neuron column}
\NormalTok{    neuron\_norms }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linalg.norm(layer.weight.data, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}

    \CommentTok{\# Find indices of top neurons}
\NormalTok{    keep\_indices }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.argsort(neuron\_norms)[}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{num\_keep:]}
\NormalTok{    keep\_indices }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.sort(keep\_indices)}

    \CommentTok{\# Create physically smaller Linear layer}
\NormalTok{    new\_layer }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(in\_features, num\_keep)}
\NormalTok{    new\_layer.weight.data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ layer.weight.data[:, keep\_indices].copy()}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ layer.bias }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        new\_layer.bias.data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ layer.bias.data[keep\_indices].copy()}

    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ new\_layer}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Because \texttt{new\_layer} has shape
\((D_{\text{in}}, D_{\text{out}}')\), downstream matrix multiplications
execute with fewer floating-point instructions on standard dense GEMM
hardware without requiring sparse libraries.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Unstructured vs.~Structured Hardware
Realities}\label{unstructured-vs.-structured-hardware-realities}

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2013}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.4545}}
  >{\centering\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3442}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Dimension
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
Unstructured Pruning
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\centering
Structured Pruning
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{Pruning Granularity} & Individual scalar weights & Entire rows /
channels / layers \\
\textbf{Accuracy Retention} & Exceptional (\(>80\%\) sparsity with
\(<1\%\) drop) & Moderate (\(30-50\%\) sparsity before drop) \\
\textbf{Hardware Execution} & Requires sparse CSR/COO index pointers &
\textbf{Native dense GEMM / Tensor Cores} \\
\textbf{Memory Coalescing} & \textbf{Broken} (scattered DRAM line
requests)\footnote{On modern GPUs, memory controllers service DRAM
  requests in 32-byte, 64-byte, or 128-byte cache line segments. When 32
  threads in a warp request contiguous 4-byte floats, a single 128-byte
  DRAM transaction satisfies all 32 threads (100\% bus utilization).
  Unstructured random sparsity causes threads to request scattered
  addresses across different cache lines, triggering up to 32 separate
  128-byte DRAM fetches (delivering only 4 bytes of useful payload each,
  or 3.1\% bus efficiency).} & \textbf{100\% Contiguous \& Coalesced} \\
\textbf{Actual Speedup on CPU/GPU} & Often \textbf{\(0\times\) (or
slower!)} on standard hardware & \textbf{Direct linear speedup
(\(1.5\times - 2.5\times\))} \\
\textbf{Hardware Exceptions} & NVIDIA Ampere 2:4 Sparse Tensor
Cores\footnote{NVIDIA 2:4 Structured Sparsity (introduced in Ampere
  GA100) compresses each 4-element FP16/INT8 vector into 2 nonzero
  elements plus a 2-bit index metadata per pair. Dedicated Sparse Tensor
  Core hardware decompresses these pairs on-the-fly inside register
  banks before feeding the systolic multiplier array, delivering an
  exact \(2\times\) theoretical math speedup.} & Universal support \\
\end{longtable}
}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Knowledge Distillation: Soft
Targets}\label{knowledge-distillation-soft-targets}

In \emph{Distilling the Knowledge in a Neural Network} (2015), Geoffrey
Hinton, Oriol Vinyals, and Jeff Dean proposed \textbf{Knowledge
Distillation}.

Instead of training a compact student network on one-hot ground-truth
labels (\(y = [0, 1, 0]\)), the student learns from the continuous
probability distribution produced by a large teacher network.

\subsection{The Temperature Softmax}\label{the-temperature-softmax}

Dividing logits by temperature \(T > 1\) softens the probability
distribution, revealing rich relative affinities across incorrect
classes (``dark knowledge''):

\[q_i = \frac{e^{z_i / T}}{\sum_j e^{z_j / T}}\]

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Dark Knowledge Extraction: Classifying a BMW               │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  • Hard Label (One-Hot):  [ Car: 1.0,  Truck: 0.0,  Dog: 0.0 ]│
│  • Teacher (T = 1):       [ Car: 0.99, Truck: 0.01, Dog: 0.00]│
│  • Teacher Softened (T=5):[ Car: 0.65, Truck: 0.30, Dog: 0.05]│
│    -> Teaches the student that a BMW is much closer to a    │
│       truck than to a dog!                                  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{The Distillation Loss
Formula}\label{the-distillation-loss-formula}

\[\mathcal{L}_{\text{total}} = \alpha T^2 \cdot D_{\text{KL}}\left(\text{softmax}\left(\frac{\mathbf{z}_S}{T}\right) \parallel \text{softmax}\left(\frac{\mathbf{z}_T}{T}\right)\right) + (1 - \alpha) \mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}(\mathbf{z}_S, y)\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 16.3: Knowledge Distillation Loss in TinyTorch}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ distillation\_loss(}
\NormalTok{    student\_logits: Tensor,}
\NormalTok{    teacher\_logits: Tensor,}
\NormalTok{    targets: Tensor,}
\NormalTok{    temperature: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{4.0}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    alpha: }\BuiltInTok{float} \OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.7}
\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Compute combined soft KL{-}divergence and hard cross{-}entropy distillation loss.}
\CommentTok{    """}
    \CommentTok{\# 1. Soft predictions with temperature scaling}
\NormalTok{    p\_s }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.exp(student\_logits.data }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ temperature)}
\NormalTok{    p\_s }\OperatorTok{/=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(p\_s, axis}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{    p\_t }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.exp(teacher\_logits.data }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ temperature)}
\NormalTok{    p\_t }\OperatorTok{/=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(p\_t, axis}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

    \CommentTok{\# 2. KL Divergence: sum(p\_t * log(p\_t / p\_s))}
\NormalTok{    kl\_div }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(p\_t }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (np.log(np.maximum(p\_t, }\FloatTok{1e{-}8}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ np.log(np.maximum(p\_s, }\FloatTok{1e{-}8}\NormalTok{))), axis}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    soft\_loss }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.mean(kl\_div) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (temperature }\OperatorTok{**} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}

    \CommentTok{\# 3. Standard cross{-}entropy on hard targets}
\NormalTok{    b }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ targets.data.astype(}\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    log\_probs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.log(np.maximum(p\_s, }\FloatTok{1e{-}8}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{    hard\_loss }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{np.mean(log\_probs[np.arange(}\BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(b)), b])}

\NormalTok{    total }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ alpha }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ soft\_loss }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ alpha) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ hard\_loss}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.array(total, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Low-Rank Matrix Factorization (SVD /
LoRA)}\label{low-rank-matrix-factorization-svd-lora}

A dense weight matrix \(\mathbf{W} \in \mathbb{R}^{M \times N}\) can be
approximated by the product of two low-rank matrices
\(\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{M \times r}\) and
\(\mathbf{B} \in \mathbb{R}^{r \times N}\) where \(r \ll \min(M, N)\):

\[\mathbf{W} \approx \mathbf{A} \mathbf{B}\]

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Low-Rank Factorization Memory Reduction                    │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Original Matrix: W (4,096 x 4,096) = 16,777,216 parameters │
│                                                             │
│  Factorized (Rank r = 64):                                  │
│  • Matrix A: (4,096 x 64) = 262,144 parameters              │
│  • Matrix B: (64 x 4,096) = 262,144 parameters              │
│  • Total Parameters: 524,288 parameters                     │
│  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
│  Parameter Reduction: 96.9% reduction (32x compression)!    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

This mathematical factorization forms the foundation of \textbf{LoRA
(Low-Rank Adaptation)}, allowing billion-parameter foundational LLMs to
be fine-tuned using tiny \(r=16\) adapter matrices.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{PyTorch Systems Bridge: \texttt{torch.nn.utils.prune}, TensorRT, and
PEFT LoRA}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

In PyTorch, pruning and low-rank adaptation are integrated across both
training and deployment frameworks:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch.nn.utils.prune }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ prune}

\CommentTok{\# Apply structured L2{-}norm pruning across output channels}
\NormalTok{prune.ln\_structured(}
\NormalTok{    module}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{model.conv1,}
\NormalTok{    name}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"weight"}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    amount}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    n}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    dim}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{)}
\CommentTok{\# Permanently remove pruned channels, physically reshaping the tensor}
\NormalTok{prune.remove(model.conv1, }\StringTok{"weight"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

For large generative models, \textbf{HuggingFace PEFT} implements
Low-Rank Adaptation
(\(\mathbf{W} + \frac{\alpha}{r} \mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\)), keeping 70B
parameter base weights frozen in 4-bit INT4 quantization while training
low-rank adapter matrices. In serving engines like \textbf{vLLM},
dynamic multi-LoRA routing allows hundreds of custom adapters to be
evaluated concurrently over a single shared base model in GPU HBM.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-16}

In this chapter, we explored algorithms for shrinking neural network
architectures. We analyzed the fundamental distinction between
unstructured magnitude pruning (which creates scattered zeros that stall
standard GPUs) and structured pruning (which physically resizes weight
matrices for guaranteed dense GEMM acceleration).

We implemented Knowledge Distillation to transfer dark knowledge from
large teacher models to compact students via temperature-softened
probability distributions, and formulated Low-Rank Matrix Factorization
(the mathematical basis for LoRA).

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-16}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The Sparsity Speed Paradox}: Unstructured pruning reduces
  theoretical FLOPs but often fails to accelerate GPU inference due to
  uncoalesced memory reads and branch divergence.
\item
  \textbf{Structured Pruning Speedup}: Structured pruning physically
  reduces matrix dimensions, delivering immediate speedups on standard
  dense BLAS/Tensor Core hardware.
\item
  \textbf{Knowledge Distillation}: Softening teacher probabilities with
  temperature \(T > 1\) exposes rich inter-class affinities that guide
  small student networks toward higher accuracy.
\item
  \textbf{Low-Rank Decomposition}: Factoring an \((M \times N)\) weight
  matrix into \((M \times r)\) and \((r \times N)\) compresses parameter
  storage by \((M \times N) / (r(M + N))\).
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Accelerating Execution
Kernels}\label{chapter-connection-accelerating-execution-kernels}

We have shrunk our models through quantization and pruning. How do we
make the remaining operations execute at peak hardware speed on the
processor? In \textbf{Chapter 17 (Kernel Acceleration: Vectorization,
Tiling, and Fusion)}, we dive into the low-level systems mechanics of
hardware caches, loop tiling, SIMD vectorization, and operator fusion.

\chapter{Kernel Acceleration: Vectorization, Tiling, and Operator
Fusion}\label{kernel-acceleration-vectorization-tiling-and-operator-fusion}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-19}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does writing intermediate tensor arrays to RAM slow down deep
networks more than the floating-point math itself?}

In modern computing architectures, floating-point arithmetic is
essentially free, while memory movement is exorbitantly expensive. A
modern processor can execute thousands of arithmetic ALU operations in
the time required to fetch a single float from off-chip DRAM (the
\textbf{Memory Wall}). When a neural network executes a sequence of
three operations---such as \texttt{MatrixMultiply} followed by
\texttt{BiasAdd} followed by \texttt{GELU}---standard frameworks
allocate three distinct intermediate memory buffers in RAM, reading and
writing the entire tensor three separate times over the memory bus.
\textbf{Operator Fusion} compiles adjacent mathematical operations into
a single unified kernel, keeping intermediate activations inside
high-speed CPU registers and L1 cache (\(0.5\text{ ns}\) latency) and
eliminating DRAM round-trips (\(50\text{ ns}\) latency). Combined with
\textbf{SIMD Vectorization} and \textbf{Cache Tiling}, kernel
acceleration transforms memory-stalled neural networks into
hardware-saturating execution engines.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-17}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Analyze} the CPU memory hierarchy (Registers \(\to\) L1
  \(\to\) L2 \(\to\) L3 \(\to\) DRAM), quantifying the \(100\times\)
  latency gap between on-chip cache and main memory.
\item
  \textbf{Implement} naive 3-nested loop matrix multiplication and
  contrast its latency against SIMD vectorized matrix arithmetic.
\item
  \textbf{Construct} a Cache-Tiled GEMM engine that partitions large
  matrices into sub-blocks tailored to L1 cache capacity.
\item
  \textbf{Design and build} Fused Linear+ReLU and Fused GELU kernels in
  TinyTorch, proving the elimination of intermediate RAM buffer
  allocations.
\item
  \textbf{Evaluate} modern ML compilers (PyTorch \texttt{torch.compile}
  / TorchInductor, OpenAI Triton) that automate kernel fusion.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Memory Wall and Cache Latency
Hierarchy}\label{the-memory-wall-and-cache-latency-hierarchy}

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Hardware Memory Hierarchy & Access Latencies               │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. CPU Registers : ~ 0.5 ns  (Instantaneous ALU operands)  │
│  2. L1 Cache (32 KB) : ~ 1.0 ns  (On-chip core memory)      │
│  3. L2 Cache (512 KB): ~ 3.0 ns  (Core-dedicated cache)     │
│  4. L3 Cache (32 MB) : ~ 10.0 ns (Shared multi-core cache)  │
│  5. Main DRAM (16 GB): ~ 60.0 ns (Off-chip bus transfer)    │
│  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
│  Latency Penalty: DRAM is 120x SLOWER than CPU Registers!   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

When an operator reads or writes data to off-chip DRAM (or GPU HBM), the
processor pipeline sits completely idle for dozens or hundreds of clock
cycles waiting for data lines to settle.\footnote{Modern GPU Streaming
  Multiprocessors (SMs) allocate 128 KB to 228 KB of ultra-fast on-chip
  SRAM configurable as L1 data cache and shared memory. Across 132 SMs
  on an H100 GPU, this represents \textasciitilde30 MB of aggregate
  on-chip SRAM delivering over 100 TB/s of bandwidth with
  \textasciitilde1--2 ns latency, compared to off-chip HBM3 access at
  3.35 TB/s and \textasciitilde50--100 ns latency.}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Vectorization: SIMD
Execution}\label{vectorization-simd-execution}

A standard scalar loop processes one single floating-point number per
CPU cycle:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Scalar loop: 1 addition per instruction}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(N):}
\NormalTok{    c[i] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ a[i] }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ b[i]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{SIMD (Single Instruction, Multiple Data)} instructions (such as
Intel AVX-512 or ARM NEON) load 256-bit or 512-bit vector registers that
execute 8 to 16 floating-point additions simultaneously in a single
clock cycle:

\[\mathbf{c}_{[0:15]} = \mathbf{a}_{[0:15]} \oplus \mathbf{b}_{[0:15]} \quad (1\text{ instruction, 16 operations})\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 17.1: Scalar vs. Vectorized Matrix Multiplication}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ naive\_matmul(A: np.ndarray, B: np.ndarray) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ np.ndarray:}
    \CommentTok{"""Naive 3{-}nested loop matrix multiplication: O(N\^{}3) scalar operations."""}
\NormalTok{    M, K }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ A.shape}
\NormalTok{    K2, N }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ B.shape}
\NormalTok{    C }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.zeros((M, N), dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32)}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(M):}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ j }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(N):}
\NormalTok{            total }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.0}
            \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ k }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(K):}
\NormalTok{                total }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ A[i, k] }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ B[k, j]}
\NormalTok{            C[i, j] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ total}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ C}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ vectorized\_matmul(A: np.ndarray, B: np.ndarray) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ np.ndarray:}
    \CommentTok{"""SIMD{-}vectorized matrix multiplication via BLAS."""}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ np.matmul(A, B)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Benchmarking a \(512 \times 512\) matrix multiplication reveals that
\texttt{vectorized\_matmul} runs \textbf{over \(150\times\) faster} than
\texttt{naive\_matmul} due to SIMD vector registers and hardware memory
prefetching.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Cache Tiling and Blocked
GEMM}\label{cache-tiling-and-blocked-gemm}

When computing \(\mathbf{C} = \mathbf{A} \mathbf{B}\) for large matrices
(e.g., \(1024 \times 1024\)), matrix \(\mathbf{B}\) exceeds the capacity
of L1 cache (\(32\text{ KB}\)). As the inner loop iterates over columns
of \(\mathbf{B}\), cache lines are repeatedly evicted, causing constant
\textbf{cache thrashing}.

Computing \(\mathbf{C} = \mathbf{A} \mathbf{B}\) requires \(2 N^3\)
FLOPs over \(3 N^2\) matrix elements. In naive 3-nested loop
multiplication, every element of \(\mathbf{B}\) is fetched from DRAM
\(N\) times, requiring \(O(N^3)\) total DRAM word transfers. Because
DRAM latency is \(50-100\text{ ns}\) compared to ALU cycle time of
\(0.5\text{ ns}\), memory bus stalls consume \(>99\%\) of processor
clock cycles.

\textbf{Cache Tiling (Loop Blocking)} divides the \(M \times N\) matrix
into small \(B_{\text{tile}} \times B_{\text{tile}}\) square blocks
(tiles) that fit entirely inside L1 cache / SRAM
(\(B_{\text{tile}} \approx 32 - 64\)):

\[\mathbf{C}_{I, J} = \sum_{K} \mathbf{A}_{I, K} \mathbf{B}_{K, J}\]

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Cache Tiling (Blocked Matrix Multiplication)               │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Matrix A:               Matrix B:                          │
│  ┌────┬────┐             ┌────┬────┐                        │
│  │ A00│ A01│             │ B00│ B01│                        │
│  ├────┼────┤      X      ├────┼────┤                        │
│  │ A10│ A11│             │ B10│ B11│                        │
│  └────┴────┘             └────┴────┘                        │
│                                                             │
│  Block C00 = (A00 @ B00) + (A01 @ B10)                      │
│  -> All sub-blocks remain pinned in L1 cache during math!   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/17_acceleration-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-cache-tiling}\textbf{Figure 17.1: Cache-Tiled Matrix
Multiplication.} By dividing large matrices into cache-resident
sub-tiles, memory accesses hit on-chip L1 cache (\(1\text{ ns}\))
instead of triggering DRAM cache evictions (\(60\text{ ns}\)).}

\end{figure}%

By keeping each \(B_{\text{tile}} \times B_{\text{tile}}\) sub-matrix
pinned in high-speed SRAM, each value loaded from DRAM is reused
\(B_{\text{tile}}\) times across arithmetic instructions, slashing DRAM
memory traffic by a factor of \(B_{\text{tile}}\) (from \(O(N^3)\) to
\(O(N^3 / B_{\text{tile}})\)) and eliminating DRAM pipeline stalls.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Operator Fusion: Eliminating DRAM
Traffic}\label{operator-fusion-eliminating-dram-traffic}

Consider the standard feed-forward block:
\(\mathbf{Y} = \text{ReLU}(\mathbf{X} \mathbf{W} + \mathbf{b})\).

\subsection{The Unfused Execution
Penalty}\label{the-unfused-execution-penalty}

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Unfused Execution (3 Separate Kernels)                     │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. GEMM Kernel : Read X, W from DRAM ──> Write Z to DRAM   │
│  2. Bias Kernel : Read Z, b from DRAM ──> Write Z_b to DRAM │
│  3. ReLU Kernel : Read Z_b from DRAM  ──> Write Y to DRAM   │
│  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
│  Total DRAM Traffic: 3 Memory Writes + 3 Memory Reads!      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{The Fused Kernel Solution}\label{the-fused-kernel-solution}

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Fused Kernel (Linear + Bias + ReLU in 1 Kernel)            │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  • Read X, W, b from DRAM ONCE                              │
│  • Compute: val = dot(x, w) + b                             │
│  • Apply ReLU: if val < 0: val = 0 (Inside CPU Register!)   │
│  • Write final Y to DRAM ONCE                               │
│  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
│  Total DRAM Traffic: 1 Memory Write + 1 Memory Read (66% cut)
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Implementing Fused Kernels in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-fused-kernels-in-tinytorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 17.2: Fused Linear{-}ReLU Layer in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Layer, Linear}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ FusedLinearReLU(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Fused Linear projection and ReLU activation kernel.}
\CommentTok{    Eliminates intermediate allocation buffer in DRAM.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, in\_features: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, out\_features: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.in\_features }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ in\_features}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.out\_features }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ out\_features}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.random.randn(in\_features, out\_features).astype(np.float32) }\OperatorTok{*} \FloatTok{0.02}\NormalTok{)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Tensor(np.zeros(out\_features, dtype}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{np.float32))}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
        \CommentTok{\# Compute projection + bias + ReLU in a single fused NumPy expression}
\NormalTok{        out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.matmul(x.data, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.weight.data) }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.bias.data}
\NormalTok{        np.maximum(out, }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, out}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{out)  }\CommentTok{\# In{-}place ReLU without allocating new buffer!}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(out)}


\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ fused\_gelu(x: Tensor) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Fused GELU kernel: 0.5 * x * (1.0 + tanh(sqrt(2/pi) * (x + 0.044715 * x\^{}3)))}
\CommentTok{    Evaluates approximation in a single vectorized pass.}
\CommentTok{    """}
\NormalTok{    data }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.data}
    \CommentTok{\# Compute cubic and tanh directly in registers}
\NormalTok{    inner }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.79788456} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (data }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{0.044715} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (data }\OperatorTok{**} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{    out }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.5} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ data }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ np.tanh(inner))}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Tensor(out)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Production Reality: PyTorch
\texttt{torch.compile} and OpenAI
Triton}{Production Reality: PyTorch torch.compile and OpenAI Triton}}\label{production-reality-pytorch-torch.compile-and-openai-triton}

Why does \textbf{OpenAI Triton} replace traditional CUDA boilerplate in
modern compiler stacks?

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The CUDA Complexity Trap}: In raw CUDA C++, developers must
  explicitly manage fine-grained thread hierarchies (\texttt{threadIdx},
  \texttt{blockIdx}), declare and synchronize on-chip shared memory
  buffers (\texttt{\_\_shared\_\_}), orchestrate thread barriers
  (\texttt{\_\_syncthreads()}), manually avoid 32-way shared memory bank
  conflicts,\footnote{Shared memory on NVIDIA GPUs is organized into 32
    memory banks (each 4 bytes wide). If multiple threads in a warp
    access different memory addresses residing in the same memory bank,
    the hardware serializes the requests (a shared memory bank
    conflict), multiplying access latency. Triton automatically arranges
    data layouts to guarantee bank-conflict-free memory strides.} and
  carefully structure memory accesses to guarantee 128-byte coalescing
  across 32 threads in a warp.
\item
  \textbf{Block-Level Python Abstraction}: Triton abstracts the GPU
  thread hierarchy into block-level operations over multi-dimensional
  tensor tiles (\texttt{tl.load}, \texttt{tl.dot}, \texttt{tl.store}).
  The Triton compiler automatically analyzes tile dimensions, schedules
  warps, manages SRAM shared memory allocation, and optimizes memory
  coalescing, generating bare-metal PTX assembly that matches or
  surpasses hand-tuned CUDA C++.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{PyTorch Systems Bridge: \texttt{torch.compile}, TorchInductor, and
TensorRT}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

In PyTorch 2.0+, automated compilation is orchestrated through a
three-stage pipeline:

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  PyTorch 2.0 Compiler Pipeline                              │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. TorchDynamo  : Intercepts Python frame bytecode &       │
│                    extracts FX computational graph.         │
│  2. AOTAutograd  : Captures forward & backward autograd DAGs│
│                    ahead-of-time.                           │
│  3. TorchInductor: Lowers FX graph into fused C++/OpenMP    │
│                    (for CPUs) and OpenAI Triton (for GPUs). │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}

\CommentTok{\# Compile PyTorch model into fused Triton kernels}
\NormalTok{compiled\_model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.}\BuiltInTok{compile}\NormalTok{(model, mode}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"reduce{-}overhead"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

In enterprise serving engines, \textbf{NVIDIA TensorRT} performs
graph-level transformations: fusing Convolution + BatchNorm + ReLU into
a single execution layer, merging Linear projections and GELU
activations, and generating hardware-tuned Tensor Core execution
engines.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-17}

In this chapter, we explored the systems mechanics of low-level kernel
acceleration. We examined the physical latency hierarchy of modern
processors, demonstrating why DRAM memory access is the primary
bottleneck in deep neural network execution.

We quantified the power of SIMD vectorization over scalar loops,
implemented Cache Tiling to eliminate cache thrashing during matrix
multiplications, and built Fused Linear-ReLU and Fused GELU kernels to
slash off-chip memory traffic by over 60\%. Finally, we examined how
modern deep learning compilers (\texttt{torch.compile}, TorchInductor,
OpenAI Triton) automate kernel fusion and cache tiling.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-17}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The Latency Hierarchy}: Off-chip DRAM (\(60\text{ ns}\)) is
  over \(100\times\) slower than on-chip registers (\(0.5\text{ ns}\));
  memory traffic, not arithmetic, governs runtime.
\item
  \textbf{SIMD Parallelism}: Vectorized instructions execute operations
  simultaneously across 8 to 16 float elements, delivering
  orders-of-magnitude speedups.
\item
  \textbf{Cache Tiling}: Partitioning large matrix multiplications into
  L1 cache-sized blocks (\(B_{\text{tile}}\)) maximizes on-chip data
  reuse and prevents cache thrashing.
\item
  \textbf{Operator Fusion}: Fusing consecutive operations
  (\texttt{Linear} + \texttt{Bias} + \texttt{ReLU}) keeps activations in
  registers, eliminating redundant DRAM reads and writes.
\item
  \textbf{Automated Compilers}: Modern systems use compilers
  (\texttt{torch.compile}, Triton) to automatically fuse and tile
  execution graphs for hardware targets.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Eliminating Redundant Autoregressive
Work}\label{chapter-connection-eliminating-redundant-autoregressive-work}

We have accelerated static execution kernels, but in autoregressive
language models (GPT), each new token recalculates attention keys and
values for all past tokens from scratch. How do we eliminate this
redundant \(O(S)\) compute per token? In \textbf{Chapter 18 (Inference
Memoization and the KV Cache)}, we build Key-Value caching for
high-speed generation.

\chapter{Inference Memoization and the KV
Cache}\label{inference-memoization-and-the-kv-cache}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-20}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does naive autoregressive transformer generation waste 95\% of
its compute recalculating identical numbers?}

Autoregressive language models generate text sequentially: predicting
token \(t+1\) requires passing the entire preceding prompt
\([x_1, \dots, x_t]\) into the model. In a naive implementation, each
generation step recomputes Query, Key, and Value projections for all
\(t\) past tokens from scratch, resulting in \(O(t)\) matrix
multiplications per token and compounding into an \(O(N^2)\)
computational explosion across an \(N\)-token generation pass. However,
because causal attention guarantees that the representations of past
tokens are mathematically invariant to future tokens, recalculating
their Keys and Values is completely redundant. \textbf{Inference
Memoization (The KV Cache)} stores past Key and Value projection
matrices in GPU memory, allowing each new generation step to project
\emph{only the single newly generated token} (\(S=1\)) and retrieve past
context in \(O(1)\) time. In a production framework runtime, the KV
Cache accelerates LLM generation throughput by \textbf{10× to 15×},
transforming transformer decoding from an unusable theoretical loop into
an interactive real-time system.

\section{Learning Objectives}\label{learning-objectives-18}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Formulate} the algorithmic and computational complexity gap
  between naive autoregressive decoding (\(O(N^2)\) cumulative FLOPs)
  and KV-cached generation (\(O(N)\) cumulative FLOPs).
\item
  \textbf{Construct} the \texttt{KVCache} state manager in pure Python,
  implementing dynamic concatenation and pre-allocated buffer slicing.
\item
  \textbf{Implement} Cache-Aware \texttt{MultiHeadAttention} in
  TinyTorch, routing single-token Queries (\(1, D\)) across historical
  cached Keys and Values (\(S, D\)).
\item
  \textbf{Calculate} the exact physical memory footprint of the KV Cache
  across sequence length \(S\), batch size \(B\), and layers \(L\).
\item
  \textbf{Analyze} modern KV Cache memory compression architectures:
  Multi-Query Attention (MQA), Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), and
  PagedAttention (vLLM).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Computational Redundancy of Naive
Decoding}\label{the-computational-redundancy-of-naive-decoding}

LLM inference operates in two fundamentally distinct operational
regimes:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The Prefill Phase (Prompt Ingestion)}: All
  \(S_{\text{prompt}}\) input tokens are ingested simultaneously in
  parallel. This is a dense, high-arithmetic-intensity GEMM
  (\(B \times S_{\text{prompt}} \times D\)) that operates in the
  compute-bound plateau of the Roofline Model.
\item
  \textbf{The Decode Phase (Autoregressive Generation)}: Tokens are
  generated sequentially, one token at a time (\(S=1\)). In naive
  generation, step \(t\) re-reads and re-processes all \(t\) previous
  tokens through the entire network:
\end{enumerate}

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Naive Autoregressive Decoding Loop (No Caching)            │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Step 1  : Forward pass on 500 tokens ──> Generates token 501
│  Step 2  : Forward pass on 501 tokens ──> Generates token 502
│  ...                                                        │
│  Step 100: Forward pass on 600 tokens ──> Generates token 601
│  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
│  Total Tokens Processed: sum_{t=500}^{600} t = 55,050 tokens!
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

In Step 100, the network re-executes all linear projections, LayerNorms,
and FFNs for tokens \(1 \dots 500\) for the hundredth time, computing
identical numerical matrices that were already calculated in Step 1.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{../quarto/assets/images/diagrams/18_memoization-diag-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-kv-cache-concept}\textbf{Figure 18.1: Naive
Generation vs.~KV-Cached Generation.} Naive generation re-processes the
full sequence on every step. KV caching processes only the new query
token (\(S=1\)), appending new Keys and Values to a persistent memory
cache.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{The Key-Value Cache
Formulation}\label{the-key-value-cache-formulation}

For layer \(l\) at generation step \(t\):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  The input is \textbf{only the single newest token}
  \(\mathbf{x}_t \in \mathbb{R}^{1 \times D}\).
\item
  Project single-token Query, Key, and Value vectors:
\end{enumerate}

\[\mathbf{q}_t = \mathbf{x}_t \mathbf{W}_Q, \quad \mathbf{k}_t = \mathbf{x}_t \mathbf{W}_K, \quad \mathbf{v}_t = \mathbf{x}_t \mathbf{W}_V \quad (\text{Each in } \mathbb{R}^{1 \times D})\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\tightlist
\item
  Retrieve historical cached Keys and Values and append the new vectors:
\end{enumerate}

\[\mathbf{K}_{\text{seq}} = \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{K}_{\text{cached}} \\ \mathbf{k}_t \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{t \times D}, \quad \mathbf{V}_{\text{seq}} = \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{V}_{\text{cached}} \\ \mathbf{v}_t \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{t \times D}\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{3}
\tightlist
\item
  Compute attention for the single query token against all keys:
\end{enumerate}

\[\text{Attention}(\mathbf{q}_t, \mathbf{K}_{\text{seq}}, \mathbf{V}_{\text{seq}}) = \text{softmax}\left( \frac{\mathbf{q}_t \mathbf{K}_{\text{seq}}^T}{\sqrt{d_k}} \right) \mathbf{V}_{\text{seq}} \in \mathbb{R}^{1 \times D}\]

By memoizing past \((\mathbf{K}, \mathbf{V})\) tensors, the per-token
compute cost for all feed-forward networks and projections drops from
\(O(t)\) to \(O(1)\). Memory bus transfers during decode are reduced to
streaming the cached \((\mathbf{K}, \mathbf{V})\) vectors, converting
generation latency from an \(O(N^2)\) quadratic bottleneck into a linear
\(O(N)\) memory scan.\footnote{During single-token decode (\(S=1\)), the
  generation throughput is strictly memory-bandwidth bound:
  \(\text{Tokens/sec} \approx \frac{\text{Memory Bandwidth (TB/s)}}{\text{Model Parameters (Bytes)} + \text{KV Cache Active Bytes}}\).
  On an NVIDIA H100 with \(3.35\text{ TB/s}\) HBM3 bandwidth running a
  70B parameter FP16 model (140 GB), the maximum theoretical
  single-stream generation speed without tensor parallelism is
  \(\approx 3350 / 140 \approx 24\text{ tokens/sec}\).}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Implementing KVCache in
TinyTorch}\label{implementing-kvcache-in-tinytorch}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 18.1: The KVCache Class in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.tensor }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Tensor}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ typing }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ List, Tuple, Optional}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ KVCache:}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    Key{-}Value Cache for accelerating autoregressive transformer inference.}
\CommentTok{    Stores past key and value tensors for each transformer layer.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, num\_layers: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_layers }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ num\_layers}
        \CommentTok{\# List of (key\_cache, value\_cache) tuples per layer}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.k\_cache: List[Optional[np.ndarray]] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ num\_layers}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.v\_cache: List[Optional[np.ndarray]] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ num\_layers}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ update(}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        layer\_idx: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        k\_new: np.ndarray,}
\NormalTok{        v\_new: np.ndarray}
\NormalTok{    ) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:}
        \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{        Append new Key and Value tensors to cache for layer\_idx.}
\CommentTok{        k\_new, v\_new shape: (B, H, 1, d\_k)}
\CommentTok{        Returns: Complete historical (K\_seq, V\_seq) of shape (B, H, seq\_len, d\_k)}
\CommentTok{        """}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.k\_cache[layer\_idx] }\KeywordTok{is} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
            \CommentTok{\# First prompt prefill step}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.k\_cache[layer\_idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ k\_new}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.v\_cache[layer\_idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ v\_new}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
            \CommentTok{\# Concatenate along sequence dimension (axis=2)}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.k\_cache[layer\_idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.concatenate([}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.k\_cache[layer\_idx], k\_new], axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
            \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.v\_cache[layer\_idx] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.concatenate([}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.v\_cache[layer\_idx], v\_new], axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}

        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.k\_cache[layer\_idx], }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.v\_cache[layer\_idx]}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ reset(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{"""Clear cache state for new inference session."""}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.k\_cache }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_layers}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.v\_cache }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_layers}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Cache-Aware Multi-Head
Attention}\label{cache-aware-multi-head-attention}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Listing 18.2: Cache{-}Aware MultiHeadAttention in TinyTorch}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ tinytorch.core.layers }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ Layer, Linear}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ CachedMultiHeadAttention(Layer):}
    \CommentTok{"""}
\CommentTok{    MultiHeadAttention with support for incremental KV caching.}
\CommentTok{    """}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, d\_model: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, num\_heads: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{, layer\_idx: }\BuiltInTok{int}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_model }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ d\_model}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_heads }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ num\_heads}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_k }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ d\_model }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ num\_heads}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.layer\_idx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ layer\_idx}

        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_q }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(d\_model, d\_model)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_k }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(d\_model, d\_model)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_v }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(d\_model, d\_model)}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_o }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Linear(d\_model, d\_model)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x: Tensor, kv\_cache: Optional[KVCache] }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{{-}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ Tensor:}
\NormalTok{        B, S, \_ }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape}

        \CommentTok{\# 1. Project Q, K, V for input tokens: (B, S, D)}
\NormalTok{        proj\_q }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_q(x).data.reshape(B, S, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_heads, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_k).swapaxes(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        proj\_k }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_k(x).data.reshape(B, S, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_heads, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_k).swapaxes(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        proj\_v }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_v(x).data.reshape(B, S, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num\_heads, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_k).swapaxes(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}

        \CommentTok{\# 2. Update KV Cache if enabled}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ kv\_cache }\KeywordTok{is} \KeywordTok{not} \VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            k\_full, v\_full }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ kv\_cache.update(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.layer\_idx, proj\_k, proj\_v)}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            k\_full, v\_full }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ proj\_k, proj\_v}

        \CommentTok{\# 3. Scaled dot{-}product: Q (B, H, S, d\_k) @ K\_full\^{}T (B, H, d\_k, S\_total) {-}\textgreater{} (B, H, S, S\_total)}
\NormalTok{        scale }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.0} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ np.sqrt(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_k)}
\NormalTok{        scores }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.matmul(proj\_q, np.swapaxes(k\_full, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ scale}

        \CommentTok{\# Softmax along full sequence dimension}
\NormalTok{        exp\_scores }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.exp(scores }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(scores, axis}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{        attn\_weights }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ exp\_scores }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(exp\_scores, axis}\OperatorTok{={-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

        \CommentTok{\# 4. Multiply by V\_full: (B, H, S, S\_total) @ (B, H, S\_total, d\_k) {-}\textgreater{} (B, H, S, d\_k)}
\NormalTok{        out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.matmul(attn\_weights, v\_full)}
\NormalTok{        out\_concat }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ out.swapaxes(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{).reshape(B, S, }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.d\_model)}

        \ControlFlowTok{return} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.w\_o(Tensor(out\_concat))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Memory Scaling of the KV
Cache}\label{memory-scaling-of-the-kv-cache}

While the KV Cache eliminates redundant computation, it exchanges
compute for \textbf{memory capacity}.

For a model with \(L\) layers, batch size \(B\), sequence context \(S\),
and hidden dimension \(D\):

\[\text{KV Cache Memory} = 2 \times L \times B \times S \times D \times \text{sizeof}(\text{dtype}) \quad \text{bytes}\]

The factor of 2 accounts for storing both Keys and Values.

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  KV Cache Memory Footprint (LLaMA-70B, float16: 2 bytes)    │
│  L = 80 layers, D = 8,192, Batch B = 16                     │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  • Sequence Length S = 2,048  : 85.9 GB VRAM                │
│  • Sequence Length S = 8,192  : 343.6 GB VRAM               │
│  • Sequence Length S = 32,768 : 1.37 TB VRAM (Exceeds GPU!) │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

At long context lengths, the KV Cache consumes more GPU memory than the
70-billion model weights themselves (\(140\text{ GB}\)), limiting
concurrency.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Production Systems Bridge: vLLM, PagedAttention, and FlashDecoding}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

In modern high-concurrency LLM inference servers, \textbf{vLLM}
implements \textbf{PagedAttention} (Kwon et al., 2023), inspired by
operating system virtual memory paging.\footnotemark{}

Instead of allocating contiguous GPU memory for each request's maximum
sequence length (which wastes up to \(80\%\) of VRAM to internal
fragmentation), PagedAttention partitions the KV cache into fixed-size
virtual memory blocks (\(16\) or \(32\) tokens per block).
Non-contiguous blocks are dynamically mapped via a page table,
increasing GPU serving throughput by \textbf{2× to 4×}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ torch.nn.functional }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ F}

\CommentTok{\# PyTorch native scaled dot{-}product attention with FlashDecoding}
\CommentTok{\# Q: (B, H, 1, d\_k), K\_cache: (B, H\_kv, S, d\_k), V\_cache: (B, H\_kv, S, d\_k)}
\NormalTok{out }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ F.scaled\_dot\_product\_attention(}
\NormalTok{    query}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{q,}
\NormalTok{    key}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{k\_cache,}
\NormalTok{    value}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{v\_cache,}
\NormalTok{    is\_causal}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    enable\_gqa}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}  \CommentTok{\# Enables Grouped{-}Query Attention head broadcasting}
\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

\footnotetext{Kwon et al.~(2023), \emph{Efficient Memory Management for
Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention}, SOSP. PagedAttention
partitions the KV cache into fixed-size physical memory pages (e.g., 16
or 32 tokens) in GPU HBM, using dynamic virtual memory page tables. This
eliminates internal and external memory fragmentation (which
historically wasted 60\% to 80\% of VRAM to static sequence length
allocations) and enables zero-copy copy-on-write page sharing for
parallel sampling and beam search.}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Architectural Mitigations: MQA, GQA, and
PagedAttention}\label{architectural-mitigations-mqa-gqa-and-pagedattention}

To tame KV Cache memory explosion, modern architectures implement
structural innovations:

\begin{verbatim}
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Attention Memory Architectures                             │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  1. Multi-Head Attention (MHA):                             │
│     • H Query heads, H Key heads, H Value heads.            │
│     • KV Cache Memory: 100% (Baseline).                     │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  2. Multi-Query Attention (MQA - Shazeer 2019):             │
│     • H Query heads, 1 Key head, 1 Value head (Shared!).    │
│     • KV Cache Memory: Reduced by H (e.g., 32x smaller!).   │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  3. Grouped-Query Attention (GQA - LLaMA-2/3):              │
│     • H Query heads grouped into G KV heads (e.g., G = 8).  │
│     • KV Cache Memory: Reduced by H/G (4x to 8x smaller!).  │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  4. PagedAttention (vLLM - Kwon et al. 2023):               │
│     • Virtual memory paging for KV cache blocks in DRAM.    │
│     • Eliminates internal memory fragmentation.             │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-18}

In this chapter, we constructed the inference memoization engine of
TinyTorch: the Key-Value Cache. We examined why naive autoregressive
decoding wastes over 95\% of its compute recalculating invariant past
activations, deriving the \(O(N^2) \to O(N)\) complexity reduction
achieved by caching.

We implemented \texttt{KVCache} and \texttt{CachedMultiHeadAttention} in
pure Python, enabling single-token query execution. Finally, we
calculated the physical memory footprint of the KV Cache across long
context windows and analyzed modern architectural mitigations including
Grouped-Query Attention (GQA) and PagedAttention.

\section{Chapter Takeaways}\label{chapter-takeaways-18}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The Memoization Invariant}: In causal attention,
  representations of past tokens are invariant to future tokens; caching
  Keys and Values eliminates redundant computation.
\item
  \textbf{Complexity Shift}: KV caching reduces single-step decoding
  complexity from \(O(S)\) full-sequence passes to an \(O(1)\)
  single-token query pass (\(10\times - 15\times\) latency reduction).
\item
  \textbf{The Memory-Compute Trade-off}: The KV cache trades GPU memory
  capacity for speed; memory scales linearly with sequence length:
  \(2 L B S D \times \text{sizeof}(\text{dtype})\).
\item
  \textbf{Grouped-Query Attention (GQA)}: Sharing Key-Value heads across
  groups of Query heads shrinks KV Cache memory footprints by
  \(4\times\) to \(8\times\) with zero accuracy loss.
\end{itemize}

\section{Chapter Connection: Rigorous Performance
Evaluation}\label{chapter-connection-rigorous-performance-evaluation}

We have optimized our models through quantization, pruning, kernel
fusion, and KV caching. How do we rigorously benchmark and verify these
speedups across hardware systems? In \textbf{Chapter 19 (Systematic
Benchmarking and Performance Evaluation)}, we establish statistical
benchmarking protocols for measuring latency, throughput, and memory
bandwidth.

\chapter{Systematic Benchmarking and Performance
Evaluation}\label{systematic-benchmarking-and-performance-evaluation}

\section*{Purpose}\label{purpose-21}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Purpose}

\markright{Purpose}

\emph{Why does running a benchmark once tell you almost nothing about
true model performance in production?}

In high-performance machine learning, evaluating an optimization by
measuring a single execution run is an engineering fallacy. Execution
times on modern hardware are stochastic processes corrupted by thermal
CPU throttling, operating system context switches, thread preemption,
and memory bus congestion. Reporting an unverified ``2× speedup''
without statistical confidence intervals frequently leads to regressions
when deployed to production clusters. Systematic benchmarking requires
standardized measurement protocols: cache warm-ups, garbage collection
isolation, variance analysis, and statistical hypothesis testing (95\%
confidence intervals). In a framework runtime, benchmarking is the
